Fifteen systems, from whiteboard to production

The method, the primitives, and then fifteen large-scale systems designed end to end — ride-hailing, seat booking, streaming, messaging, food delivery, feeds, file sync, commerce, marketplaces, payments, notifications, search, geo, crawling. Each one carries the estimation, the high-level architecture, the data model, the low-level design of the hard component, and the failure modes that decide whether it survives a real Saturday night.

00

How to design a system

Every case study in this book follows the same seven steps, in the same order. The order is the skill: candidates who start drawing boxes before agreeing on the numbers design the wrong system, correctly.

1 SCOPE what is in, what is explicitly out, who the users are │ ↳ ask, do not assume. "Do we need group chat?" is a real question. ▼ 2 REQUIREMENTS functional (verbs) + non-functional (numbers) │ ↳ latency, availability, consistency, durability, cost ▼ 3 ESTIMATE DAU → QPS → storage → bandwidth → machines │ ↳ round hard. The magnitude is the point, not the digits. ▼ 4 API + DATA the contract first, then the entities and access patterns │ ↳ the access patterns choose the database, not the other way round ▼ 5 HIGH LEVEL boxes and arrows: clients, edge, services, stores, async │ ↳ one diagram, no more than a dozen boxes ▼ 6 DEEP DIVE the one or two components that are genuinely hard │ ↳ this is where the interview is actually won ▼ 7 FAILURE + SCALE what breaks first, what you shard, what degrades ↳ and what you deliberately choose not to build yet
Fig. 00.1 — the order of operations used by every case study in Part II

The four numbers that shape every design

Read : write
ratiodecides caching, replication and whether denormalisation pays
Peak QPS
DAU × actions ÷ 86 400 × peak factorpeak is typically 3–10× the average
Working set
hot bytesif it fits in RAM the design is easy; if not, everything changes
Consistency need
per operationa seat and a like have completely different requirements

What separates a strong answer

State assumptions aloud

“I'll assume 100 million daily users, 10:1 read:write, and that a five-second delay on the feed is acceptable but not on the seat lock.” Now every later decision is defensible.

Name the trade-off, then choose

Nobody scores points for listing options. Say which one you'd take, why, and what you'd have to give up. Then say what would change your mind.

Design for the failure

The interesting half of every system is what happens when the queue backs up, the region goes dark, or one tenant sends ten million requests. Get there before you're asked.

The single most common mistake

Jumping to microservices, Kafka and a five-box diagram in the first ninety seconds. The strongest candidates spend the first five minutes on requirements and numbers, draw a deliberately simple architecture, and then evolve it under pressure — “at 500 requests per second this is fine; here is what breaks at 50 000 and what I'd change.” Design is a sequence of justified changes, not a finished picture.

01

Requirements & estimation

A design prompt is deliberately underspecified, and the first ten minutes decide whether you are architecting a real system or decorating a whiteboard. Requirements tell you what to build; the numbers tell you what it must survive — and it is almost always the numbers, not the features, that pick the architecture.

"Design Twitter" is not a specification. It is a negotiation opener. Two engineers given that prompt will produce systems that differ by three orders of magnitude in cost, and both can be correct, because one assumed 100k users in one region with eventual consistency and the other assumed 500M users globally with read-your-writes. Neither design is defensible until the assumptions are on the board. Write them down, in ink, before you draw a single box.

Interrogating a vague prompt

You have roughly five minutes and about eight questions. Spend them on the axes that change the shape of the system, not on product trivia. The test for a good clarifying question is simple: if the answer flipped, would my diagram change? If not, do not ask it.

five minutes of questions, one page of spec "Design X" │ ├──▶ WHO how many users? DAU vs MAU? human or machine callers? │ drives ──▶ QPS, shard count, cost model │ ├──▶ WHAT the 3 core flows only. everything else is v2. │ drives ──▶ API surface, data model │ ├──▶ WHERE one region or global? where do writes originate? │ drives ──▶ replication topology, latency floor │ ├──▶ WHEN how fresh? read-your-writes? bounded stale? tolerable lag? │ drives ──▶ consistency model — the costliest answer │ ├──▶ HOW FAST p50 and p99 targets, per endpoint, not per system │ drives ──▶ cache tiers, sync vs async boundary │ └──▶ NOT explicitly out of scope, said aloud and written down drives ──▶ auth, billing, moderation, analytics output: one spec sheet + one figures block. everything after this is derived, and every box must trace back to a line here.
Fig. 1 — The requirements funnel. Six questions, one spec sheet, zero boxes drawn yet.

The questions worth asking, in the order they pay off:

  1. Users and shape of load. DAU, not registered users. Are requests human-paced (a few per minute, diurnal) or machine-paced (sensors, mobile background sync, retries)? Machine traffic has no diurnal trough and a much worse thundering-herd profile.
  2. The three core flows. Force the scope down to a triple: for a ride-hailing app, request a ride, match a driver, track the trip. If the interviewer wants more they will add it; if you volunteer more you will run out of clock.
  3. Geography. Single region, multi-region active-passive, or active-active? This one question sets your latency floor and decides whether you need conflict resolution at all. Do not let it go unanswered.
  4. Consistency and freshness. Per flow, not per system. "A like count may lag 30 seconds; a payment balance may not lag at all" is a real answer and it partitions your storage immediately.
  5. Latency targets. Ask for p99, not average. An average hides the tail that actually pages you, and the tail is what a design must be built for.
  6. Durability and retention. Can you lose the last second of writes on a node failure? How long must data live? Retention is the single largest multiplier on storage cost.
  7. Out of scope. Say it out loud: "I am not designing auth, payments, moderation, or the analytics pipeline; I will show the hooks." This buys back ten minutes and reads as seniority, not evasion.

Functional versus non-functional, and why only one of them matters

Functional requirements are what the system does: post a photo, follow a user, fetch a feed. They determine your API and your data model, and they are usually the easy part — a CRUD service with a relational schema satisfies the functional requirements of nearly every famous system on the first try. Non-functional requirements are the constraints: scale, latency, availability, consistency, durability, cost. They are what breaks the CRUD service, and therefore they are what you are actually being asked to design for.

Non-functional requirements and the architecture each one forces
RequirementThreshold where it bitesArchitectural consequence
Write throughputBeyond ~10–20k writes/s on one primaryShard by entity key; give up cross-shard transactions
Read throughputRead:write beyond ~10:1Cache tier and read replicas; accept staleness
p99 latencyBelow ~100 ms end to endPrecompute (fan-out on write); no synchronous cross-region hop
AvailabilityAbove 99.9% (43 min/month down)Multi-AZ, no single writer in the request path, graceful degradation
ConsistencyRead-your-writes requiredRoute to primary or use sticky sessions and version tokens
DurabilityZero acknowledged-write lossQuorum or synchronous replication; ~1 RTT added per write
RetentionBeyond ~12 months of hot dataTiering to object storage; a separate cold read path
Say this out loud

"Before I draw anything: the functional requirements here are satisfiable by a single Postgres instance. What makes this a design problem is 30k writes per second, a 250-to-1 read ratio, and a 150 ms p99 for users on three continents. So I am going to let those three numbers pick the architecture, and I will call out where I am trading consistency for them." That sentence reframes the whole interview, and it is exactly how the conversation runs in a real design review.

Arithmetic you can do standing up

Whiteboard estimation is not mental arithmetic; it is rounding aggressively to numbers that cancel, then being honest about the error bar. Two constants carry most of the load. First, 86,400 seconds per day rounds to 10^5 — a 16% overestimate of the day, which conveniently offsets your habit of underestimating peaks. So 1 QPS ≈ 100,000 requests/day, and 1M requests/day ≈ 12 QPS. Second, a year is about 3 × 10^7 seconds. Memorise those two and most estimation collapses into moving decimal points.

Powers of two and the round numbers worth memorising
PowerExactRound asWhy you care
2^101,0241 thousand · KBBase unit of every size estimate
2^201,048,5761 million · MBPhoto, page, message payloads
2^301.07 × 10^91 billion · GBRAM per node, cache sizing
2^324.29 × 10^94 billionIPv4 space; signed int32 overflow at 2.1B
2^401.1 × 10^121 trillion · TBDisk per node, yearly dataset growth
2^501.1 × 10^15PBBlob store totals
2^539.0 × 10^15—Largest exact integer in a JS double: 64-bit IDs break JSON clients
2^639.2 × 10^18—Signed 64-bit ceiling; Snowflake ID headroom

The derivation chain runs in one direction and you should recite it in that order:

  • DAU → QPS. QPS_avg = DAU × actions_per_user_per_day / 86,400. Round 86,400 to 10^5.
  • Average → peak. Multiply by a peak factor. For a consumer app with one dominant timezone, 3–5×. For a global app whose load flattens across timezones, 2×. For anything with a scheduled event — a match kick-off, a ticket drop, a cron fan-out — the factor is not a factor, it is a spike, and you size for the spike or you shed load deliberately.
  • Read:write ratio. Compute both independently, then state the ratio; it is the single number that decides whether you cache, replicate, or precompute. Social feeds run 100:1 to 1000:1. Order systems run closer to 5:1. Telemetry ingestion inverts entirely.
  • Payload → bandwidth. bytes/s = QPS × payload_bytes. Then convert once, at the end: 1 Gbps = 125 MB/s.
  • Rate → storage. writes/day × bytes/write × retention_days, then multiply by replication, then add index overhead. Skipping either multiplier is the most common estimation error in this book.
  • Replication factor. 3× for quorum-replicated databases. 1.3–1.5× for erasure-coded blob storage (a real saving worth naming). Multiply again by the number of regions holding a full copy.
  • Index overhead. Budget 20–40% of table size for a lightly indexed OLTP table, and up to 100% for one with several secondary indexes. A secondary index on a 512-byte row costs roughly the key plus the primary key plus tree overhead — call it 40–60 bytes per row per index.
python · the whole toolkit in twenty lines
SEC_PER_DAY = 86_400          # round to 1e5 on a whiteboard
GB, TB, PB  = 2**30, 2**40, 2**50

def qps(dau, actions_per_day, peak=4):
    avg = dau * actions_per_day / SEC_PER_DAY
    return avg, avg * peak

def bandwidth_gbps(rps, payload_bytes):
    return rps * payload_bytes * 8 / 1e9        # bits, not bytes

def storage(writes_per_day, bytes_per_write, days,
            replication=3.0, index_overhead=0.3):
    logical = writes_per_day * bytes_per_write * days
    return logical * replication * (1 + index_overhead)

def servers(peak_rps, cpu_ms_per_req, cores, target_util=0.5):
    per_core = 1000 / cpu_ms_per_req            # req/s a core sustains
    return peak_rps / (per_core * cores * target_util)

# 200M DAU, 50 photo views each, 150 KB served per view
avg, peak = qps(200e6, 50)                      # 116k/s avg, 463k/s peak
print(bandwidth_gbps(peak, 150 * 1024))         # 569 Gbps at the edge

Latency numbers, and how they compose

Latency is not a property you tune at the end; it is a budget you allocate at the start. Physics sets the floor. Light in fibre covers roughly 200 km per millisecond, so a useful shortcut is RTT_ms ≈ distance_km / 100, with real routes landing 30–50% above that because fibre does not run in straight lines.

The latency ladder — orders of magnitude, not exact values
OperationTimeRelative to L1
L1 cache reference1 ns1×
Branch mispredict3 ns3×
L2 cache reference4 ns4×
Mutex lock/unlock (uncontended)20 ns20×
Main memory reference100 ns100×
Compress 1 KB (Snappy)2 µs2,000×
Send 1 KB over 10 Gbps NIC1 µs1,000×
NVMe SSD random read (4 KB)16 µs16,000×
Read 1 MB sequentially from RAM50 µs50,000×
Read 1 MB sequentially from NVMe300 µs3 × 10^5×
Round trip within one datacentre500 µs5 × 10^5×
Spinning disk seek8 ms8 × 10^6×
Cross-country RTT (SF ↔ NY, 4,100 km)65 ms6.5 × 10^7×
Cross-continent RTT (NY ↔ Frankfurt)90 ms9 × 10^7×
Antipodal RTT (London ↔ Sydney)250 ms2.5 × 10^8×

Three conclusions fall straight out of that table and you should state them as rules. A memory read is a thousand times cheaper than an SSD read, so a cache hit is worth an enormous amount of engineering. One datacentre round trip costs as much as thirty SSD reads, so chatty service meshes lose to a single fatter query. And one cross-continent round trip costs more than everything else in a well-built request put together — which is why a synchronous cross-region write in the user path is a design error, not a tuning problem.

latency budget · feed request · target p99 = 200 ms ├─ TLS + client RTT ████████████████ 40 ms edge PoP, resumed ├─ edge ▶ origin region ████████████████████ 50 ms fixed by geography ├─ LB ▶ app ▌ 1 ms ├─ auth (cached token) ▌ 1 ms ├─ feed cache lookup ██ 3 ms Redis, same AZ ├─ hydrate 30 posts ██████ 12 ms one multi-get ├─ DB fallback on miss ████████████ 25 ms only 8% of requests ├─ render + serialise ████ 8 ms └─ response transfer ████████ 15 ms 180 KB at 100 Mbit ─────────────────────────────── committed 130 ms headroom for GC pause, retry, tail 70 ms ═══════════════════════════════ budget 200 ms
Fig. 2 — A latency budget is a ledger. Allocate first, then build to the allocation.

Two rules govern how these compose. Serial hops add; parallel hops take the maximum — so fan out wherever a dependency graph allows it. And tails compound viciously: if one backend call has a 1% chance of exceeding 100 ms, a request that fans out to ten of them has a 1 - 0.99^10 ≈ 9.6% chance of a slow tail. Ten parallel calls turn a p99 dependency into a p90 user experience. Hedged requests and per-call deadlines exist for exactly this arithmetic.

Little's Law: the bridge between the three numbers

Concurrency, throughput and latency are not independent, and the relationship is one line: L = λW — average number of requests in the system equals arrival rate times average time in system. It holds for any stable system regardless of arrival distribution or scheduling discipline, which is why it is the most useful formula in capacity planning.

L — concurrency
Requests in flight. Sets thread-pool sizes, connection-pool sizes, and memory footprint.
λ — throughput
Arrivals per second. What load tests report and what dashboards graph.
W — latency
Time in system, including queueing. Not service time — the difference is where outages live.

Use it in both directions. Forward: at 185,000 requests/s peak with a 30 ms average latency, you have 185,000 × 0.030 = 5,550 requests in flight; if each holds a database connection, you need 5,550 connections and your pool of 2,000 will become the bottleneck before the database does. Backward: a worker pool of 100 threads processing 200 ms jobs can never exceed 100 / 0.2 = 500 jobs/s, no matter how deep the queue grows — and the queue growing is the symptom, not the cause.

Failure mode · the latency death spiral

Little's Law also explains why overloaded systems collapse rather than degrade. When λ exceeds capacity, W rises; rising W raises L; more in-flight requests mean more memory, more context switching and more GC, which raises W further. Meanwhile clients time out at 1 s and retry, adding to λ. Real incidents look like this: p99 goes from 80 ms to 8 s in under two minutes, CPU sits at 60%, and nothing is obviously broken. The fix is admission control — bound L explicitly with a concurrency limiter and shed the excess with a fast 429 — not more replicas, which arrive after the retry storm has already saturated them.

Worked example: a photo-sharing service

Functional
  • Upload a photo with a caption
  • View a reverse-chronological feed of followed accounts
  • View any user's profile grid
Non-functional
  • 200M DAU, global, three regions
  • Feed p99 under 200 ms at the edge
  • Photos durable forever; feed may lag 5 s
  • 99.95% availability for reads, 99.9% for writes
Out of scope
  • Ranking, ads, moderation
  • Stories, DMs, video
  • Auth and account recovery
Uploads
40M/day463/s avg, ~2k/s peak
Views
10B/day116k/s avg, 463k/s peak
Read:write
250:1caching is mandatory, not an optimisation
Blob growth
100 TB/day37 PB logical per year
Peak egress
~570 Gbps95% must come off the CDN

Traffic. 200M DAU at 0.2 uploads per user per day gives 40M uploads/day; 40M / 86,400 = 463/s average, and a 4× peak factor for a consumer app gives ~2,000 uploads/s. Reads: 50 photo views per DAU per day is 10B views/day, or 10 × 10^9 / 86,400 ≈ 116,000/s average and ~463,000/s at peak. The read:write ratio is 116,000 / 463 ≈ 250:1. That single number, computed in thirty seconds, tells you the entire architecture is a caching and delivery problem with a small write path bolted on.

Storage. A phone-compressed original averages 2 MB. Derivatives — 2048 px at 400 KB, 640 px at 80 KB, 160 px thumbnail at 12 KB — add roughly 0.5 MB, so budget 2.5 MB per upload. Daily growth is 40M × 2.5 MB = 100 TB/day, or 100 TB × 365 ≈ 37 PB logical per year. Blobs are erasure-coded at 1.4× rather than replicated 3× — a saving of 59 PB per year that is worth saying out loud — giving ~52 PB of raw capacity per year, doubled to ~104 PB if a second region holds a full copy.

sql · the metadata row you are about to multiply by 15 billion
CREATE TABLE photo (
  photo_id     BIGINT      PRIMARY KEY,   --  8 B  Snowflake, time-ordered
  owner_id     BIGINT      NOT NULL,      --  8 B
  created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,      --  8 B
  blob_key     CHAR(40)    NOT NULL,      -- 40 B  content hash
  width        INT, height INT,           --  8 B
  caption      VARCHAR(300),              -- 180 B average, not 300
  state        SMALLINT    NOT NULL       --  2 B  pending/live/deleted
);                                        -- ~254 B + ~100 B row overhead
CREATE INDEX ON photo (owner_id, created_at DESC);  -- profile grid
CREATE INDEX ON photo (created_at) WHERE state = 1; -- backfill jobs

Call the row 512 bytes with page overhead and both indexes. Metadata grows at 40M × 512 B ≈ 20 GB/day, or 7.3 TB/year logical, 22 TB/year at 3× replication. That is 0.06% of the blob bytes — and noticing that ratio is the point of the exercise. Metadata fits comfortably in a sharded relational cluster; photos never will. The two must live in different systems, and you have just proved it with arithmetic rather than asserting it from memory.

One year of storage, every multiplier shown
TierLogical/yrRedundancyIndex/overheadProvisioned
Photo blobs37 PB1.4× erasure code—52 PB (×2 regions = 104 PB)
Photo metadata5.8 TB3× quorum+30% indexes22 TB
Follow graph (2B edges, 24 B)48 GB3×+100% (both directions)290 GB
Feed cache (fan-out)—2× replicas500 entries × 16 B × 200M1.6 TB × 2 = 3.2 TB

Bandwidth. Average bytes served per view is ~150 KB, mixing 640 px feed images with occasional full-size opens. Peak egress is 463,000 /s × 150 KB × 8 = 555 Gbps, call it 0.6 Tbps. No origin fleet should serve that. With a 95% CDN hit ratio the origin sees ~28 Gbps at peak, which is four or five well-provisioned boxes — the CDN is not a performance nicety here, it is the difference between a normal cluster and a bespoke one.

Cache sizing. Photo access is brutally recency-skewed: roughly 90% of views land on media less than 72 hours old. Three days of uploads is 120M photos × ~92 KB (feed derivative plus thumbnail) ≈ 11 TB of hot blobs — an easy working set for a CDN tier and the reason the 95% hit ratio above is credible rather than optimistic. For metadata, cache the hot 20% of a year's rows: ~1.5 TB, which at 128 GB usable per node is a 12-node Redis cluster; round to 20 nodes for headroom and replica placement.

Servers. Users do not fetch images through your app tier, so size on feed requests: 200M DAU × 20 feed loads/day = 4B/day, 4 × 10^9 / 86,400 ≈ 46,000/s average, ~185,000/s at peak. At 5 ms of CPU per feed request, one core sustains 200 req/s, a 16-core box sustains 3,200, and at a 50% utilisation target that is 1,600 req/s per box. 185,000 / 1,600 ≈ 116 boxes. Round to 150 for deploy headroom and AZ imbalance, and to ~300 across two active regions. Cross-check with Little's Law: 185,000 × 0.030 s = 5,550 in flight, or 37 per box — comfortable for an async runtime, and a clear warning if anyone proposes one thread per request.

photo service · numbers annotated on the path 2k uploads/s peak 463k views/s peak │ │ ▼ ▼ Upload API ──▶ Object store ◀──────────── CDN edge 95% hit, 11 TB hot presigned PUT 37 PB/yr, EC 1.4x │ 5% miss │ ▲ ▼ │ event │ origin gateway 28 Gbps ▼ │ Transcode workers ────┘ Feed API 185k rps peak · 150 boxes 4 sizes · 400 ms/job │ Little: 2k/s x 0.4s = 800 ├──▶ Feed cache 1.6 TB, 92% hit concurrent slots needed └──▶ Metadata shards 22 TB/yr single writer per shard
Fig. 3 — The same design, with every box carrying the number that justifies it.

Sanity-checking a number

Every estimate should be checked against something you already know, because an order-of-magnitude error is invisible inside the arithmetic and obvious from the outside. Useful anchors: a modern server has 64–256 GB of RAM and 32–128 cores; a single NVMe drive delivers 3 GB/s and ~500k IOPS; a 25 Gbps NIC is standard and 100 Gbps is available; one well-tuned Postgres primary handles roughly 10–20k writes/s; a Kafka broker ingests 100–200 MB/s comfortably; a Redis node serves ~100k ops/s per core. If your design implies 900 GB of RAM on one box or 400k writes/s to a single primary, you have not found a hard problem — you have made an arithmetic mistake, or you have just discovered your shard count.

The second check is a cost sniff test. 100 PB of replicated object storage is a nine-figure annual line item; 0.6 Tbps of CDN egress is eight figures. If a number implies a budget larger than the company, revisit the assumption that produced it — usually retention, usually stated as "forever" by someone who has never paid for forever.

The five errors that ruin an estimate

Forgetting the peak factor

Capacity planned on daily average is capacity that fails at 8pm. Always state the factor and where it came from: 3–5× for one-timezone consumer traffic, 2× for genuinely global load, and a named spike for scheduled events.

Forgetting replication

Logical bytes are not provisioned bytes. Multiply by 3 for quorum stores or 1.4 for erasure coding, then again by regions holding a full copy. This error alone routinely understates storage by 4–6×.

Forgetting indexes and overhead

A 254-byte row is a 512-byte row once page headers, tuple overhead and two secondary indexes are counted. Budget +30% for a lean table and +100% for a heavily indexed one.

Confusing bits and bytes

Network capacity is bits per second, storage and payloads are bytes. 1 Gbps = 125 MB/s. Getting this backwards is an 8× error and it is the one most likely to be caught by the person watching you.

Production failure · New Year's Eve, every year

The canonical version of the peak-factor error is a messaging service sized on daily-average QPS. At midnight local time, message volume runs 10–20× the daily average and arrives in a 60-second window across each timezone in turn. Systems sized on average collapse in a predictable sequence: connection pools saturate, latency rises, clients time out and retry, retries double the offered load, and the fan-out workers back up until the queue exhausts its retention and silently drops. The postmortem always contains the same sentence — "we had provisioned for 2× headroom" — and the fix is always the same pair: a peak factor derived from last year's graph rather than from the mean, and load shedding that returns errors fast instead of queueing indefinitely.

Precision discipline

The fifth error is quoting more precision than your assumptions support. If "50 views per user per day" is a guess accurate to ±50%, then "116,203 QPS" is a lie with three false digits. Say "about 120k QPS, call it half a million at peak" and state the assumption that dominates the error bar. A senior engineer's numbers come with visible error bars; a junior engineer's come with decimal places. When someone challenges an estimate, the right response is never to defend the digits — it is to name which input is softest and show how the conclusion changes if it moves by 2×. If the architecture is unchanged, the estimate has already done its job.

Estimation is not about being right. It is about being wrong by a factor small enough that the design decision stays the same. Every number in this chapter exists to answer one question — does this go on one machine or a thousand, in one region or three, on disk or in RAM, computed now or computed ahead of time. Once the arithmetic answers that, the boxes draw themselves, and the rest of this book is about what goes inside them.

02

The building blocks

Almost every system you will ever design is assembled from about twenty components, and the interesting work is not inventing new ones but knowing precisely what each costs, what it guarantees, and how it fails at 3 a.m. This chapter is the parts catalogue: one decision rule per part, and the failure mode that shows up in the postmortem.

The canonical path, and what each hop buys

Before the catalogue, the map. A request from a phone to a durable write touches at most eight layers, and each layer exists to absorb load or latency that the layer behind it cannot. Latencies below are realistic single-region figures for a warm path; treat them as a budget, not a promise.

+0 ms +25 ms +15 ms +1 ms +6 ms +1 ms ┌─────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌─────────┐ │CLIENT │──▶│DNS │──▶│CDN POP │──▶│L7 LB │──▶│APP TIER │──▶│CACHE │ │local $ │ │anycast │ │TLS term │ │health │ │stateless │ │redis │ └─────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └────────┘ └──────────┘ └────┬────┘ │ miss ~8% ▼ ┌────────────────────┐ │PRIMARY DB +4 ms │ │rel. / sharded │ └─────────┬──────────┘ │ outbox row ▼ ┌──────────┐ ┌───────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌────────────────────────┐ │SEARCH │◀───│STREAM │◀───│EVENT LOG │◀───│RELAY (CDC / poller) │ │OLAP │ │flink/ksql│ │kafka, 7 d │ └────────────────────────┘ │graph │ └───────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────┘ async — off the request budget entirely
Fig. 02.1 — the synchronous spine (top) and the asynchronous fan-out (bottom); nothing below the outbox may block a user

Read the diagram as an economic argument. Each box earlier in the chain is cheaper per request and less durable than the one after it; each box later is more expensive and more truthful. Good design pushes work leftwards until correctness objects.

DNS and anycast

DNS is the first routing decision and the only one you cannot roll back quickly. It maps a name to addresses and, with weighted or geo-aware records, performs coarse traffic steering. Anycast advertises the same IP prefix from many locations so BGP delivers the packet to the topologically nearest one — this is how a CDN or a global load balancer gets a 15 ms first hop worldwide without any DNS trickery.

Decision rule: use anycast for the data path and DNS only for coarse, slow steering; never for failover you need inside five minutes.

Failure mode · the TTL nobody honours

You set a 60-second TTL, drain the region, and 6% of traffic keeps arriving for forty minutes. Resolvers, mobile OSes and JVMs (which historically cached DNS forever under the default networkaddress.cache.ttl) ignore short TTLs. Plan failover at a layer that does not ask a client's permission: withdraw the BGP announcement, or fail over behind a stable anycast VIP.

CDN and edge compute

A CDN is a distributed reverse-proxy cache with a fast, private backbone to your origin. It buys three things: latency (terminate TLS 15 ms from the user instead of 120 ms), origin offload (a 95% hit ratio turns 100k RPS of static traffic into 5k RPS of origin traffic), and a DDoS-absorbing surface. Modern CDNs also run code at the POP — request rewriting, auth token checks, A/B bucketing, personalisation of an otherwise cacheable page.

Decision rule: put everything with a stable URL and a cacheable body behind a CDN; use edge compute only for work that is stateless, sub-millisecond and would otherwise force a cache miss.

The hard part is invalidation. Prefer immutable, content-hashed URLs (/img/a1b2c3.jpg) with a one-year max-age over any purge API; where content must change under a stable URL, use stale-while-revalidate so no user ever blocks on a revalidation round trip.

Load balancers: L4 and L7

An L4 balancer forwards packets or TCP connections by hashing the 4-tuple; it never reads the payload, sustains millions of connections per node, and adds tens of microseconds. An L7 balancer terminates TLS, parses HTTP, and can route by path, header or cookie, retry idempotent requests, shed load, and split traffic 1%/99% for a canary — at the cost of roughly 1 ms and far more CPU.

The proxy family — four boxes that candidates routinely conflate
ComponentSits whereGenuinely providesReach for it when
L4 load balancerIn front of everythingConnection distribution, DSR, huge throughput, protocol-agnostic (gRPC, MQTT, Postgres)You need raw fan-out or non-HTTP traffic
L7 reverse proxyIn front of a serviceTLS termination, path routing, retries, timeouts, compression, response cachingAlways, for HTTP; it is the default edge
API gatewayBetween clients and many servicesAuthN/AuthZ, per-key rate limits, quota and billing, request/response transformation, one public contract over N servicesYou have external or partner clients, or more than ~5 services behind one public API
Service meshSidecar next to every servicemTLS everywhere, per-hop retries/timeouts/circuit breaking, uniform golden-signal telemetry, traffic shiftingYou have tens of services in several languages and cannot standardise a client library

Decision rules: a reverse proxy is a component; a gateway is a product boundary; a mesh is an operations strategy. Below roughly twenty services, a shared client library plus an L7 proxy does 90% of what a mesh does at 5% of the operational cost — a mesh adds a second failure domain (the control plane) and a sidecar's worth of latency and memory to every hop.

yaml · gateway route vs mesh policy — different jobs
# API gateway: the PUBLIC contract. Identity, quota, shape.
route:
  match:   { host: api.example.com, path: /v1/orders/* }
  auth:    { type: oauth2, scopes: [orders.read] }
  ratelimit:
    key: api_key            # per customer, not per IP
    limit: 1000
    window: 60s
    burst: 200
  upstream: orders-service.prod.svc:8080
  timeout: 2s

# Mesh policy: INTERNAL reliability between two services. No identity of the caller's caller.
destination:
  host: payments-service.prod.svc
  tls: { mode: ISTIO_MUTUAL }
  retries:      { attempts: 2, perTryTimeout: 400ms, retryOn: 5xx,connect-failure }
  outlierDetection:                # eject a bad pod, do not retry into it
    consecutive5xx: 5
    baseEjectionTime: 30s
    maxEjectionPercent: 20         # never eject the whole fleet

Stateless application servers

The application tier should hold no durable state: no sticky sessions, no local uploads, no in-memory counters that matter. That single property makes the tier horizontally scalable, trivially replaceable, and safe to deploy with a rolling restart. Sessions move to a signed token or a shared cache; uploads move to object storage; counters move to Redis or the log.

Decision rule: if killing a random app server loses data or logs a user out, it is not stateless, and every scaling and deployment story you tell about it is fiction.

The cache hierarchy

There are four caches in a typical system and they are not interchangeable. Each absorbs a different multiple of load.

1,000 logical reads │ ├─▶ CLIENT / BROWSER hit 40% · 0 ms, 0 bytes on the wire · Cache-Control, ETag │ risk: you cannot invalidate it — only expire it ▼ 600 ├─▶ CDN EDGE hit 85% · 15 ms · shared across all users, purgeable ▼ 90 ├─▶ IN-PROCESS (LRU) hit 50% · 0.001 ms · per pod, so N copies, N truths │ risk: inconsistent across the fleet for the whole TTL ▼ 45 ├─▶ DISTRIBUTED (redis) hit 90% · 0.5 ms · one truth, one more thing to run ▼ 4-5 └─▶ DATABASE 4-5 of the original 1,000 reach disk · 4 ms
Fig. 02.2 — a 1,000-read funnel; four modest hit ratios compound into a 200:1 reduction at the database

The in-process tier is the only one that is free and the only one that is inconsistent by construction: ten pods holding a 60-second copy of a flag disagree for up to 60 seconds. Use it for data that is tiny, near-immutable and read constantly — configuration, currency tables, tokenisers — never for anything a user can watch change.

Failure mode · the stampede that took the database down

A hot key with a 300-second TTL expires. Two thousand concurrent requests miss simultaneously, all issue the same expensive query, and the database — sized for the 5 reads/s it normally sees for that key — collapses. The cache then cannot be repopulated because every filling query times out, so the system stays down after the load subsides. Fixes, in order of value: request coalescing (single-flight, so one filler per key per node), early probabilistic expiry (refresh at ttl - beta * log(rand()) so refreshes spread out), and a stale-on-error serve path. Randomised TTLs alone do not save you from a single hot key.

Relational databases, and the stores that are not

Start relational. A single well-tuned Postgres or MySQL instance on modern NVMe handles 10–50k simple reads/s and 5–10k writes/s, and gives you transactions, joins, secondary indexes, constraints and a query planner that will outlive three of your microservices. Most designs that "need NoSQL" need an index and a cache.

You leave relational for three honest reasons: write volume past what one primary can take (roughly 50k writes/s of small rows), an access pattern the model serves badly (full-text ranking, k-NN, multi-hop traversal, downsampling), or a cost profile where a purpose-built engine is an order of magnitude cheaper.

Storage engines — one decision rule each
StoreData modelReach for it whenWhat it costs you
Relational (Postgres, MySQL)Tables, joins, ACIDDefault. Entities with relationships and invariantsOne writable primary; schema changes need care at scale
Key-value (Redis, DynamoDB)Opaque value by keyYou know the key on every access and never query by anything elseNo joins, no ad-hoc queries; every access pattern must be designed up front
Document (MongoDB, DocumentDB)Nested JSON per aggregateThe aggregate is read and written whole and varies in shapeCross-document consistency is manual; unbounded arrays become hot documents
Wide-column (Cassandra, HBase, Bigtable)Partition key + sorted clustering keyEnormous write volume, time- or id-ordered range scans, multi-region active-activeLast-write-wins semantics, tombstones, no joins; a bad partition key is unfixable
Time-series (Prometheus, InfluxDB, Timescale)(series, timestamp) → valueAppend-only metrics with downsampling and retention tiersCardinality explosions; one unbounded label kills the cluster
Graph (Neo4j, JanusGraph)Nodes and edgesQueries are genuinely 3+ hops and variable depthHard to shard; usually a recursive CTE or an adjacency table would have done
Search (Elasticsearch, OpenSearch)Inverted index + BM25Ranked free-text, facets, typo toleranceNear-real-time, not real-time; it is a derived index and must be rebuildable
Object storage (S3, GCS)Immutable blobs by keyAnything over ~100 KB: media, backups, logs, data-lake files~20–100 ms first byte; no partial update; list operations are slow and costly

The rule that matters: a search index, an OLAP table and a graph projection are derived state. They must be reconstructible from the system of record, or you have silently acquired a second primary database with none of a primary's guarantees.

Object storage and the origin

Never stream user uploads or downloads through your application tier. Issue a presigned URL and let the client talk to object storage directly; your app handles a 200-byte authorisation, not a 50 MB body. Object storage then acts as the CDN's origin, and the CDN serves 95%+ of reads without touching your infrastructure at all.

Queues versus event logs

These are drawn identically and behave nothing alike. A queue (SQS, RabbitMQ) distributes work: a message goes to one consumer, is acknowledged, and disappears. A log (Kafka, Pulsar, Kinesis) is an ordered, replayable, retained sequence: many independent consumer groups read the same records at their own offsets, and the record stays for its retention window regardless.

QUEUE — work distribution LOG — shared fact stream ┌──────────────────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────────────────────┐ │ msg ▶ ▶ ▶ ▶ ▶ (consumed) │ │ partition 0: [0][1][2][3][4][5] │ │ │ │ │ ▲ ▲ ▲ │ │ ▼ │ │ grp A grp B grp C │ │ worker pool (any one) │ │ off=2 off=4 off=5 │ └──────────────────────────────┘ └───────────────────────────────────┘ ack ▶ gone forever retention ▶ 7 days, replay any time order: none (per-msg) order: total, per partition only scaling: add workers freely scaling: capped by partition count redelivery: visibility timeout redelivery: rewind the offset use: send email, resize image use: CDC, audit, feed N consumers
Fig. 02.3 — the same box on a whiteboard, two entirely different contracts

Decision rule: if exactly one consumer must do the job once, use a queue; if several independent consumers need the same facts, or you will ever want to replay history into a new consumer, use a log. Ordering in a log is per partition, so the partition key is your ordering guarantee — key by entity id (user, order, account) and never by anything random.

Failure mode · the queue as landfill

Producers outpace consumers by 5%. For six hours nothing looks wrong; then the queue holds 40 million messages, consumer lag is nine hours, and every message you now process is acting on stale state. A queue is a shock absorber, not storage. Alarm on age of the oldest unprocessed message, not on depth — depth tells you nothing without throughput — and decide in advance whether you drain, shard, or drop.

Getting data out of the database reliably

The most common correctness bug in an event-driven design is a dual write: commit to the database, then publish to Kafka, and lose the event whenever the process dies in between. The transactional outbox removes the gap by making the event part of the same transaction.

sql · transactional outbox
CREATE TABLE outbox (
  id           BIGSERIAL PRIMARY KEY,
  aggregate_id TEXT        NOT NULL,   -- becomes the partition key: preserves per-entity order
  event_type   TEXT        NOT NULL,
  payload      JSONB       NOT NULL,
  created_at   TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  published_at TIMESTAMPTZ
);

-- Only unpublished rows are indexed: the index stays small no matter how large the table grows.
CREATE INDEX outbox_unpublished ON outbox (id) WHERE published_at IS NULL;

BEGIN;
  UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 2500 WHERE id = 'acc_71' AND balance >= 2500;
  INSERT INTO outbox (aggregate_id, event_type, payload)
       VALUES ('acc_71', 'funds.debited', '{"minor_units":2500,"ccy":"GBP"}');
COMMIT;   -- state change and its announcement commit together, or neither does

-- Relay: one worker per shard, SKIP LOCKED so N relays never fight over the same rows.
SELECT id, aggregate_id, event_type, payload
  FROM outbox
 WHERE published_at IS NULL
 ORDER BY id
 LIMIT 500
   FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED;

The relay publishes at-least-once, so consumers must be idempotent — key every side effect by (aggregate_id, id) and make replay a no-op. This is not optional: at-least-once plus a non-idempotent consumer is a duplicate charge waiting for its first network blip.

Stream processors and batch

A stream processor (Flink, Spark Structured Streaming, Kafka Streams) does stateful computation over a log — windowed aggregation, stream joins, sessionisation, dedup — with event-time watermarks so late data is handled deliberately rather than accidentally. Batch/ETL does the same computation over bounded data, cheaply and hours later.

Decision rule: stream when the answer loses value in minutes (fraud, trending, live counters); batch when it does not (billing, reporting, training, reconciliation). Batch is roughly 5–10× cheaper per unit of computation and far easier to debug, because you can simply re-run it.

Coordination services — and what they are genuinely for

ZooKeeper, etcd and Consul implement consensus (ZAB or Raft) over a small, strongly consistent key-value store. Every write must reach a quorum and be fsynced, so throughput is modest — roughly 10k small writes/s for etcd, far less across regions — and the dataset should stay under a few hundred megabytes. They exist for exactly four jobs.

Leader election
Exactly one instance runs the cron, the compactor, the shard rebalancer. Implemented as a lease with a TTL that the leader renews; correctness depends on fencing tokens, not on the lease alone.
Dynamic configuration
Cluster membership, shard maps, topology. Watched by clients, cached locally, and used on every request from that local copy.
Service discovery
Which instances are healthy right now. Consumed via a sidecar or client library that caches aggressively and keeps serving the last known-good set when the registry is unreachable.
Distributed locks
Mutual exclusion across processes — the most abused of the four, because a lock in an asynchronous network is an optimisation, never a guarantee.
Failure mode · the control plane in the request path

Two related outages recur across the industry. First: a service calls etcd or Consul per request to resolve a dependency; the coordination cluster degrades, and every service that ever discovered anything goes down with it. Cache the topology in-process and fail static — stale routing beats no routing. Second: a process holds a lock, is paused by a 20-second GC or a VM migration, its lease expires, a second process takes the lock, and then the first wakes and writes as though it still owns it. A lease alone cannot stop this; the resource must reject writes carrying a stale fencing token — a monotonically increasing number issued with the lock and checked on every write.

Decision rule: put configuration, membership and elections in a coordination service; put nothing on its request path. If you find yourself needing a distributed lock for correctness, ask instead whether the operation can be made idempotent or serialised by a single-writer partition — both are cheaper and actually correct.

The control plane: limits, schedules, flags, secrets, telemetry

Rate limiter

Protects a shared resource from any single tenant. Token bucket for smooth limits with burst; sliding window for strict quotas. Enforce per API key at the gateway, and add a per-user or per-IP layer for anonymous endpoints.

Scheduler

Runs work at a time or on an interval. Needs leader election (so it fires once), a durable record of the last successful run (so a deploy does not skip a window), and per-job idempotency (so a retry is harmless).

Feature flags / config

Decouples deploy from release. Evaluated in-process against a cached ruleset with a fail-open default; the SDK must work correctly with the flag service completely unreachable.

Secrets

Vault, KMS, or cloud secret manager. Short-lived, rotatable credentials fetched at start-up and refreshed on a lease. Never in an environment variable that lands in a crash dump or a log line.

Observability

Metrics for what (RED and USE, low cardinality, seconds of delay), logs for detail (structured, sampled, expensive), traces for where (a request id across every hop). Three signals, three costs, three purposes.

Job/queue backpressure

Bounded queues, concurrency limits and load shedding at the edge. A system that queues without bound converts a latency problem into an outage; one that sheds 2% converts it into a slightly worse Tuesday.

lua · atomic token bucket (redis EVAL) — plaintext
-- KEYS[1] bucket key   ARGV: rate/sec, burst, now (ms), cost
-- Atomic on one node: refill, test, deduct. No read-modify-write race between pods.
local rate, burst, now, cost = tonumber(ARGV[1]), tonumber(ARGV[2]), tonumber(ARGV[3]), tonumber(ARGV[4])
local b     = redis.call('HMGET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', 'ts')
local tok   = tonumber(b[1]) or burst
local ts    = tonumber(b[2]) or now

tok = math.min(burst, tok + (now - ts) / 1000 * rate)   -- refill by elapsed time
local ok = tok >= cost
if ok then tok = tok - cost end

redis.call('HMSET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', tok, 'ts', now)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], math.ceil(burst / rate * 1000) * 2)  -- idle buckets self-clean
return { ok and 1 or 0, math.floor(tok) }

One Redis node running this script sustains roughly 100k decisions per second. Beyond that, do not shard the counter — give each gateway node a local bucket of global_rate / node_count tokens and reconcile every few seconds, trading exactness at the boundary for one fewer network hop per request. For a limiter that is almost always the right trade.

Assembly patterns

THE CATALOGUE ENDS; COMPOSITION BEGINS
Four ways the same parts get wired together
PatternShapeBuysCosts
Layered webCDN → LB → stateless app → cache → relational DBSimplicity; one place to reason about consistency; fastest to build and operateRead and write scale share one primary; heavy reads eventually need replicas or sharding
CQRSWrite model to the primary; read models projected into caches, search, or denormalised tablesRead paths shaped exactly for their queries; read and write scale independentlyEventual consistency the user can see (read-your-writes must be engineered), plus N projections to rebuild and monitor
Event-drivenServices publish facts to a log; consumers reactLoose coupling; new consumers without touching producers; a durable audit trailNo global transaction; debugging spans many hops; schema evolution becomes a contract negotiation
Lambda / KappaLambda: a fast approximate streaming layer beside an accurate batch layer. Kappa: one streaming pipeline, replayed from the log to correct itselfLambda gives seconds-fresh answers with a nightly-correct backstop; Kappa gives one codebaseLambda means the same logic implemented twice and drifting; Kappa demands long retention and genuinely replayable state
Chosen · Kappa (one streaming pipeline, replay to correct)
  • One implementation of the business logic, so batch and stream cannot disagree.
  • Corrections are a replay from a retained offset, not a separate nightly job.
  • Backfilling a new consumer uses the same code path as live traffic — tested continuously.
Rejected · Lambda (parallel batch + speed layers)
  • Two codebases computing the same metric drift within a quarter; reconciling them becomes a standing task.
  • Every logic change must ship twice, in two languages and two runtimes.
  • Justified only when the batch engine can express something the streaming engine genuinely cannot — a full re-scan over years of cold storage, for instance.

Draw fewer boxes

Whiteboard designs fail far more often from unearned complexity than from missing components. A CDN, a load balancer, a stateless app tier, Postgres, Redis, S3 and one queue will serve a product with ten million users and a few thousand requests per second. Every additional box adds a failure mode, a consistency question, an on-call runbook and a person who has to understand it.

Minimum viable stack
7 boxesCDN, LB, app, cache, RDBMS, object store, queue — good to ~10k RPS
Typical interview drawing
15–20 boxesMost of them unjustified; each one is a question you must now answer
Cost of one extra store
~1 engineerSchema, backups, capacity, upgrades, on-call, and a rebuild path
Reads a single Postgres serves
10–50k/sWith an index and a warm buffer cache, before any sharding
Interview · say this out loud when you place a component

"I'm starting with the smallest stack that meets the requirements: CDN, L7 load balancer, a stateless app tier, Postgres as the system of record, Redis for the hot read path, and S3 for media. I'm not adding Kafka, a search cluster or a mesh yet, because nothing in the requirements needs them." Then earn each addition against a number: "the requirement says search must tolerate typos and rank by relevance, which Postgres full-text will not do well at 50M documents — so I'm adding Elasticsearch as a derived index, fed from the outbox, and I can rebuild it from Postgres in a few hours if it is lost." That sentence — a component, the requirement that forced it, and its recovery story — is what separates a senior answer from a box collection. Interviewers are far more impressed by a defensible omission than by an extra box.

The one-line rules, condensed

Anycast for the data path, DNS for slow steering. CDN everything with a stable URL. L7 by default; a gateway when there is an external contract; a mesh only past ~20 services. Stateless app tier or you have no scaling story. Cache in tiers and coalesce every fill. Relational until a number forces you out. Object storage for anything over 100 KB, accessed by presigned URL. Queue for work, log for facts. Stream when staleness costs money, batch otherwise. Coordination services for config and elections, never on the request path. And when in doubt, delete a box.

03

Choosing a data store

A store is not chosen by name, benchmark or fashion; it is chosen by the shape of the queries the product will actually issue, the consistency each of those queries demands, and how much of the data has to stay hot. Choose wrong and the remedy is not a refactor — it is a dual-write migration measured in quarters, run while the business keeps shipping on top of the mistake.

Start from access patterns, not from families

Before naming a single product, write the access-pattern sheet: every operation the system performs, the predicate it filters on, its rate, its latency budget and the consistency it needs. That sheet is the specification the store must satisfy. Nearly every bad choice in production traces to skipping it — a team picks a store for its write throughput, then discovers the product needs an ad-hoc filter on a non-key column; or picks a relational engine, then discovers one table absorbs 200 000 writes per second.

The sheet does the work because storage engines are not general-purpose. Each one makes a small set of accesses cheap by making everything else expensive. A B-tree makes ordered range scans and secondary indexes cheap, and pays for it with random write amplification. An LSM tree makes sequential ingest cheap, and pays for it with read amplification and compaction debt. An inverted index makes relevance ranking cheap, and pays for it by not being transactional. You are not shopping for features; you are picking which asymmetry you want.

every operation the product issues │ ┌─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ key known at predicate is predicate is read time? a range / join free text │ │ │ ┌────┴─────┐ ┌────┴─────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ search index single key + time needs scans derived · never truth entity ordering joins + billions │ │ txns of rows ▼ ▼ │ │ KV wide-column ▼ ▼ doc partition key relational columnar │ only OLTP OLAP │ │ └──────────────▶ if you cannot name the read key, you do not have a KV workload
Fig. 1 — The funnel runs from the query to the family, never the other way round

The six questions that actually decide it

  1. What are the access patterns? Enumerate them. For each, name the exact predicate. "Fetch a user by id" and "list users in a city sorted by signup date" are different stores' problems. If more than about 20 % of your reads use predicates you cannot enumerate today, you need a relational engine or a derived search index — not a partition-key store.
  2. What is the read:write ratio? A 1000:1 ratio (product catalogue) is solved by caching and read replicas; almost any store works. A 1:1 or write-heavy ratio (telemetry, chat, feeds, audit logs) eliminates the single-primary designs immediately, because you cannot cache a write.
  3. What consistency does each operation need — individually? Consistency is per-operation, not per-system. "Debit the wallet" needs serialisable. "Show the follower count" tolerates 30 seconds of staleness. Designing the whole store for the strictest operation is the commonest way to overspend by an order of magnitude.
  4. How big is the working set? Not the total data — the bytes touched in a typical five-minute window. If the working set fits in RAM across a modest cluster, an in-memory store or a well-warmed buffer pool solves latency outright. If it does not, you are designing for disk and every random read costs an SSD round trip (~100 µs) or a page fault storm.
  5. What is the growth curve? Linear with users, quadratic with relationships, or unbounded with time? Event and log data is unbounded and needs retention or tiering designed on day one. Entity data grows with the business and is usually fine.
  6. Do you need transactions across entities? If a single user action must atomically mutate two or more entities that could live on different shards, you either keep them in one relational database or you build sagas with compensating actions and accept intermediate states visible to users. Decide this before choosing; it is the hardest thing to retrofit.

Question three is the one that separates a design from a shopping list, so make it concrete. The same product will contain operations three orders of magnitude apart in what they demand:

Consistency is a per-operation property — pricing the strictest one across the whole system is the classic overspend
OperationNeedsImplication for the store
Debit a wallet, reserve the last seatSerialisable, or single-row atomic with a conditional writeOne shard owns the balance; a relational primary or a conditional-update KV. Never a cache-then-write pattern.
Change a password, revoke a tokenRead-your-writes, globally, within secondsRead from the primary after write, or route the session to the primary for a bounded window.
Post a message to a roomDurable and ordered within the room onlyQuorum write to the room's partition; no global ordering required, which is what makes it scale.
Render a follower count, a trending listBounded staleness — 30 s is invisible to usersServe from cache or a derived counter; a stale read here costs nothing and saves a primary round trip.
Weekly revenue reportCorrect as of a stated cut-off, hours oldColumnar warehouse fed by batch or CDC; never the OLTP primary.
What a strong candidate says

"Before I name a database I want the access-pattern sheet, because the answer is determined by three numbers: the write rate on the hottest entity, the fraction of reads whose predicate I cannot enumerate, and whether any operation needs a cross-entity transaction. Here those are 40k writes/s, under 5 %, and no — so this is a wide-column workload with a relational store retained for accounts and billing. If the write rate were 4k/s I would use one Postgres primary and stop, because operational simplicity is worth more than headroom I do not need."

The families, with their real failure modes

What follows is the honest version: what each family makes cheap, and what it does at 3 a.m.

Family cheat sheet — per-node figures are order-of-magnitude for commodity NVMe hardware
FamilyMakes cheapCannot doWrite ceiling / nodeSignature failure at 3 a.m.
Relational (Postgres, MySQL)Joins, constraints, ACID transactions, ad-hoc predicates, mature toolingScale writes past one primary without sharding~10k–30k simple write txns/sWAL fsync and index maintenance saturate; replication lag climbs; a long transaction blocks vacuum and the table bloats
Document (MongoDB, DocumentDB)Embedded aggregates fetched in one read; heterogeneous shape; schema evolution without migrationsJoins across collections at scale; multi-document transactions cheaply~10k–20k writes/sUnbounded array growth inside a document rewrites the whole document each append; a hot shard key serialises the cluster
Key-value (Redis, Memcached, DynamoDB)O(1) get/put by key at sub-millisecond p99Anything not keyed: no scans, no secondary predicates worth the name100k+ ops/s (in-memory)Hot key or hot partition throttles while the cluster idles; eviction silently drops data an application treated as durable
Wide-column (Cassandra, HBase, ScyllaDB)Enormous linear-scaling ingest; ordered reads within a partition; tunable per-query consistencyQuery by anything but the partition key; joins; aggregation~10k–20k writes/s, scales linearly with nodesAn unbounded partition grows past ~100 MB and read latency and GC pauses explode; ALLOW FILTERING turns one query into a cluster-wide scan; tombstones from deletes make reads slower than writes
Time-series (Prometheus, InfluxDB, TimescaleDB)10–20x compression on regular numeric series, downsampling, automatic retentionHigh-cardinality dimensions; entity-shaped readsMillions of samples/sCardinality explosion — someone puts a request id or user id in a label and the index outgrows RAM
Graph (Neo4j, JanusGraph)Variable-depth traversal at index-free adjacency cost — O(degree) per hop rather than a join per hopScale horizontally cleanly; bulk analyticsModest; usually single-writerA supernode with millions of edges makes every traversal through it quadratic
Search (Elasticsearch, OpenSearch, Solr)Inverted index, relevance ranking, faceting, typo tolerance, aggregation over textBe a source of truth: no real transactions, near-real-time not real-timeIndexing-bound; refresh interval ~1 sSegment merges stall indexing; a mapping change requires a full reindex; data written only here is data you cannot prove
Object storage (S3, GCS, Azure Blob)Cheap, durable (11 nines), effectively unbounded immutable blobs; direct client upload/downloadLow-latency small reads; mutation in place; querying content~3.5k PUT/s, 5.5k GET/s per prefixSomeone treats it as a filesystem — LIST on a million-object prefix, or read-modify-write races with no locking
Columnar / OLAP (ClickHouse, BigQuery, Snowflake, Redshift)Scans and aggregates over 10⁹–10¹² rows by reading only the referenced columnsPoint lookups, single-row updates, high-concurrency OLTPBulk ingest; poor at per-row writesAn application points its live API at the warehouse; concurrency collapses and one analyst's query starves the product

Three of these deserve extra emphasis because they are misapplied most often.

Wide-column is a contract

Cassandra does not give you a database; it gives you a distributed hash table with sorted rows inside each bucket. You must know the partition key at query time, always. That is not a limitation to work around — it is the entire reason it ingests linearly. Model one table per query and duplicate the data.

Document is relational without the safety net

Embedding an aggregate is a genuine win when the aggregate is bounded and always read whole — an order with its line items. It is a trap when the embedded collection grows without limit, or when a second access pattern needs the children independently.

Columnar is not "fast Postgres"

A warehouse scanning 2 GB/s per node will beat an OLTP engine at a cohort query by 100x and lose to it by 1000x on WHERE id = 42. They are not substitutes; they sit at opposite ends of one pipeline.

Sizing: do the arithmetic before the argument

Take a messaging product: 200 M monthly users, 40 M daily active, each sending 40 messages a day, average payload 400 bytes plus ~200 bytes of metadata.

Write rate
~18k/s40M x 40 / 86 400 = 18.5k avg; x3 evening peak ≈ 56k/s
Ingest volume
~1 TB/day1.6B msgs x 600 B ≈ 960 GB/day, ~350 TB/year before replication
Replicated
~1 PB/yrRF=3 turns 350 TB into ~1 PB of disk to buy and repair
Working set
~30 GBReads concentrate on the last hour: 18.5k/s x 3600 x 600 B ≈ 40 GB — cacheable

Those four numbers settle the design without a single opinion being expressed. 56k writes/s at peak is four to six times what one relational primary will absorb, so the message table cannot live on a single primary. A 30–40 GB working set fits in a modest Redis or in the page cache of the storage nodes, so recent-message reads will be fast whatever the storage engine. And 1 PB a year means retention and tiering are a functional requirement, not an optimisation: cold messages move to object storage or the business signs up for a permanently growing bill.

Chosen · Wide-column for the message log
  • Every read is "last N messages in room R" — the partition key is known at query time, 100 % of the time.
  • Linear write scaling: 56k/s peak lands on ~12–16 nodes with headroom, and doubling traffic means adding nodes, not re-architecting.
  • Per-query tunable consistency: write at QUORUM, read the room list at ONE.
  • Cost: no joins, no ad-hoc queries, and time-bucketed partitions must be designed up front to stay under ~100 MB.
Rejected · Sharded relational
  • Application-level sharding by room id gives the same write scaling but you now own rebalancing, cross-shard queries and per-shard failover.
  • The joins and constraints you sharded to keep stop working across shards anyway — you paid the price and lost the benefit.
  • Justified only if messages genuinely need transactions with other entities. They do not.
sql · the same domain in two engines
-- RELATIONAL: normalised, query shape is free, one primary absorbs every write.
CREATE TABLE message (
  id          bigserial   PRIMARY KEY,
  room_id     bigint      NOT NULL REFERENCES room(id),
  sender_id   bigint      NOT NULL REFERENCES account(id),
  body        text        NOT NULL,
  created_at  timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
CREATE INDEX ON message (room_id, created_at DESC);
-- Any predicate works: sender, date range, join to account, COUNT(*) GROUP BY room.
-- Ceiling: ~10k-30k write txns/s. The wall is WAL fsync + index maintenance, not CPU.

-- WIDE-COLUMN (CQL): the query IS the schema. Partition = room+bucket, clustering = time.
CREATE TABLE message_by_room (
  room_id   uuid,
  bucket    text,        -- 'YYYY-MM': keeps any one partition under ~100 MB
  msg_id    timeuuid,
  sender_id uuid,
  body      text,
  PRIMARY KEY ((room_id, bucket), msg_id)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (msg_id DESC);
-- SELECT * FROM message_by_room WHERE room_id=? AND bucket=? LIMIT 50
--   -> one coordinator, one replica set, one sequential read. Scales linearly.
-- SELECT * FROM message_by_room WHERE sender_id=?
--   -> impossible. You write a second table, message_by_sender, at insert time.
Failure mode · unbounded partitions

The most common Cassandra outage is self-inflicted at schema time. Partition by room_id alone and a busy support channel accumulates tens of millions of rows in one partition. Reads of that partition pull enormous rows into heap, the JVM enters back-to-back full GCs, the node stops responding to gossip, the ring marks it down, hinted handoff piles up on its peers, and the failure propagates. The fix — a time bucket in the partition key — is trivial before launch and requires rewriting every row afterwards.

Derived stores are never the system of record

Exactly one store may accept a write as authoritative. Everything else — cache, search index, warehouse, materialised view, read replica — is derived: rebuildable, by definition, from the system of record. This is not a style preference. It is what makes the rest of the architecture recoverable.

Never · search or cache as truth

Search engines lose writes by design and by accident: indexing is asynchronous (a default 1 s refresh interval means a document acknowledged is not a document searchable), mappings cannot be changed without a full reindex, and historic cluster-partition behaviour in Elasticsearch dropped acknowledged documents outright. Caches lose writes by policy: maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru will evict anything under pressure, and a Redis instance with RDB snapshots every 60 seconds loses up to a minute of writes on an unclean restart. Neither has cross-entity transactions. If the only copy of a fact is in your search index or your cache, you have chosen an architecture in which a routine reindex or an OOM kill is a data-loss incident. Write to the source of truth, then project into the derived store.

Polyglot persistence, and what it actually costs

Using several stores is normal and often correct: accounts in Postgres, messages in Cassandra, search in Elasticsearch, media in S3, analytics in ClickHouse. The cost is not licence fees — it is that every additional store adds a consistency boundary you must define, a failure mode you must handle, and an on-call skill set you must maintain. Three stores mean three backup and restore procedures, three upgrade paths, three sets of client library quirks, and three ways for the same fact to disagree with itself.

Pay that cost deliberately, and pay it once, by adopting a single fan-out discipline: writes go to the system of record, changes propagate outward via change data capture, and every derived store publishes an explicit staleness budget the product has agreed to.

writes (the only place a write is accepted) │ ┌────────▼─────────┐ │ Postgres │ │ system of record│ └────────┬─────────┘ │ WAL ▶ CDC (Debezium ▶ Kafka) ┌───────────────┬───────────────┼───────────────┬───────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ Redis Elasticsearch ClickHouse S3 read replica cache inverted index columnar OLAP blobs, archive same engine stale ≤ 5 s stale ≤ 30 s stale ≤ 5 min immutable lag ≤ 1 s │ │ │ │ │ └───────────────┴───────────────┴───────────────┴───────────────┘ every one is rebuildable from truth · none accepts a write anti-pattern: application dual-writes to Postgres AND Elasticsearch in one request handler. The second write fails, the first is committed, and the two stores diverge permanently with no process that will ever reconcile them.
Fig. 2 — One writer, many readers; staleness is a published number, not an accident

A worked decision table

Common workloads mapped to a store, with the reason the obvious alternative loses
WorkloadDeciding access patternStoreWhy not the alternative
Accounts, billing, entitlementsLow volume, ad-hoc predicates, cross-entity transactions mandatoryRelational (Postgres)Document stores make the invariants the auditor asks about unenforceable
Sessions, rate-limit counters, feature flagsGet/put by key, sub-ms p99, tolerable to loseKey-value (Redis)Postgres works but wastes a durable write on data with a 30-minute lifespan
Product catalogue, CMS contentRead-dominated 1000:1, heterogeneous attributes per categoryRelational with JSONB, cachedA dedicated document store rarely earns a second operational surface at this volume
Chat history, activity feeds, IoT events, audit logAppend-only, 10k+ writes/s, always read by a known key + time orderWide-column (Cassandra)Sharded relational costs the same effort and loses joins anyway
Metrics, dashboards, alertingRegular numeric samples, low cardinality, aggregate over windows, retentionTime-series (Prometheus, Timescale)Generic stores lack delta-of-delta compression, downsampling and TTL — 10–20x more disk
Friend-of-friend, fraud rings, permission inheritanceVariable-depth traversal, 3+ hops, path-shaped answersGraph (Neo4j)Recursive SQL costs an index lookup per hop and degrades non-linearly with depth
Product / document search with facetsFree-text predicate, relevance ranking, typo toleranceSearch index (Elasticsearch), derivedPostgres full-text handles modest corpora; neither may be the source of truth
Images, video, backups, model weightsImmutable blobs, MB–GB each, served via CDN or signed URLObject storage (S3)Blobs in a database destroy buffer-cache efficiency and make backups unmanageable
Funnels, cohorts, BI over 10¹⁰ rowsScan and aggregate few columns over enormous row counts, low concurrencyColumnar (ClickHouse, BigQuery)Running these on the OLTP primary is how analysts take production down
Job queue / outboxClaim-one-and-lock, at-least-once, ordering per keyPostgres SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED under ~5k jobs/s; a log broker above itReaching for Kafka at 50 jobs/s buys a cluster to operate and no capability you need
Default

When the sheet does not clearly point elsewhere, choose the relational engine. It is the only family that stays correct while your access patterns change, and access patterns change more often than scale arrives. Move an entity out only when a specific number — write rate, working-set size, scan volume — proves it no longer fits.

The migration cost of getting it wrong

Changing a store under a live product is the most expensive routine work in infrastructure, because there is no atomic switch. You run both stores simultaneously, sometimes for months, while every feature team keeps writing code against a moving target. Budget it honestly: for a core entity in a system with meaningful traffic, expect one to two engineers for a quarter, plus the risk of silent divergence for the entire dual-write window.

PHASE 0 read A ─────────────────────────────────────────────────▶ baseline write A PHASE 1 read A ───────────────────────────────────────────────────▶ write A then B (B async, failures logged not raised) divergence starts here PHASE 2 backfill B from an A snapshot at LSN/timestamp T ↳ reconcile: replay CDC from T ▶ B, then diff-scan A vs B PHASE 3 read A + shadow-read B on 1 % of traffic, compare, alert on mismatch ↳ gate: mismatch rate < 1 in 10⁶ sustained for 7 days PHASE 4 read B, write A + B ◀── rollback point: flip reads back to A PHASE 5 read B, write B; A read-only for 30 days ── then drop A Skipping phase 3 is how teams discover, after decommissioning A, that 0.4 % of rows never migrated because a nullable column serialised differently.
Fig. 3 — The only safe migration is boring, gated and reversible until phase 5
javascript · phase 3 shadow read
// Serve from truth. Compare against the candidate off the hot path. Never block on B.
async function getProfile(userId) {
  const truth = await pg.getProfile(userId);

  if (Math.random() < 0.01) {                  // 1 % sample keeps p99 unchanged
    queueMicrotask(async () => {
      try {
        const candidate = await dynamo.getProfile(userId);
        const a = normalise(truth), b = normalise(candidate);
        if (!deepEqual(a, b)) {
          metrics.increment('migration.mismatch', { entity: 'profile' });
          log.warn({ userId, diff: diffKeys(a, b) }, 'shadow read mismatch');
        }
      } catch (err) {
        metrics.increment('migration.shadow_error', { entity: 'profile' });
      }
    });
  }
  return truth;                                 // A is still the source of truth
}

// normalise() is the load-bearing function: it strips fields that legitimately
// differ (write timestamps, server-assigned ids) and canonicalises the ones that
// only LOOK different -- null vs absent, integer vs numeric-string, timezone
// offsets, float precision. Every migration mismatch report is dominated by
// these before it surfaces a single real bug.

Two economies make the difference. First, migrate one entity at a time, never the whole schema; each entity gets its own phase ladder and its own rollback. Second, put the store behind a repository interface before you start, so feature teams keep coding against one API while the implementation moves underneath. Teams that skip the second step end up freezing feature work during the migration, which is how a one-quarter project becomes a two-quarter one with an angry product organisation attached.

System of record
The single store authorised to accept a write as authoritative. Every other copy is derived and must be rebuildable from it without human judgement.
Working set
The bytes actually touched in a short window, not the total stored. Latency design targets this number; capacity planning targets the total.
Staleness budget
The maximum lag a derived store is allowed and the product has agreed to, expressed in seconds and monitored as an SLO — not an accident to be discovered.
Write amplification
Bytes physically written per logical byte. B-trees pay it in random page writes and index maintenance; LSM trees pay it in compaction. It is the real ceiling on a single node.
Polyglot persistence
Using several stores for their respective strengths. Correct when each is justified by a specific access pattern, expensive when each adds a consistency boundary and an on-call skill set.

The discipline reduces to one habit: state the numbers, then state the decision and its cost. "We chose a wide-column store for the message log because peak write rate is 56k/s and every read carries the room id; the cost is that we can never query by sender without a second table, and every partition needs a time bucket." That sentence is a data store choice. Anything shorter is a preference.

04

Consistency, CAP & PACELC

Consistency is not a dial with two settings; it is a family of guarantees, each with a different price in latency, availability and engineering effort. The senior move is never to pick one for the whole system — it is to name the guarantee each individual operation requires, and to be able to say what breaks if it is weakened.

Definitions, before the slogans

The word consistency is used for at least three unrelated things, and conversations collapse when they are not separated. Separate them once, here, and the rest of the chapter is mechanical.

C in ACID
A database invariant holds before and after a transaction: foreign keys resolve, CHECK constraints pass, balances stay non-negative. This is a property of your schema, enforced by the engine. It has nothing to do with distribution.
C in CAP
Linearizability. Every operation appears to take effect at a single instant between its invocation and its response, and once a write returns, every subsequent read anywhere returns that value or a later one. It is a property of a single object, not a whole transaction.
Isolation
How concurrent transactions interleave. Serialisability means the outcome equals some serial order of those transactions. It says nothing about which order, and nothing about replicas.
Strict serialisability
Serialisable and linearizable: the equivalent serial order also respects real time. This is what Spanner and CockroachDB sell, and it is the strongest and most expensive thing on offer.
Consensus
A set of nodes agreeing on one value, or on the order of a log of values, despite crashes. Raft and Paxos solve this. It is the mechanism that implements linearizability, not a synonym for it.
Durability
An acknowledged write survives crashes. Orthogonal to all of the above — a system can be linearizable and lose data, if it acknowledges before the log reaches disk on a quorum.

CAP, stated correctly

CAP is narrower and far less interesting than its reputation. Formally: no distributed system can simultaneously guarantee linearizability, availability for every non-failing node, and tolerance of arbitrary network partitions. Since a partition is a fact of the network rather than a design choice, the theorem reduces to a conditional: when a partition occurs, you must choose between returning a possibly-stale answer and returning no answer at all.

What CAP does not say is the part candidates miss. It says nothing about the normal case, which is 99.9% of the system's life. It is not a menu of three from which you pick two — “CA” is not a deployable category, it is the description of a single-node system. It does not say a CP system is unavailable in general, only that it refuses writes on the minority side of a partition. And its C is linearizability of one object — a CP store gives you nothing about multi-object transactions unless it separately implements them.

partition: the link between AZ-a and AZ-b drops ┌──────────────── AZ-a (majority, 2 of 3) ─────────────┐ ╳ ┌── AZ-b (minority) ──┐ │ replica 1 ◀──▶ replica 2 │ ╳ │ replica 3 │ │ ▲ │ ╳ │ ▲ │ └──────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ╳ └──────┼──────────────┘ │ write(x=2) │ read(x) ▼ ▼ CP choice — quorum reachable, commit x=2 CP choice — no quorum minority is refused: error, retry error / timeout invariant preserved; some users see 5xx client blocked AP choice — accept write locally, x=2 AP choice — serve stale x=1 both sides accept, diverge accept write x=9 too heal later via vector clocks / CRDT / LWW conflict at merge time the choice is not global: the seat-reservation call can be CP while the profile-photo read on the same page is AP. Choose per operation.
Fig. 04.1 — a partition forces the choice; nothing above applies when the network is healthy

PACELC — the framing that applies the other 99.9% of the time

Daniel Abadi's extension is the one worth memorising, because it covers the normal case: if Partitioned, choose Availability or Consistency; Else, choose Latency or Consistency. The second clause is where real systems spend their lives. A single-region Raft group is not partitioned today, yet every write still pays a round trip to a majority of replicas before it is acknowledged. That cost is not partition tolerance, it is the standing price of linearizability, and it is paid on every request forever.

Concretely: a three-node Raft group in one region commits in roughly fsync (~1 ms on NVMe) + RTT to the second-fastest peer (~1 ms across AZs), so about 2–3 ms per write. Stretch the same group across us-east-1, us-west-2 and eu-west-1 and the majority now requires the second-nearest peer at ~60 ms RTT — write latency goes from 3 ms to ~65 ms, a twentyfold regression with no code change. That is PACELC's E clause charging rent.

Real systems classified — the E clause is the one that shapes your p99
SystemP: A or CE: L or CWhat that means in practice
PostgreSQL, single primaryPCECNot partition-tolerant by design; failover is a human or a controller, and split-brain is prevented by fencing
Spanner / CockroachDBPCECStrict serialisability bought with Paxos plus a bounded-uncertainty clock; commit waits out clock error (~7 ms in Spanner)
etcd / ZooKeeper / ConsulPCECMinority nodes reject writes; used for metadata and locks, never for throughput
DynamoDB (default reads)PAELEventually-consistent reads are ~half the price and ~half the latency; ConsistentRead=true flips both clauses per call
Cassandra / ScyllaDBPAELTunable per query via consistency level; QUORUM+QUORUM buys back C at the cost of latency and availability
MongoDB replica setPCEC by defaultw:majority plus primary reads is CP; readPreference:secondary silently drops to EL
Redis (async replication)PAELAcknowledges before replicating; a failover after ack loses those writes — correct behaviour, frequently a surprise

The consistency spectrum, and what each operation actually needs
