Between linearizable and eventual there is a ladder of named guarantees, each cheap enough to be worth knowing by name. Most product requirements land in the middle, and the middle is where the money is: session guarantees cost almost nothing and eliminate the majority of user-visible weirdness.

STRONGEST ─ most coordination, highest latency, least available under partition ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Linearizable one global order + real time consensus per op │ │ Sequential one global order, no real time consensus, reads local │ │ Causal happens-before preserved metadata, no consensus│ │ ├─ Read-your-writes you see your own updates sticky routing / token│ │ ├─ Monotonic reads time never runs backwards pin session to replica │ │ └─ Monotonic writes your writes apply in order per-session sequencing │ │ Bounded staleness “at most 5 s or 100 versions” replica lag SLO + fence│ │ Eventual converges if writes stop no guarantee meanwhile │ └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ WEAKEST ─ cheapest, most available, most surprising to users the four session guarantees cost a routing decision, not a round trip. Skipping them is why users refresh a page and watch their own comment vanish.
Fig. 04.2 — the ladder; causal+session is the sweet spot for most product surfaces
Three operations from the same product page, three different answers
OperationGuarantee requiredWhy, and what breaks if weakened
Reserve seat 14CLinearizable + serialisable transactionUniqueness is the product. Two clients reading “free” concurrently and both writing produces a double-booked seat and a refund, an apology and a support ticket. Must be a single-key CAS or a transaction, on the primary.
Increment like countEventual + monotonic readsNobody can verify the true count, so exactness is not a requirement — but a count that reads 402, then 399, then 402 as requests land on different replicas looks broken. Pin the session to one replica; correctness costs nothing.
Change profile photoRead-your-writes for the owner, eventual for everyone elseThe uploader must see the new photo immediately or they will upload it three more times. Other users seeing the old photo for thirty seconds is invisible. Route the owner's reads to the primary for a short window after write.
Bank balance displayBounded staleness (~1 s)Reading a replica is fine; reading a replica that is nine minutes behind because a long-running query blocked apply is a fraud report. Enforce a lag ceiling and fail over to the primary above it.
Debit an accountSerialisable, single shardThe invariant balance >= 0 spans a read and a write. Weakening it permits overdraft under concurrency — see write skew, below.

ACID and BASE are postures, not products

ACID — atomicity, consistency, isolation, durability — is the contract a single-node relational engine has offered since the 1980s, and modern distributed SQL engines reproduce it at real cost. BASE — basically available, soft state, eventually consistent — is not a weaker implementation of the same contract; it is the decision to move correctness out of the database and into the application, where you now own conflict resolution, compensation and reconciliation. That is sometimes right and always more work. Note that most stores are not purely one or the other: DynamoDB has per-item ACID and optional multi-item transactions; Cassandra has lightweight transactions via Paxos at roughly 4× the latency of a normal write. Ask what the store guarantees per operation, never what camp it belongs to.

Isolation levels and the anomalies each permits

Isolation is the single-node half of the problem and it is where most real data corruption originates, because the defaults are weak and silent. Nothing warns you that your transaction just read a value that another transaction is about to change.

SQL isolation levels and what each still allows
LevelDirty readNon-repeatable readPhantomWrite skew / lost update
Read uncommittedpossiblepossiblepossiblepossible
Read committed (Postgres default)preventedpossiblepossiblepossible
Repeatable read (MySQL InnoDB default)preventedpreventedprevented in InnoDB via gap locks; permitted by the standardpossible
Snapshot isolation (Postgres calls it repeatable read)preventedpreventedpreventedpossible — this is the trap
Serializablepreventedpreventedpreventedprevented
Dirty read
Reading a row another transaction has written but not committed. If that transaction rolls back, you acted on a value that never existed.
Non-repeatable read
Reading the same row twice in one transaction and getting different values, because someone committed in between.
Phantom
Re-running the same WHERE predicate and getting a different set of rows, because someone inserted a row matching it.
Write skew
Two transactions each read an overlapping set, each check an invariant that currently holds, and each write a different row. No row conflicts, so no lock catches it — but the combined result violates the invariant.
sql · write skew — both transactions succeed, the invariant dies
-- Invariant: at least one doctor must remain on call for each shift.
-- Alice and Bob are both on call for shift 42; both request leave at the same instant.

-- Session A                                  -- Session B
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;        BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;

SELECT count(*) FROM on_call                  SELECT count(*) FROM on_call
 WHERE shift = 42 AND on_duty = true;          WHERE shift = 42 AND on_duty = true;
-- returns 2  ->  "safe, one will remain"     -- returns 2  ->  "safe, one will remain"

UPDATE on_call SET on_duty = false            UPDATE on_call SET on_duty = false
 WHERE doctor = 'alice' AND shift = 42;        WHERE doctor = 'bob' AND shift = 42;

COMMIT;                                       COMMIT;
-- Both commit. Different rows were written, so no write-write conflict fired.
-- Shift 42 now has ZERO doctors on call. Snapshot isolation permitted this.

-- Three fixes, in order of preference:
-- 1. SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;   -- SSI detects the cycle, aborts one
-- 2. SELECT ... FROM on_call WHERE shift = 42 FOR UPDATE;  -- materialise the read as a lock
-- 3. Move the invariant into a single row the engine can lock:
--    UPDATE shifts SET on_duty_count = on_duty_count - 1
--     WHERE shift = 42 AND on_duty_count > 1;   -- 0 rows affected => reject the request
Production failure — “we set repeatable read, so we're safe”

Postgres's REPEATABLE READ is snapshot isolation, and snapshot isolation is not serialisable. Every transaction reads from a consistent snapshot taken at start, and conflicts are detected only between transactions writing the same row. Any invariant that spans rows — a balance across two accounts, an aggregate limit, a uniqueness rule enforced by a preceding SELECT, an on-call roster — is unprotected. The classic loss is a double-spend on a wallet that has separate debit and credit rows: the balance check passes twice, both writes land, and the account goes negative with no error in any log. Postgres's SERIALIZABLE uses SSI, which detects the dangerous read-write dependency cycle and aborts one transaction with 40001 — so serialisable is only usable if every caller retries on 40001. Ship the retry wrapper first, then raise the level. MySQL's REPEATABLE READ is weaker still: it permits lost updates on read-modify-write without FOR UPDATE.

Optimistic and pessimistic control, and compare-and-swap

Pessimistic control takes a lock before touching data — SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, or an external lock service. It is correct, it serialises access, and its cost is that a lock held across a network call or a slow query converts contention into a queue, and a client that dies holding a lock blocks everyone until a timeout fires. Optimistic control reads a version, does the work unlocked, and writes conditionally on the version being unchanged; if it changed, the write affects zero rows and the caller retries. The decision rule is contention: below roughly 10% conflict probability, OCC wins on throughput and has no deadlocks; above it, retry storms waste more work than locks would, and every retry re-reads the same contended row.

Chosen · Optimistic (version column / CAS)
  • No locks held across application think-time or network calls; a crashed client blocks nobody
  • No deadlocks, no lock-wait timeouts, no lock-manager memory
  • Maps directly onto UPDATE ... WHERE version = ?, DynamoDB conditional writes, ETag/If-Match and Redis WATCH/MULTI — one mechanism at every layer
  • Cost: work is wasted on conflict, so the caller must retry with jitter and a bounded attempt count, and hot keys degrade badly
Rejected · Pessimistic row locks by default
  • Correct, but converts a latency spike into a lock convoy: one slow transaction stalls every waiter behind it
  • Deadlock risk grows with the number of rows touched and requires a consistent lock ordering nobody maintains
  • Still necessary for genuinely hot single rows (seat 14C, the last unit of stock) — use it there, deliberately, and keep the critical section free of I/O
javascript · compare-and-swap with bounded retry — the shape to reuse everywhere
async function reserveSeat(db, seatId, userId, maxAttempts = 5) {
  for (let attempt = 0; attempt < maxAttempts; attempt++) {
    const seat = await db.one(
      'SELECT id, status, version FROM seats WHERE id = $1', [seatId]);

    if (seat.status !== 'free') throw new Error('SEAT_TAKEN');

    // The predicate on `version` is the whole safety argument: the row must be
    // exactly as we read it, or the UPDATE matches zero rows and we lost the race.
    const { rowCount } = await db.query(
      `UPDATE seats SET status = 'held', held_by = $1, version = version + 1
        WHERE id = $2 AND version = $3`,
      [userId, seatId, seat.version]);

    if (rowCount === 1) return { ok: true, attempts: attempt + 1 };

    // Lost the race. Back off with full jitter so retries do not synchronise.
    await sleep(Math.random() * 20 * 2 ** attempt);
  }
  throw new Error('SEAT_CONTENTION');   // surface it; never loop unbounded
}

Consensus: what Raft actually buys, and what it costs

Consensus protocols give you one thing: a replicated log on which a majority of nodes agree, in the same order, surviving the crash of a minority. Everything else — leader election, linearizable reads, distributed locks, cluster membership — is built on that log. Raft structures it as an explicit leader elected for a term: followers that hear no heartbeat within a randomised election timeout (typically 150–300 ms) become candidates, request votes, and a candidate with a majority and a sufficiently up-to-date log becomes leader. Randomised timeouts are what prevent perpetual split votes. FLP tells us no asynchronous consensus protocol can guarantee termination; randomisation makes the pathological case vanishingly unlikely rather than impossible.

RAFT WRITE PATH — every acknowledged write costs one majority round trip client │ set(x=2) ▼ ┌──────────┐ 1. append to local log (uncommitted) │ LEADER │ 2. AppendEntries ──────┬──────────────┐ │ term 7 │◀──── ack ──────────────┐ │ │ └──────────┘ │ ▼ ▼ │ 3. majority (2 of 3) acked ──┘ follower A follower B │ => entry COMMITTED (may lag) ▼ 4. apply to state machine, reply to client client commit latency = fsync + RTT to the second-fastest peer, not the slowest 3 nodes, one region, 3 AZs ...... ~1 ms fsync + ~1 ms RTT = 2–3 ms 5 nodes, us-east + us-west + eu .. majority needs 3rd node = ~65 ms 20x throughput ceiling: ALL writes serialise through one leader = tens of k/s reads: a leader may serve a stale read if it was deposed and does not know it. Linearizable reads need a lease or a no-op log entry — check which your store does.
Fig. 04.3 — consensus is a coordination tax paid on every write, partition or no partition

The practical consequences follow directly from that diagram. Consensus groups do not scale by adding nodes: five nodes tolerate two failures instead of one, but the majority is larger and writes get slower, and seven is almost never right. Throughput scales only by sharding into many independent Raft groups — which is exactly what Spanner, CockroachDB and TiKV do, one group per range. And because every write is a network round trip plus a durable log append, you put small, critical, low-rate state behind consensus — cluster membership, leases, shard assignments, the ledger — and keep high-volume data out of it. A team that puts a 200 000 writes/second event stream behind etcd has misread what the tool is for.

Quorum arithmetic

Leaderless stores replace a leader with counting. With N replicas, a write waits for W acknowledgements and a read waits for R responses. If R + W > N, the read set and the write set must overlap in at least one replica, so a read is guaranteed to observe at least one copy of the latest acknowledged write — and version numbers or timestamps decide which of the returned values is newest.

Choosing R and W against N — the same three replicas, four different systems
NWROverlap?Behaviour and the workload it fits
3224 > 3 yesThe default. Tolerates one replica down for both reads and writes. Balanced latency; the general-purpose choice.
3314 > 3 yesFastest possible reads, zero write availability — any single node down blocks all writes. Read-mostly config data only.
3134 > 3 yesFastest writes, brittle reads. Ingest paths where writes must never block and reads are rare.
3112 > 3 noEventual consistency. Lowest latency, highest availability, reads may be arbitrarily stale. Metrics, feeds, caches.
5336 > 5 yesTolerates two simultaneous failures with overlap intact. Use when a whole AZ loss must not degrade the quorum.
R + W > N is weaker than it looks

Quorum overlap is not linearizability, and treating it as such causes real bugs. Four leaks: (1) a sloppy quorum — Dynamo-style hinted handoff — counts acknowledgements from replacement nodes outside the key's preference list, so the overlap guarantee is void exactly when you needed it, during a failure. (2) A write that reaches only W-1 replicas returns an error to the client but is not rolled back, so a later read may return it — a failed write that becomes visible. (3) Two concurrent writes that both reach a quorum leave the store to break the tie, and if that tie-break is last-write-wins, one client's data is silently discarded. (4) Nothing here gives monotonic reads: consecutive quorum reads can hit different replica subsets and go backwards in time. If you need linearizability, use a consensus-backed store, not arithmetic.

Resolving conflicts: LWW, vector clocks, CRDTs

Once you allow two replicas to accept writes concurrently, something has to reconcile them, and the choice is a data-loss policy whether or not you write it down.

Last-write-wins

Compare timestamps, keep the newest, drop the rest. Trivial to implement and O(1) metadata, which is why Cassandra uses it per column. It is data loss by design: two concurrent updates, one vanishes with no error. Worse, it depends on wall clocks — 100 ms of NTP skew means a node whose clock runs slow has its writes silently discarded even when they genuinely came later.

Vector clocks

Each replica keeps a per-node counter; comparing two vectors tells you whether one causally descends from the other or whether they are concurrent. Concurrent versions are returned to the client as siblings to merge. No data is lost, but metadata grows with the number of writers and someone must write real merge logic — the reason Riak's siblings were widely disliked.

CRDTs

Data types whose merge is commutative, associative and idempotent, so replicas converge regardless of order: G/PN-counters, OR-sets, LWW-registers, sequence CRDTs behind Yjs and Automerge. Convergence is automatic and provable. The cost is tombstones and metadata growth, and the hard limit is that a CRDT cannot express a global invariant — no CRDT set enforces “at most 100 members”, because that needs coordination.

javascript · vector-clock comparison and the LWW loss it exposes
// Cart A: user added a scarf on replica R1.  Cart B: removed gloves on R2.
const A = { clock: { R1: 2, R2: 1 }, items: ['hat', 'scarf'] };
const B = { clock: { R1: 1, R2: 2 }, items: ['hat', 'gloves'] };

function compare(a, b) {
  const nodes = new Set([...Object.keys(a), ...Object.keys(b)]);
  let aAhead = false, bAhead = false;
  for (const n of nodes) {
    const x = a[n] || 0, y = b[n] || 0;
    if (x > y) aAhead = true;
    if (y > x) bAhead = true;
  }
  if (aAhead && bAhead) return 'concurrent';   // neither descends from the other
  return aAhead ? 'a-newer' : bAhead ? 'b-newer' : 'equal';
}

compare(A.clock, B.clock);   // => 'concurrent'

// LWW would keep whichever wall-clock timestamp is larger and DISCARD the other
// cart entirely: the scarf or the glove-removal disappears, silently, no error.
// A CRDT OR-Set converges instead:  add(scarf) ∪ add(hat) minus remove(gloves)
// => ['hat', 'scarf'] on both replicas, with no merge code in the application.

Idempotency: the practical substitute for exactly-once

Exactly-once delivery across a network is impossible — the sender cannot distinguish a lost request from a lost response, so it must either retry (at-least-once) or not (at-most-once). What is achievable is exactly-once effect: at-least-once delivery plus an idempotent handler. This is not a consolation prize; it is how every payment system on earth works, and it dissolves an entire class of consistency problems without any coordination.

The mechanism is a client-generated key, a uniqueness constraint, and a stored response. The client generates a UUID per logical operation and sends it on every retry of that operation. The server inserts the key inside the same transaction as the effect, so the database's uniqueness constraint — not application logic — makes duplication impossible. On a duplicate the server replays the stored response with the original status code. The subtlety that catches teams out is the in-flight case: two retries racing means the second must wait or return 409, never proceed.

sql · idempotency key and effect committed atomically
CREATE TABLE idempotency_keys (
  key            uuid PRIMARY KEY,          -- client-generated, one per logical op
  request_hash   bytea       NOT NULL,      -- reject key reuse with a different body
  state          text        NOT NULL,      -- 'in_flight' | 'done'
  response_code  int,
  response_body  jsonb,
  created_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  expires_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now() + interval '24 hours'
);

BEGIN;
  -- The constraint, not the application, is what makes this safe.
  INSERT INTO idempotency_keys (key, request_hash, state)
  VALUES ($1, $2, 'in_flight')
  ON CONFLICT (key) DO NOTHING;
  -- 0 rows => duplicate. If state='done', replay the stored response;
  -- if 'in_flight', return 409 and let the client retry. Never re-execute.

  INSERT INTO payments (id, account_id, amount_cents) VALUES ($3, $4, $5);
  UPDATE accounts SET balance_cents = balance_cents - $5
   WHERE id = $4 AND balance_cents >= $5;      -- 0 rows => insufficient funds, ROLLBACK

  UPDATE idempotency_keys
     SET state = 'done', response_code = 201, response_body = $6 WHERE key = $1;
COMMIT;   -- effect and key commit together, or neither does
Idempotency is not only for HTTP

The same three ingredients work at every layer. A consumer processing a Kafka partition writes (topic, partition, offset) into the same transaction as its output, and skips anything it has already recorded. A distributed lock is only safe with a monotonically increasing fencing token issued at acquisition and validated by the storage layer, so a paused lock-holder that wakes after its lease expired has its write rejected. And any operation you can express as “set to X” rather than “add 1” is idempotent for free — prefer absolute assignments over deltas whenever the domain allows it.

What a strong candidate says out loud

“I won't pick one consistency level for the system — I'll go operation by operation. Seat reservation is the only linearizable path here: it's a uniqueness invariant, so it's a conditional update on a single row in the primary, and I'd rather take the write-availability hit under a partition than double-book. Everything else drops down the ladder. The like counter is eventual, but I'll pin the session to a replica so the number never goes backwards — that's monotonic reads and it costs a routing decision, not a round trip. The profile photo needs read-your-writes for the owner only, so I route their reads to the primary for about ten seconds after an upload. Then I'd say the quiet part: PACELC's E clause is what actually shapes my p99. There's no partition today and a cross-region Raft group still costs sixty-five milliseconds per write, so the consensus group stays single-region and holds only metadata. And I'll make every write path idempotent with a client-supplied key and a unique constraint, because at-least-once delivery plus idempotent handlers is the only exactly-once that exists.” The move that scores is refusing the global answer and naming the cost of each local one.

CARRY FORWARD

Every case study in Part II states, per operation, which rung of Fig. 04.2 it stands on and what the weaker rung would cost the user. Where a design accepts concurrent writes, it names the conflict-resolution policy explicitly — LWW, siblings or a CRDT — because “we'll sort it out later” resolves to silent last-write-wins in production. The chapter on sharding and replication covers how replicas get the data in the first place; the chapter on asynchronous messaging covers delivery semantics and the idempotent consumers that make them survivable.

05

Replication & partitioning

Every stateful system scales by making copies and by making cuts. Replication buys read throughput and survivability, and charges you in staleness and conflicts; partitioning buys write throughput and capacity, and charges you in cross-shard operations. The two are orthogonal, they are always used together, and nearly every hard bug in distributed data lives in the seam between them.

The two mechanisms, kept apart

Say the sentence out loud before designing anything: replication answers “how many copies of this row exist and which one may I read”; partitioning answers “which machine owns this row at all”. Teams that blur the two add replicas to relieve a write bottleneck and are baffled when nothing improves — every replica takes every write, so replication multiplies write load rather than dividing it. Only partitioning divides writes.

They compose multiplicatively. A cluster of 64 shards at replication factor 3 is 192 data-bearing processes, of which 64 are leaders. Sizing follows from the hottest shard, not the average — a point we will spend most of this chapter defending.

Single-leader write ceiling
~5–10k/sCommodity NVMe, WAL fsync, 1 KB rows. Beyond this you shard, not replicate.
Intra-AZ replication lag
1–10 msThe window in which read-your-writes breaks on a follower read.
Cross-region RTT
60–90 msus-east-1 to eu-west-1. The standing cost of a synchronous remote replica.
Practical shard size
200 GB–1 TBSmall enough to rebuild or move in under an hour at 1 Gbit/s.

Three replication topologies, three conflict stories

There are only three shapes. Learn each by the conflict it can produce, because that is the only thing that distinguishes them operationally.

SINGLE-LEADER MULTI-LEADER LEADERLESS (n=3, w=2, r=2) w ─▶ LEADER ─┬─▶ follower A w ─▶ LEADER eu ◀── async ──▶ LEADER us ◀─ w w ─▶ N1 N2 N3 │ └─▶ follower B │ │ ack ◀── 2 of 3 └── r linearizable └── both accept the same row ──┘ r ─▶ N1 N2 N3 r on follower = stale CONFLICT: two versions of one key 2 of 3 respond conflict story: conflict story: conflict story: none — one writer serialises lost update unless you merge concurrent writes = siblings failure story: resolution: resolution: failover, split brain LWW / CRDT / app merge version vectors, read repair chained / cascading replication — a follower feeding followers; same rules, more lag
Fig. 5.1 — the only three replication topologies, and the conflict each one owns
When each topology is the right answer
TopologyUse whenConflictsReal examples
Single-leaderDefault. One region owns the write path; you need read scaling and simple semantics.Impossible by construction — the leader imposes a total order.PostgreSQL, MySQL, MongoDB replica sets, Kafka partitions
Multi-leaderWriters are genuinely in several regions and must survive a region loss with local write latency; or offline-capable clients.Guaranteed. You must supply a merge function.Active-active MySQL/Postgres (BDR), CouchDB, Cosmos DB multi-write, calendars and note apps
LeaderlessWrite availability matters more than write ordering; the workload is last-writer-wins-safe or CRDT-shaped.Siblings from concurrent writes; resolved by version vectors, LWW or read repair.Cassandra, DynamoDB internals, Riak, ScyllaDB

Choose single-leader unless you can name the conflict merge function. That is the whole test. “We will use active-active for resilience” without an answer for two users renaming the same document in two regions is a plan to silently lose one of the renames. Multi-leader with last-write-wins on wall-clock timestamps is defined data loss with clock skew as the trigger; it is acceptable for a cache or a presence flag and unacceptable for a balance or an inventory count.

Leaderless systems make the durability arithmetic explicit through quorums: with n replicas, if w + r > n the read set and the write set must overlap in at least one node, so a read sees the latest acknowledged write. With n = 3, w = 2, r = 2 you tolerate one node down on either path. Note the caveats that interviewers probe: the overlap guarantee is broken by sloppy quorums (hinted handoff writing to nodes outside the preference list), by concurrent writes to the same key, and by a write that succeeds on fewer than w nodes and is never rolled back.

Synchronous, asynchronous, and the honest middle

Synchronous replication means the leader does not acknowledge until a replica has the write durably. Asynchronous means it acknowledges immediately and ships the log afterwards. The trade is stark and quantifiable.

Chosen · Semi-synchronous (1 sync + N async)
  • One designated standby must ack; the rest stream asynchronously.
  • Commit latency rises by one intra-AZ RTT: roughly +1–2 ms.
  • Zero committed-write loss on a clean failover to the sync standby.
  • Cost: if the sync standby dies, writes stall until the system demotes it to async — you must configure that timeout, or an unnoticed standby failure becomes a full write outage.
Rejected · Fully synchronous to all replicas
  • Commit latency becomes the slowest replica's latency — a tail, not an average.
  • Availability falls as replicas rise: any one replica down blocks all writes.
  • Across regions it adds 60–90 ms to every write, permanently.
  • Only correct for tiny, high-value state: config, leases, coordination metadata.

Fully asynchronous is the other extreme and is the right default for read replicas, analytics copies and cross-region disaster recovery — but understand exactly what you have bought: a data loss window equal to the lag at the moment the leader dies. At 30k writes/s with 200 ms of lag, an unplanned failover discards up to 6,000 acknowledged writes. If those were payments, you have a reconciliation incident, not a database incident.

Replication lag and its three anomalies

Lag is not a bug to be eliminated; it is a property to be bounded and routed around. Asynchronous replication produces exactly three user-visible anomalies, and each has a specific, cheap mitigation.

The three lag anomalies
AnomalyWhat the user seesMitigationCost
Read-your-writes“I updated my profile photo and the page still shows the old one.”Route a session's reads to the leader for t seconds after its last write, or pin reads to a replica that has caught up to the write's log position (LSN / GTID / readConcern afterClusterTime).Leader read load spikes after write bursts; needs sticky session state.
Monotonic reads“I refreshed and the comment I just saw vanished.” Two reads hit replicas with different lag.Pin a session to one replica by hashing the user ID, not round-robin. Time moves forward per session even if that replica is behind.Uneven replica load; must re-pin on replica failure, which reintroduces the anomaly once.
Consistent prefixThe answer appears before the question. Ordering violated across partitions.Keep causally related writes in one partition (same conversation key), or carry causal metadata / Lamport timestamps and delay application.Constrains the partition key; metadata on every write.

Note that consistent-prefix violations are a partitioning bug wearing a replication costume: within one single-leader partition, order is total and the anomaly cannot occur. It appears only when a logical stream is split across independently replicated shards. This is the seam referred to in the lead, and it is why “which key do these two writes share?” is a consistency question, not just a capacity one.

javascript · read-your-writes without pinning every read to the leader
// After any write, the leader returns its log position. Carry it in the session
// (a cookie, or a token the client echoes). Reads then demand a replica that
// has applied at least that position; otherwise fall back to the leader.

async function writeProfile(session, userId, patch) {
  const { lsn } = await leader.query(
    'UPDATE profiles SET bio = $2 WHERE id = $1 RETURNING pg_current_wal_lsn() AS lsn',
    [userId, patch.bio]
  );
  session.minLsn = lsn;                 // monotonic high-water mark for this user
  session.minLsnAt = Date.now();
  return { ok: true };
}

async function readProfile(session, userId) {
  const fresh = replicas.filter(r => r.appliedLsn >= (session.minLsn ?? 0));
  // Stable choice, not round-robin: also gives us monotonic reads for free.
  const target = fresh.length
    ? fresh[hash(session.id) % fresh.length]
    : leader;                           // no replica caught up yet -> pay the leader
  return target.query('SELECT * FROM profiles WHERE id = $1', [userId]);
}

// appliedLsn is refreshed every ~200 ms by a background poller per replica.
// Staleness of that poll is safe in one direction only: it may send a read to
// the leader unnecessarily. It must never round *up*, or the guarantee is fiction.

Failover, split brain and fencing tokens

Failover is where replication stops being a throughput feature and becomes a correctness problem. The sequence is: detect that the leader is gone, elect a new one, redirect writes. Every step has a failure mode.

  1. Detection is a guess. You cannot distinguish a dead leader from a slow one. A timeout that is too short causes needless failovers under load; too long extends the outage. Typical settings land at 10–30 s, and the number is a direct trade between availability and churn.
  2. Election must be by majority. A new leader chosen by a minority is a second leader. Use Raft/Paxos or an external majority-quorum service; never a single monitoring host's opinion.
  3. Unreplicated writes must be discarded or reconciled. The old leader may hold writes the new one never saw. Standard practice is to throw them away on rejoin — which is precisely the data loss window above. Say so explicitly in a design review.
  4. The old leader must be fenced. A leader that was merely paused will wake up believing it is still in charge and resume writing.
┌──────────── partition / 12 s GC pause ────────────┐ │ │ clients ──▶ OLD LEADER (token 33, still writing) NEW LEADER (token 34) │ │ └──▶ STORAGE / LOG ◀────────────────────────────────┘ │ ├─ highest token seen = 34 ├─ write(token 34) ▶ accepted └─ write(token 33) ▶ REJECTED — stale fencing token The lock/lease service issues a monotonically increasing token with every grant. Resources reject anything below the highest token they have observed. Without fencing, both leaders write and the split is only discovered by corruption.
Fig. 5.2 — split brain is not prevented by leases alone; it is prevented at the resource
Production failure mode · automated failover across a WAN

GitHub's October 2018 outage began with a 43-second loss of connectivity between two data centres. An automated topology manager promoted a leader on the far side; writes landed on both coasts for those 43 seconds, and the two MySQL clusters could not be automatically reconciled. Restoring consistency took over 24 hours of degraded service. The lesson is not “avoid automation” — it is that automated failover across a link whose failures are common and brief is usually wrong. Within an AZ or region, automate it. Across regions, require a human or a genuine majority quorum, and always make failover idempotent and fenced so a false positive costs an outage rather than a divergence.

Partitioning: four schemes, and the one you will actually use

Partitioning splits the keyspace so each shard owns a disjoint slice. The scheme determines which queries stay cheap and which become fan-outs.

Partitioning schemes compared
SchemeMechanismGood atBad atSeen in
Key rangeSorted keyspace cut into contiguous intervals; each shard owns [lo, hi).Range scans, time-window queries, ordered iteration, cheap splits.Hotspots — sequential keys concentrate all writes on the last shard.HBase, Bigtable, CockroachDB, TiKV, FoundationDB
HashShard = hash(key) mod N, or a position on a hash ring.Even distribution with almost no thought; trivial point lookups.No range scans; ordering destroyed; resharding under mod N is catastrophic.Cassandra, DynamoDB, Redis Cluster (16,384 slots), Vitess
Directory / lookupAn explicit table maps key or tenant to shard, consulted on every request.Arbitrary placement: move one noisy tenant, pin a region, split by hand.Extra hop; the directory is a hot dependency and a single point of failure if not replicated and cached.Vitess vindexes, Slack & Salesforce style tenant maps, most sharded monoliths
Entity / tenantA subtree of related rows shares the partition key (all rows for tenant_id).Single-shard transactions and joins for the common case; clean blast radius.Tenant size variance — one customer 1000× the median breaks the model.Salesforce, Shopify pods, DynamoDB item collections, Spanner interleaved tables

In practice most designs are hash on a well-chosen entity key — which quietly delivers the entity scheme's locality and the hash scheme's evenness at once, because everything for one entity hashes to one shard. Reach for range partitioning only when range scans are a first-class access pattern (time-series, log storage, anything with a cursor), and accept that you have signed up for hotspot management.

Choosing the partition key: cardinality, uniformity, alignment

A partition key is judged on three properties, in this order:

Cardinality
Distinct values must vastly exceed shard count — target at least 100× so that load averages out. country_code has ~200 values and is a poor key for 64 shards; user_id has millions and is a good one. Booleans and status enums are never partition keys.
Uniformity
Access must be roughly even across those values. High cardinality does not save you if 1% of keys take 90% of traffic — the celebrity problem below.
Query alignment
The key must appear in the WHERE clause of your dominant read path. A perfectly uniform key that no query filters on turns every read into a scatter-gather across all shards, which is the most expensive way to run a database.

The two hotspot archetypes

The celebrity key. One partition value receives orders of magnitude more traffic than the rest: a pop star's follower list, a flash-sale SKU, the system tenant. DynamoDB makes the ceiling explicit — a single physical partition caps at roughly 3,000 read units and 1,000 write units per second regardless of how much capacity the table has provisioned. You will be throttled at 1% utilisation while the table looks idle in the dashboard.

The monotonic key. Partitioning by an auto-increment ID, a UUIDv1/v7 timestamp prefix or a raw created_at under a range scheme sends 100% of writes to the last shard, while every other shard idles. This is the single most common sharding mistake in production, because it passes every test on an empty dataset.

sql · fixing both archetypes with the same tool: a compound key
-- BAD: range-partitioned on a monotonic prefix. All writes hit the newest shard.
--   PRIMARY KEY (created_at, event_id)

-- FIX 1 - salting. Prepend a bucket derived from a *uniform* field. Writes spread
-- across 16 shards; a time-range query becomes 16 parallel scans, merged.
CREATE TABLE events (
  bucket      smallint NOT NULL,            -- abs(hashtext(device_id)) % 16
  created_at  timestamptz NOT NULL,
  event_id    uuid NOT NULL,
  payload     jsonb,
  PRIMARY KEY (bucket, created_at, event_id)
);

-- FIX 2 - field promotion. If queries always name a device, lead with it.
-- Zero fan-out, no salt to remember, and the range scan survives intact.
--   PRIMARY KEY (device_id, created_at)

-- FIX 3 - celebrity write sharding. Split one hot counter into k sub-keys,
-- write to a random one, read the sum. k=100 turns 1 hot row into 100 warm ones.
INSERT INTO follower_counts (user_id, shard, delta)
VALUES ($1, floor(random() * 100)::int, 1)
ON CONFLICT (user_id, shard) DO UPDATE SET delta = follower_counts.delta + 1;

SELECT sum(delta) FROM follower_counts WHERE user_id = $1;   -- 100-row aggregate

-- The cost is stated plainly: reads got 100x more expensive so writes could be
-- 100x more parallel. Only correct where writes dominate, or reads are cached.
Interview · what a strong candidate says about the partition key

“I'll shard by user_id, hashed, into a fixed 1,024 logical partitions mapped onto physical nodes. That gives me millions of distinct values, co-locates everything a user reads in one place so the hot path is a single-shard query, and lets me grow from 8 nodes to 80 by moving whole partitions rather than rehashing keys. The known weakness is celebrity accounts, so before launch I'd instrument per-partition QPS and bytes, alert when any partition exceeds 3× the median, and keep two remedies ready: write-sharding the hot counters, and a directory override that pins a single oversized tenant to dedicated hardware. The thing I explicitly give up is efficient global queries like ‘all orders in the last hour across all users' — those go to a separate time-partitioned store fed from the change log, not to the OLTP shards.” Naming the workload you cannot serve, and where you will serve it instead, is what separates a design from a diagram.

Consistent hashing, done properly

Naïve hash(key) mod N is fine until N changes. Going from 10 nodes to 11 changes the modulus for essentially every key: about 10/11 ≈ 91% of the dataset must move. Consistent hashing exists to make that number 1/N instead.

Map both keys and nodes onto the same circular hash space (typically 2^32 or 2^64 points). A key is owned by the first node encountered walking clockwise from the key's position. Adding a node captures only the arc between it and its predecessor — so only the keys in that arc move, and only from one node. Removing a node hands its arc to its clockwise successor. Nothing else in the ring is disturbed.

hash space 0 ─────────────────────────────────────────────▶ 2^32 (wraps) ring: ┌───▶ B#7 ──▶ k1 k2 ──▶ C#3 ──▶ k3 ──▶ A#1 ──▶ k4 k5 k6 ──▶ B#2 ──┐ └────────────────────────────── (wrap) ─────────────────────────────┘ rule: key belongs to the FIRST node token clockwise of hash(key) add node D with tokens D#0..D#255 scattered around the ring: ... C#3 ──▶ k3 ──▶ D#88 ──▶ A#1 ... only k3 moves: C ─▶ D k1 k2 k4 k5 k6 untouched WITHOUT virtual nodes (1 token per node) ▶ arc sizes vary wildly; one node gets 3x load ▶ a departing node dumps its entire range on ONE peer WITH 128-256 vnodes per node ▶ load spread stdev ~ 1/sqrt(V) ≈ 6-9% ▶ a departing node's data is refilled BY ALL peers, in parallel
Fig. 5.3 — virtual nodes are what make consistent hashing usable, not an optimisation

Virtual nodes solve two distinct problems and it is worth separating them. First, balance: with one token per node, arc lengths are random and variance is enormous; with V tokens per node, relative load deviation falls roughly as 1/√V, so V = 256 gives single-digit percentage skew. Second, and more important operationally, parallel recovery: when a node dies, its 256 arcs are scattered across all surviving peers, so all of them stream data to the replacement simultaneously. With one token, a single unlucky peer must serve the entire rebuild alone, at the exact moment the cluster is already degraded. Vnodes also let you weight heterogeneous hardware — give a machine with twice the disk twice the tokens.

javascript · a consistent hash ring with virtual nodes and a preference list
const hash32 = (s) => {                    // FNV-1a: fast, adequate for placement
  let h = 0x811c9dc5;
  for (let i = 0; i < s.length; i++) {
    h ^= s.charCodeAt(i);
    h = Math.imul(h, 0x01000193) >>> 0;
  }
  return h >>> 0;
};

class Ring {
  constructor(nodes, vnodes = 256) {
    this.v = vnodes;
    this.tokens = [];                      // sorted array of [position, node]
    nodes.forEach((n) => this.add(n, false));
    this.sort();
  }
  add(node, sort = true) {
    for (let i = 0; i < this.v; i++) this.tokens.push([hash32(node + '#' + i), node]);
    if (sort) this.sort();
  }
  remove(node) { this.tokens = this.tokens.filter(([, n]) => n !== node); }
  sort() { this.tokens.sort((a, b) => a[0] - b[0]); }

  indexOf(key) {                           // first token clockwise, wrapping to 0
    const h = hash32(key);
    let lo = 0, hi = this.tokens.length - 1, ans = 0;
    while (lo <= hi) {
      const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1;
      if (this.tokens[mid][0] >= h) { ans = mid; hi = mid - 1; } else { lo = mid + 1; }
    }
    return ans;
  }

  // Replicas = the next R DISTINCT physical nodes clockwise. Skipping duplicates
  // matters: without it, 3 vnodes of the same box counts as 3 replicas and you
  // lose all copies when that one machine dies.
  preferenceList(key, r = 3) {
    const out = [];
    let i = this.indexOf(key);
    for (let step = 0; step < this.tokens.length && out.length < r; step++) {
      const node = this.tokens[(i + step) % this.tokens.length][1];
      if (!out.includes(node)) out.push(node);
    }
    return out;                            // [primary, replica2, replica3]
  }
}

Rebalancing: why a fixed partition count wins

Whatever the placement function, you eventually add or lose machines and data must move. There are three strategies and they are not equally good.

Fixed partition count (recommended)

Create far more logical partitions than nodes at cluster creation — 1,024 partitions for 10 nodes, or Redis Cluster's 16,384 hash slots. Rebalancing moves whole partitions between nodes; the key-to-partition mapping never changes. Simple, predictable, and reversible.

Dynamic partitioning

Partitions split when they exceed a size threshold and merge when they shrink (HBase regions, Bigtable tablets, CockroachDB ranges at 512 MB). Adapts to skew automatically and to any dataset size — at the cost of split storms under bulk load, and an empty database that starts life as a single partition on a single node.

Proportional to nodes

A fixed number of partitions per node (Cassandra's vnodes). Partition count grows with the cluster, keeping sizes stable — but a node joining must split existing ranges, and the mapping is only sensible with random hashing.

Pick the fixed count unless your dataset spans several orders of magnitude in size. The reasoning is operational: with a fixed count, “rebalance” is a well-defined, resumable, throttleable list of partition moves you can pause halfway through at 3 a.m. Choose the number so that partitions are 200 GB–1 TB at the largest cluster you can imagine, and remember it is expensive to change later — 1,024 or 4,096 are sane defaults; 16 is a decision you will regret within two years.

Production failure mode · automatic rebalancing plus a flaky node

Never make rebalancing fully automatic. The classic cascade: a node's latency rises under GC pressure, the coordinator declares it dead and starts moving its partitions elsewhere, the resulting rebuild traffic saturates the network, more nodes appear slow, more rebalances start — and a single degraded machine becomes a cluster-wide outage. Rebalancing must be rate-limited (cap concurrent moves and bytes/second), must be gated on a human confirmation for node removal, and must never fire on a single missed heartbeat. Detecting failure and reacting to failure are separate decisions and should be separately controlled.

Routing: who knows where the data lives

Something must map key to node, and keep that map current during rebalancing. There are exactly three places to put that knowledge.

(1) CLIENT-SIDE (2) ROUTING TIER (3) COORDINATION SERVICE client client client │ knows the map │ │ asks once, caches ▼ ▼ ▼ NODE PROXY / ROUTER ZooKeeper / etcd │ (stateless, N of them) │ authoritative map ▼ ▼ NODE NODE 1 hop, lowest latency 2 hops, +0.3-1 ms 1 hop after cache warm every client must be upgraded polyglot clients for free hard dependency; must be HA Cassandra, Redis Cluster Vitess vtgate, mongos, Envoy HBase+ZK, Kafka metadata ▶ all three need the SAME invariant: a stale map must FAIL, not misroute silently. Nodes reject requests for partitions they no longer own; the client refreshes and retries. Redis Cluster does this with MOVED / ASK redirects. Silent forwarding hides the bug.
Fig. 5.4 — three routing topologies; the redirect-on-stale-map invariant is not optional

Default to a routing tier for anything with more than one client language or more than a handful of client teams: it decouples map changes from client deploys, gives you a place to put connection pooling and query rewriting, and costs sub-millisecond latency. Choose client-side routing only when you control every client and latency is genuinely critical. In all cases, the map lives in a consensus store; the disagreement is only about who reads it.

Secondary indexes in a partitioned world

Partitioning is defined on the primary key. Any query that filters on something else must either search everywhere or maintain a second structure — and there is no third option.

Local vs global secondary indexes
Local (document-partitioned)Global (term-partitioned)
Index livesOn the same shard as the row it indexesPartitioned by the indexed term, on other shards
Write pathOne shard, atomic with the row, cheapRow on shard A, index entry on shard B — a distributed write
Read pathScatter-gather across all shards, merge resultsSingle shard lookup by term
ConsistencyTrivially consistent with the dataUsually asynchronous, therefore eventually consistent
ExamplesDynamoDB LSI, Elasticsearch (each shard indexes its own docs), MongoDB indexesDynamoDB GSI, Riak search, a purpose-built inverted index service
Right whenFan-out is bounded (tens of shards), or the query already names the partition keyReads dominate, low latency required, and the index term is high-cardinality

The decisive number is fan-out tail latency. With 64 shards each having a p99 of 20 ms, the probability that all respond quickly is 0.99^64 ≈ 0.52 — so roughly half of your scatter-gather queries wait on a shard's p99, and the query's median latency approaches the shard's tail. That is the tail-at-scale effect, and it is why local indexes stop working around a few dozen shards unless you add hedged requests or accept partial results. Global indexes trade that for a write-time fan-out and eventual consistency: a GSI update lands milliseconds to seconds after the base row, so “write then immediately query the index” returns nothing. Design around it rather than discovering it.

Cross-shard queries, joins and transactions

Everything above is easy compared with an operation spanning shards. A cross-shard transaction requires two-phase commit: a coordinator writes a prepare record, every participant durably promises, then the coordinator writes the commit decision and everyone applies it. That is two round trips plus two fsyncs per participant, and — critically — if the coordinator dies between prepare and commit, participants hold their locks indefinitely. In-doubt transactions block other work on those rows until an operator intervenes. Throughput for 2PC is typically an order of magnitude below single-shard commits.

So the answer is almost always to not need it:

  • Co-locate by entity. Choose the partition key so that transactional units live together — all rows for an order, all rows for a tenant. Spanner's interleaved tables and DynamoDB's item collections exist precisely for this. This converts most would-be distributed transactions into local ones and is by far the highest-leverage decision in the design.
  • Denormalise the join away. Store the seller's name on the order row. The join disappears; you accept a fan-out update when a seller renames, which is rare, and drive it from the change log.
  • Use a saga for genuinely multi-entity workflows. A sequence of local transactions, each with a compensating action, coordinated by an orchestrator. You give up isolation — intermediate states are visible — and gain the ability to run at all. Money movement, order fulfilment and booking flows are all sagas in practice.
  • Push analytics elsewhere. “Top 100 sellers this month” is not an OLTP query. Stream changes via CDC into a columnar store and answer it there. Every attempt to serve analytical fan-out from sharded OLTP ends with a scatter-gather that takes the cluster down at month end.
  • Keep 2PC for the residue. When you truly need atomicity across shards — and some systems do — use a database that implements it properly (Spanner, CockroachDB, YugabyteDB) rather than hand-rolling a coordinator. Budget for the latency and make the coordinator's log as available as the data.
The decision procedure

Work in this order and the design falls out. One: can a single leader take the write volume? If yes, use single-leader replication and stop — you have bought simplicity and total ordering for free. Two: if not, choose the partition key from the dominant read path, then verify it has the cardinality and uniformity to be even; if those conflict, the read path wins and you fix skew with salting or write-sharding. Three: pick a fixed logical partition count sized for the largest cluster you can imagine, and route through a tier that fails loudly on a stale map. Four: per shard, choose synchronous or asynchronous replication by naming the data loss window you will accept in seconds and in rows. Five: list the queries the partition key cannot serve, and say now where they will be served instead. The candidate who does step five unprompted is the one who has run a sharded system, not just read about one.

06

Caching at scale

A cache is a bet that the recent past predicts the near future, paid for in staleness and in a new class of failure that only appears when the cache is empty or wrong. Caching is easy to add and hard to remove, so every layer you add must be justified by an origin you are actually protecting and a staleness budget you have actually written down.

Where caches live, and what belongs at each layer

There is never one cache. A request that returns a product page may pass through five of them, each with a different owner, a different eviction rule and a different blast radius when it goes stale. Draw the stack before you tune anything, because the cheapest cache hit is always the one furthest from your servers.

CLIENT EDGE SERVICE ORIGIN ┌─────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐ │ browser │───▶│ CDN / PoP │───▶│ L1 in-process │───▶│ PostgreSQL │ │ HTTP │ │ 300+ edges │ │ Caffeine / W-TinyLFU│ │ primary + 2 repl │ └─────────┘ └──────────────┘ └─────────┬─────────┘ └──────────────────┘ 0 ms 5–30 ms │ L1 miss 1–20 ms, ~8k QPS/replica private shared, anonymous ▼ ▲ per-user ~95% of bytes ┌───────────────────┐ │ L2 miss revalidate never personalised │ L2 Redis cluster │──────────┘ with ETag │ 6 shards, RF=2 │ └───────────────────┘ 0.3–1 ms same-AZ · 1.5 ms cross-AZ hit ratio compounds: 0.95 edge × 0.30 L1 × 0.90 L2 ▶ origin sees 0.35% of edge traffic
Fig. 1 — the layer stack, with the latency and the fan-out reduction each layer buys
What belongs where — and what must not
LayerStoreTypical TTLPut hereNever here
BrowserHTTP cacheImmutable assets: 1 year. HTML: 0 with ETag.Fingerprinted JS/CSS/images, user avatarsAnything you may need to retract — you cannot purge a browser
CDN / edgeVarnish, Fastly, CloudFrontSeconds to hours, with stale-while-revalidateAnonymous HTML, API responses keyed only by public inputs, mediaAuthenticated responses, Set-Cookie, anything varying on a header you forgot to declare
L1 in-processCaffeine, Guava, a bounded Map1–30 sConfig, feature flags, hot keys, decoded JWKS, compiled templatesLarge objects (they hit GC), anything needing prompt invalidation across the fleet
L2 distributedRedis, Memcached1 min – 24 hQuery results, rendered fragments, session state, rate-limit countersYour only copy of anything — a cache is by definition allowed to lose data
DatabaseBuffer pool, materialised viewsManaged by the engineHot pages, precomputed aggregatesNothing — but do not count it as capacity; a cold restart drops it

The rule that keeps this sane: each layer must have a distinct reason to exist. L1 exists to survive hot keys and to remove network round-trips from the p99. L2 exists to protect the origin and to share work across the fleet. The CDN exists to remove distance. If you cannot name which of those a layer provides, delete it — every cache is a permanent source of "why is this user seeing old data" tickets.

The five patterns and what each one costs

Patterns differ in who writes to the cache and when. That single choice determines your consistency story.

Caching patterns, ranked by how often you should reach for them
PatternRead pathWrite pathConsistency implicationReach for it when
Cache-aside (lazy)App checks cache, on miss loads origin and populatesApp writes origin, then invalidates the keyBounded staleness = TTL. Vulnerable to the read-modify race below.Default. 90% of systems should stop here.
Read-throughCache library loads on missSame as cache-asideIdentical to cache-aside; the difference is code hygiene, not semanticsYou want one loader definition instead of a miss-handling branch at every call site
Write-throughCache is always populatedWrite cache and origin synchronouslyCache never stale, but write latency = both hops and the write can half-failRead-heavy keys written rarely and read immediately after write
Write-back (write-behind)Cache is authoritativeWrite cache, flush to origin asynchronously in batchesDurability hole: a cache node loss loses acknowledged writesCounters, view tallies, telemetry — data whose loss is an accounting error, not a correctness one
Refresh-aheadServe current value; recompute before expiryUnchangedNever serves a cold miss on a hot key, but wastes work on keys that go coldA small, known set of expensive hot keys — homepage, leaderboards, pricing tables
javascript · cache-aside with single-flight, negative caching and TTL jitter
const NULL_SENTINEL = 'nil';
const inflight = new Map();               // single-flight registry, per process

async function get(key, loader, { ttl = 300, negTtl = 30 } = {}) {
  const hit = await redis.get(key);
  if (hit !== null) {
    return hit === NULL_SENTINEL ? null : JSON.parse(hit);   // negative-cache hit
  }
  if (inflight.has(key)) return inflight.get(key);           // collapse concurrent misses

  const p = (async () => {
    try {
      const row = await loader(key);                          // the only origin call
      if (row === null) {
        await redis.set(key, NULL_SENTINEL, 'EX', negTtl);    // penetration defence
      } else {
        const jitter = Math.floor(Math.random() * ttl * 0.2); // spread expiries 0–20%
        await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(row), 'EX', ttl + jitter);
      }
      return row;
    } finally {
      inflight.delete(key);
    }
  })();
  inflight.set(key, p);
  return p;
}

Thirty lines, and it already defeats two of the three stampede modes. Note the limit honestly: inflight collapses misses within one process. With 200 pods you still get 200 concurrent origin calls, not 3,000 — a 15x reduction for free, which is usually enough. Cross-process collapsing needs a distributed lock, and that is a worse trade than it looks.

Eviction: pick by access pattern, not by reputation

Eviction only matters when the cache is full, which — if you have sized it correctly — is always. The policy decides which 8% of your working set gets thrown away, and therefore decides your hit ratio.

Eviction policies
PolicyCost per accessStrong onFails on
LRUO(1), one pointer swap; approximated by sampling in RedisTemporal locality — sessions, recently viewed itemsA single large scan evicts the entire hot set ("cache pollution")
LFUO(1) with a counter; needs decay or it fossilisesSkewed popularity — the same 10k products foreverShifting popularity; yesterday's viral item squats in memory for hours
W-TinyLFUO(1), ~4 bits/key sketch admission filter in front of an LRU windowBoth. Beats LRU by 5–15 points of hit ratio on Zipfian trafficNothing much — it is the correct default for L1 (Caffeine implements it)
ARCO(1), two LRU lists plus ghost entries; patent history made it rareAdapting between recency and frequency without tuningMemory overhead of ghost lists; availability in mainstream libraries
TTL onlyFreeCorrectness — it is the only policy that bounds stalenessCapacity. TTL is not an eviction policy, it is an expiry policy
RandomFree, no metadata, no lock contentionUniform access, or as a tie-break under extreme write ratesAny skew at all — it evicts your hottest key as readily as your coldest

The decision, stated plainly: use W-TinyLFU for in-process L1 and sampled-LRU with a TTL on every key for Redis L2 (maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru, never noeviction on a cache). The one policy that is always wrong is TTL alone with no memory bound, because it converts a memory-pressure problem into an OOM kill at 03:00.

Invalidation, and why it is genuinely hard

Invalidation is hard because it is a distributed transaction that nobody writes as one. You must change the origin and remove the derived copy, in the presence of concurrent readers, and the two operations have no shared commit point. There are four strategies and they compose.

Invalidation strategies
StrategyHowStalenessCost
TTLEvery key expiresBounded by TTL, alwaysFree, and the only strategy that self-heals from bugs in the other three
Explicit deleteWriter deletes the key after committingNear-zero when it worksYou must enumerate every key derived from the row. Miss one and it is stale until TTL.
Versioned keyKey embeds a version or content hash; new version = new keyZero — old value is unreachableOld entries linger until evicted; you pay memory for garbage
Tag-basedKeys carry tags; purge by tag (surrogate keys at the CDN)Near-zeroA tag index to maintain, and purge fan-out that can be enormous
plaintext · key schemas that make invalidation cheap
user:v3:{user_id}          # schema version in the key: a deploy invalidates everything, atomically
product:{id}:e{etag}       # content-addressed: never invalidate, just stop referencing the old key
tenant:{tid}:g{gen}        # generation counter in one tiny key; bump gen to drop a whole tenant
feed:{uid}:{yyyymmdd}      # time-boxed: expiry is implicit in the key, no delete needed
render:{tpl}:{locale}:{ab} # EVERY input that changes the output must appear in the key
Production failure — the missing key dimension

The single most damaging cache bug is a key that omits an input the response depends on. A response fragment cached as header:home instead of header:home:{locale}:{user_tier} will serve one user's greeting, entitlements or basket to everyone. At the CDN the same bug appears as a missing Vary header or a cookie stripped in the cache key, and it has taken down major sites by serving authenticated pages to anonymous visitors. The rule is absolute: the cache key must be a pure function of every input to the value, and any response that varies by identity must either be uncacheable at shared layers or be keyed by that identity. Enforce it with a lint rule on key builders, not with code review.

The second hard part is a race, not an omission. With cache-aside, a reader that misses can be paused after reading the origin but before writing the cache; if a writer commits and invalidates in that window, the reader then writes the pre-write value into an empty slot. That stale value now survives until TTL. Mitigations in increasing order of cost: keep TTLs short so the damage window is bounded; write with a Lua compare-and-set that refuses to overwrite a newer version stamp; do a delayed second delete a few hundred milliseconds after the write; or move that key to write-through.

Chosen · Invalidate on write (delete, don't update)
  • Deleting is idempotent and order-insensitive; two concurrent deletes cannot corrupt state, two concurrent updates can.
  • Cost is one extra miss on the next read — a single origin query.
  • Works when the cached value is a projection of many rows, where "the new value" is not knowable at the writer.
  • No write-amplification: a row read once per hour but written ten times per second populates the cache once.
Rejected · Update the cache on write
  • Two writers racing can leave the older value resident permanently — cache and origin disagree with no self-healing.
  • The writer must be able to compute the full cached representation, coupling the write path to every read-path projection.
  • Wasted work on cold keys: you pay serialisation cost for values nobody reads.
  • Justified only for a small set of read-immediately-after-write keys, and then with a version stamp checked by a CAS.

The three stampede modes

A · THUNDERING HERD — one hot key expires B · PENETRATION — key that never exists t=0 TTL ends on product:1 (12k rps) GET /user/random-id × 50k rps ┌─ 12,000 concurrent misses ─┐ cache: MISS MISS MISS MISS … ├─ all recompute one value ─┼──▶ ORIGIN every request reaches ─────▶ ORIGIN └─ 11,999 are pure waste ─┘ fix: negative cache 30 s + bloom filter fix: single-flight, XFetch, stale-while-revalidate C · AVALANCHE — many keys expire at the same instant 12:00 deploy warms 400k keys, all with TTL = 3600 13:00:00 ──▶ 400k simultaneous misses ──▶ origin saturates, latency climbs ──▶ timeouts ──▶ retries ──▶ more load ──▶ cache stays empty ──▶ collapse fix: TTL = 3600 + rand(0..600) on every write, plus a concurrency limiter at the origin
Fig. 2 — the three ways a cache turns into a load amplifier

All three share one shape: the cache stops absorbing and starts multiplying origin load. Herd and avalanche are the same physics at different granularities — one key with huge fan-out versus many keys expiring in phase — and jitter fixes the second while single-flight fixes the first. Penetration is different: the key legitimately has no value, so no amount of TTL tuning helps. Cache the absence, with a shorter TTL than the positive case, and put a Bloom filter (about 1.2 MB per million keys at a 1% false-positive rate) in front of the origin for ID spaces an attacker can enumerate.

The most elegant herd defence is probabilistic early recomputation: rather than everyone expiring at once, each reader independently rolls a die that becomes more likely to say "refresh" as expiry approaches, weighted by how expensive the recompute was. Expensive values get refreshed earlier and by exactly one caller with high probability, and no lock is involved.

python · XFetch probabilistic early expiration
import math, random, time

# Cache stores (value, delta, expiry): delta = seconds the last recompute took.
def get(key, loader, ttl=300, beta=1.0):
    value, delta, expiry = cache.get_with_meta(key)
    now = time.time()
    if value is not None:
        # -delta * beta * ln(U) is an exponential draw; it grows with recompute cost.
        if now - delta * beta * math.log(random.random()) < expiry:
            return value                      # not yet my turn to refresh
    start = time.time()
    value = loader(key)                       # exactly one caller lands here, w.h.p.
    cache.set_with_meta(key, value, delta=time.time() - start, ttl=ttl)
    return value

Tune beta upward to refresh earlier and more eagerly. In practice, pair it with stale-while-revalidate: serve the expired value to readers while one caller recomputes. That converts a latency spike into a bounded staleness window, which is almost always the better trade.

Hot keys

Distributed caches shard by key, so a single key is served by a single shard, and one Redis shard executes commands on one core — call it 100k simple GETs per second. A key at 400k QPS therefore cannot be served, no matter how many nodes you add. The cluster is not overloaded; one core is. Three fixes, in the order you should apply them:

  1. L1 in front of L2. A 200-instance fleet with a 500 ms local TTL asks Redis for that key at most 200 ÷ 0.5 = 400 times per second. That is a 1,000x reduction for roughly twenty lines of code, and it costs you at most 500 ms of extra staleness. Do this first, always.
  2. Split the key. Store N identical copies as product:1#0 … product:1#15 and have readers pick a suffix at random. Sixteen copies spread across shards give 16x headroom. The cost is that invalidation must now delete all sixteen, so only do this for keys with a pure TTL policy.
  3. Replicate the shard. Add read replicas for the shard holding the hot key and route reads to them. Least effective per unit of money, and it adds replication lag to the staleness budget — but it needs no application change, which sometimes matters at 03:00.

Detect hot keys before they detect you: Redis --hotkeys sampling, or client-side count-min sketch reporting the top 20 keys per minute. Any key above roughly 2% of a shard's throughput is on its way to becoming an incident.

Cross-region coherence: usually, don't

The instinct after building a two-region deployment is to broadcast invalidations so both caches agree. Resist it. Cross-region propagation is 70–150 ms and unreliable; you cannot make the origin write and two remote deletes atomic without a consensus protocol you do not want in the read path. Worse, an invalidation broadcast that outruns your data replication actively causes harm: the far region evicts, immediately re-reads its local, still-lagging replica, and re-caches the old value with a fresh TTL. You have converted a 200 ms staleness window into a full TTL of staleness.

Default
Region-local caches, region-local TTLs, no cross-region invalidation. Staleness is bounded by max(TTL, replication lag) and you write that number in the SLO.
If you need better
Carry the invalidation in the replication stream itself — a CDC consumer per region that deletes keys as it applies the change. The invalidation then cannot arrive before the data, which is the entire failure mode above.
If you need exact
Do not cache that read. Route it to the owning region, or read the origin with a version check. Exact global coherence and a cache are close to mutually exclusive; pick one deliberately.

Sizing: hit ratio, working set, and whether the cache pays for itself

Start from the origin's capacity, not from the cache's size. Suppose peak read traffic is 60k QPS and a database replica sustains 8k indexed reads per second at an acceptable p99. Origin load is (1 - h) × 60,000. Without a cache you need 60,000 / 8,000 = 8 replicas, call it 10 with headroom. At h = 0.85 the origin sees 9k QPS and you need 2. At h = 0.95 it sees 3k QPS and you need 1. At h = 0.99 it sees 600 QPS and the primary alone suffices.

Break-even hit ratio
84%(1-h)×60k ≤ 8k for one replica-worth of load
Cache cost
~$3.6k/mo6 nodes × ~$600/mo list, 3 shards RF=2
Replicas saved
~$8.7k/mo6 fewer replicas × ~$1.45k/mo list
Each miss-rate halving
2x95%→97.5% removes as much origin load as 0%→50%

That last tile is the point people miss. Origin load is proportional to the miss rate, so the value of hit ratio is hyperbolic, not linear. Moving from 90% to 95% halves origin traffic. Moving from 99% to 99.5% halves it again. Reporting "we improved hit ratio by 1 point" is meaningless without saying which point.

Size the memory by measuring, not by modelling. Plot hit ratio against cache size on a log axis and find the knee. A realistic catalogue workload — 200M items averaging 1.5 KB, so 300 GB of data that will never fit — behaves like this: 8 GB → 71%, 16 GB → 82%, 32 GB → 89%, 64 GB → 92%, 128 GB → 93.5%. The knee is at 32–64 GB. Doubling to 128 GB buys 1.5 points, which sounds negligible until you notice it cuts miss traffic from 8% to 6.5%, a 19% reduction in origin load, for $600/month. Do that arithmetic before dismissing the tail. Budget overhead honestly too: Redis costs roughly 80–100 bytes of metadata per key, so 50M small keys is ~4–5 GB before a single value, and you should run at 70–75% of maxmemory so that fragmentation and replication buffers do not trigger eviction storms.

Consistent hashing for the cache cluster

Hash ring, 0 … 2^32-1, with 150 virtual nodes per physical node (imbalance stays under ~10%) ├───A───┬──C──┬────B────┬───A───┬──D──┬───B───┬──C──┬───A───┤ ▲ key ─ hash ┘ walk clockwise to the first vnode ▶ owner = B ADD NODE E: ├───A───┬─C─┬E┬────B────┬───A───┬─D─┬E┬───B───┬──C──┤ only keys falling in the E arcs move: 1/(n+1) = 1/5 = 20% MODULO n: hash % 4 ▶ hash % 5 relocates ~80% of all keys ▶ instant cold-cache avalanche
Fig. 3 — why cache clusters hash on a ring and not with modulo

This is the whole argument for consistent hashing in a cache tier: a topology change must not invalidate the cache. With modulo hashing, replacing one failed node out of ten misplaces roughly 90% of keys and the resulting miss flood hits the origin as an avalanche — the node failure becomes a database outage. With a ring at 150 vnodes per node, losing one of ten moves about 10% of keys, which the origin absorbs. Two refinements are worth the code: bounded loads (skip to the next vnode if the chosen one is above 1.25x average) to stop a single unlucky shard collecting hot keys, and rendezvous hashing if you want the same property with less state and simpler reasoning about replica sets. Memcached clients do this in the client library; Redis Cluster instead uses 16,384 fixed hash slots explicitly assigned to nodes, which gives the same guarantee with an operator-controlled mapping.

Warming, and the cold-cache herd after a restart

Production failure — the empty cache

The classic outage is not a slow cache, it is an absent one. A Redis failover, a cluster resize, a FLUSHALL from a misfired script, or a rolling deploy that restarts every process at once takes hit ratio from 95% to 0. Origin load jumps 20x instantly — the exact multiplier is 1/(1-h), so the better your steady-state hit ratio, the more violent the cliff. The origin saturates, clients time out and retry, and retries add load faster than the cache refills. The system cannot recover on its own because recovery requires the origin to serve the misses that are saturating it.

Design for the empty cache explicitly, before you need to:

  • Cap origin concurrency. A semaphore of, say, 200 in-flight origin queries per service means a cold cache produces a slow site, not a dead database. Shed the excess with a fast 503 and a Retry-After. This is the single highest-value control in this chapter.
  • Deploy in waves. Restart 10% of instances at a time so 90% of L1 caches stay warm and the load shift is gradual. Never restart the whole fleet on a single health-check flap.
  • Warm deliberately. Replay the top-N keys from the previous hour's access log into the new cache before it takes traffic. Warming 50k keys at 500 loads/second takes 100 seconds — schedule it as a pre-traffic step in the deploy, not as a hope.
  • Keep the L2 out of the deploy blast radius. Redis should not restart when your application does. If it must, use replica promotion rather than a cold start, and prefer maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru with persistence enabled purely so a restart reloads a warm dataset.
  • Rehearse it. Flush the cache in staging at production-shaped load, monthly. If nobody has ever seen the graph, you do not know your 1/(1-h).

Measuring: three numbers, by layer

An aggregate hit ratio hides everything interesting. Instrument per layer and per key prefix.

The instrumentation that actually catches problems
MetricCut byHealthyWhat a regression means
Hit ratioLayer × key prefixL1 20–40%, L2 90–98%, CDN 92%+New key dimension added, TTL shortened, or working set grew past the knee
Origin QPS from missesServiceWell under the capacity you sized forThe number that actually pages you — hit ratio is a proxy for it, not a substitute
Latency by layerp50 / p99 per hopL1 < 50 µs, L2 p99 < 2 msRedis p99 spikes usually mean a big key, a KEYS scan, or eviction pressure
Eviction rateNodeSteady and low relative to write rateRising evictions with a falling hit ratio = undersized memory; act before hit ratio dips
Key/value size distributionp99, maxp99 under ~100 KBOne 10 MB value blocks a single-threaded shard for milliseconds and wrecks everyone's p99
Stale-serve rateEndpointKnown, and inside the staleness SLOYou cannot claim a consistency guarantee you do not measure

What must never be cached

  • Authorisation decisions beyond their revocation budget. Cache the token signature verification and the JWKS; do not cache "user X may access resource Y" for an hour unless you can also revoke it in under an hour. If you cache it, key it on a permissions version you can bump.
  • Anything personalised at a shared layer. If the value depends on identity, it belongs in a private cache or in a key containing that identity. There is no third option.
  • Data under a legal erasure obligation unless the cache is enumerable and purgeable. A GDPR deletion request must reach every copy; a TTL of 30 days is not a deletion mechanism.
  • Payment authorisations, one-time tokens, nonces, OTPs. Replaying a cached "approved" is fraud, not staleness.
  • Values whose loss is unacceptable. Caches evict without asking. If losing it breaks correctness, it belongs in a database.
  • Cheap queries. A 0.4 ms primary-key lookup cached in a 0.8 ms Redis round-trip is slower, less consistent and more code. Measure the origin before caching it.
What a strong candidate says

"I'd cache read-heavy product reads with cache-aside in Redis Cluster, TTL 300 seconds plus up to 20% jitter, keys product:v2:{id}:{locale} — locale is in the key because it changes the output. Origin capacity is about 8k QPS per replica against 60k peak, so I need roughly 85% hit ratio to run on two replicas; I'd expect 92–95% and I'd track origin miss-QPS rather than hit ratio because that's the number that actually saturates. Three protections: single-flight in-process plus stale-while-revalidate for the herd, a null sentinel with a 30-second TTL for penetration, and jittered TTLs for avalanche. Writers delete rather than update, and I accept a bounded stale window from the read-modify race — with a version-checked CAS on the handful of write-then-read-immediately keys. Cross-region I keep caches region-local and put the staleness in the SLO instead of broadcasting invalidations, because a broadcast that beats the replication stream re-caches stale data for a full TTL. And the thing I'd build first is a concurrency limiter on the origin, because the outage that actually happens is the cold cache after a failover, where load jumps by 1/(1-h) — twenty-fold at 95%."

Rule of thumb

Before adding a cache, try making the query fast. An index, a denormalised column or a smaller payload gives you a permanent improvement with no staleness, no invalidation bugs, no extra tier to operate and no cold-start cliff. Caching is what you reach for when the origin work is genuinely irreducible — a fan-out, an aggregation, a third-party call. Reaching for it to paper over a missing index buys you the same latency and a new pager rotation.

Caching is the cheapest performance lever in systems design and the most expensive correctness liability. Three things carry across every design in the rest of this book: the key must be a total function of the value's inputs, or you will serve the wrong data to the wrong person; the TTL is a staleness contract, so write it down and measure whether you keep it; and every cache is also a load amplifier waiting for the moment it goes empty, so size the origin for a miss rate you have actually survived in a drill rather than for the hit ratio you enjoy on a good afternoon.

07

Queues, logs & streaming

Asynchrony is the only primitive that lets a system accept more work than it can currently do without either failing the caller or falling over. Everything else in this chapter — partitions, offsets, watermarks, outboxes — exists to make that trade survivable: you buy latency tolerance and decoupling, and you pay in duplicates, reordering and a queue that can silently become the deepest part of your architecture.

What async actually buys, priced honestly

Three distinct wins get bundled under "make it async", and they are worth separating because each has a different cost.

  • Absorbing spikes. A queue converts a load problem into a latency problem. That is only a win if someone has agreed that the latency is acceptable.
  • Decoupling availability. A synchronous fan-out multiplies failure. Five 99.9% dependencies in series give 0.9995 = 99.50%, or 3.6 hours of downtime a month. Publishing once to a durable log and letting each consumer fail independently takes the checkout path back to its own availability.
  • Getting slow work off the request path. The caller waits for the append, not for the work.
SYNCHRONOUS FAN-OUT ASYNC OFF THE REQUEST PATH ┌────────────┐ ┌────────────┐ │ checkout │ │ checkout │ └─────┬──────┘ └─────┬──────┘ │ 4 blocking calls │ 1 append · 8 ms p99 ├──▶ fraud 120 ms ▼ ├──▶ email 180 ms p99 ┌──────────────────┐ ├──▶ warehouse 90 ms │ orders.v1 │ 24 partitions └──▶ analytics 60 ms └──┬────┬────┬────┬─┘ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ own work 210 ms fraud email whse analytics dependencies + 450 ms each fails, retries and scales alone p99 = 660 ms · avail 99.50% p99 = 218 ms · avail 99.90%
Fig. 1 — the same four side effects, priced as a blocking fan-out and as one append

Size the buffer with arithmetic, not vibes. Take a flash sale: producers peak at 50k messages/s for 90 seconds while the consumer fleet sustains 12k/s. Backlog = (50,000 − 12,000) × 90 = 3.42M messages. Once the surge ends and producers settle at 8k/s, net drain is 4k/s, so the tail clears in 3.42M / 4,000 = ~855 s, about 14 minutes. At 2 KB per message that is 6.8 GB of broker disk — irrelevant on a machine with terabytes. The cost is not storage. The cost is that an order confirmation email sent 14 minutes late is a support ticket, so someone has to sign off on that number before you build it.

Work queue versus durable log
