                   / sum(sum(db_time_ms)) OVER (), 1)  AS pct_of_db_time,
       round(sum(db_time_ms)::numeric / count(*), 1)   AS ms_per_req,
       max(rows_scanned)                               AS worst_scan
FROM   request_log
WHERE  ts >= now() - interval '5 minutes'
GROUP  BY tenant_id
HAVING sum(db_time_ms) > 5000
ORDER  BY db_seconds DESC
LIMIT  10;

Alert on share, not on absolutes: page when any single tenant exceeds ~25% of total database time over five minutes, or when a tenant's ms_per_req moves more than 3× against its own 7-day baseline. Absolute thresholds go stale the moment you sign a bigger customer; share thresholds do not. At very high cardinality, keep the top-K with a Count-Min sketch or a Space-Saving counter in the gateway rather than aggregating full logs — you need the heavy hitters in seconds, and you do not need the tail at all.

Production failure — attribution by request count

The classic wrong dashboard ranks tenants by request rate. The tenant that takes a service down is routinely somewhere around rank 40 by request count and rank 1 by resource-seconds: a few hundred requests an hour, each one an unindexed scan holding a connection for 8 seconds. Rate limits pass it, the top-talkers dashboard exonerates it, and the connection pool is exhausted for everyone. If you build one thing from this section, build per-tenant resource-second attribution — and enforce a per-tenant connection cap so the pool cannot be monopolised while you are still reading the dashboard.

Admission control and priority shedding

Every limit above is configured from the capacity you believed you had. Load shedding is the control that responds to the capacity you actually have right now, and it is what stops a partial degradation from becoming a total one. Trigger it on queue delay, not on CPU or queue length: CPU at 90% may be healthy, and a queue of 500 means nothing until you know how fast it drains. Measure the time each request spent waiting before a worker picked it up; when that exceeds a target (a CoDel-style 5–20 ms sustained over ~100 ms), start refusing.

Shed trigger
20 mssustained queue delay, not CPU or queue depth — delay is the only signal that means "we are behind"
Retry budget
10%retries capped as a fraction of successes; above this a partial outage becomes a total one
Queue discipline
LIFOunder overload only: FIFO serves requests whose clients already gave up, so all work is wasted
Reserved headroom
5%capacity that only CRITICAL traffic may use, so health checks and auth survive the shed

Shed by priority, and set the priority at the edge of the system where intent is known, then propagate it through every downstream call so a background job cannot borrow a user's criticality. Four classes are enough: CRITICAL_PLUS (authentication, payments, health checks), CRITICAL (interactive user requests), SHEDDABLE_PLUS (prefetch, analytics writes, non-urgent webhooks), SHEDDABLE (batch, backfills, re-indexing). Shed from the bottom, in order, in 10% increments, and recover slowly — halving admission instantly and restoring it instantly produces oscillation that is worse than steady degradation. Combine with adaptive concurrency (a gradient or Vegas-style controller that discovers the in-flight limit from observed latency) so the limit tracks real capacity across deploys, instance types and dependency slowdowns instead of a number someone tuned in 2023.

Cheap rejection is the whole point

A shed only helps if refusing is dramatically cheaper than serving. Reject before deserialising the body, before authenticating against a remote service, before acquiring a connection. If your 429 path costs 8 ms because it hits Redis, renders a templated error and writes a structured audit log, you have built a limiter that fails hardest exactly when it is needed most. Budget under 1 ms for a rejection, and sample the rejection logs rather than writing one line per refusal.

Interview · say this in the first ninety seconds

"I'd put a cost-weighted token bucket in Redis behind a Lua script, keyed by tenant and endpoint class, with a local pre-check so most requests never make the round trip. Token bucket because it separates sustained rate from allowed burst, and the cost weight lets one mechanism price a 0.4 ms read and a 4 s export correctly. Then three things people usually forget: a per-tenant concurrency limit, because rate limits stop protecting you the moment a tenant's requests get slower; a documented fail-open policy for protection limits and fail-closed for auth limits, with a 10 ms timeout on the limiter itself; and priority-based shedding on queue delay as the last line, because every limit above is calibrated for capacity I merely assumed. Quotas I'd build separately as an event-sourced meter — a rate limiter is allowed to lose its counters, a biller is not." Then ask what the tenant size distribution looks like: if it is a power law, the interesting design work is placement and isolation, not the algorithm.

Build order

  1. Tenant id on every request and every log line. Without attribution nothing else in this chapter can be measured, tuned or defended in a postmortem.
  2. Per-tenant concurrency limit. One semaphore. It protects against the failure mode that actually takes systems down, and it needs no shared state.
  3. Cost-weighted token bucket per tenant per endpoint class. Publish the numbers, ship the headers, honour Retry-After in your own SDKs.
  4. Resource-second attribution and share-based alerting. Top ten tenants by database time over five minutes, alerted on share of total.
  5. Priority-based shedding on queue delay. Four classes, propagated downstream, rejecting in under a millisecond.
  6. Event-sourced metering. Only once you are billing on usage — and built as a durable pipeline, never as a long-window rate limit.
  7. Isolation upgrades, per tenant, for a named reason. Residency, blast radius or working-set size. Never as a default, and never for the whole fleet at once.
10

Reliability & failure design

Every component you depend on will fail, and the ones that fail slowly are far more dangerous than the ones that stop. Reliability is not the absence of failure; it is the property that failures stay small.

A taxonomy worth having

Crash
The component stops. Easiest to handle: health checks detect it, traffic moves away.
Omission
Requests are silently dropped. Looks like packet loss; retries usually cover it.
Timing
Responses arrive, but too late to be useful. Timeouts convert this into a crash you can handle.
Gray failure
Partially working: 5% error rate, or p99 at ten seconds while p50 is fine. Health checks pass. This is the hardest class, and it is the most common in practice.
Byzantine
Wrong answers rather than no answers — a corrupted cache, a bad deploy on one node. Detected by cross-checking, not by health probes.
Gray failure defeats naive health checking

A node returning errors for one in twenty requests still answers /health with 200. Load balancers keep sending it traffic, and your error rate sits at 5% indefinitely. The defence is outlier detection — compare each instance's error rate and latency against its peers and eject the ones that deviate — rather than asking each instance whether it thinks it is healthy.

Availability arithmetic

99%
7h 18mper 30 days
99.9%
43mthe usual target
99.99%
4m 19sno manual step in any recovery path
99.999%
26srarely worth what it costs
Dependencies in series MULTIPLY. API (99.9) → auth (99.9) → database (99.9) → cache (99.9) → payments (99.9) 0.999^5 = 99.5% — 3.6 hours/month, from five "three nines" components Redundancy in parallel ADDS NINES. two independent replicas at 99% each: 1 − (0.01)² = 99.99% …but only if the failures are INDEPENDENT. Two instances in one rack, on one power feed, running one bad deploy, are not independent at all. Which is why the useful questions are: · how many dependencies are in my critical path, and can any be made optional? · what is my largest correlated failure domain? · what is the blast radius of the most likely single fault?
Removing a dependency is worth more than adding a nine

Making the recommendations service optional — serve a cached fallback when it fails — takes it out of the multiplication entirely. That is usually cheaper and more effective than making it more reliable, and it is the first question to ask about anything in the critical path.

Cascading failure

How a slow dependency becomes a total outage, step by step: 1. service C slows from 50 ms to 3 s (a garbage-collection pause, a bad query) 2. B's threads/connections fill waiting on C 3. B stops answering health checks → the load balancer ejects instances of B 4. remaining B instances get MORE traffic → they fill faster 5. A retries B's failures → 3× the load on a system already failing 6. C, now receiving 3× traffic, degrades further 7. total outage — and C was never actually down Break the loop at any point and it does not happen: · timeouts at step 2 (B fails fast instead of filling) · bulkheads at step 2 (only C's pool fills, not B's whole capacity) · circuit breaker at 5 (B stops calling C at all) · retry budget at 5 (retries capped at a % of traffic) · load shedding at 4 (B refuses work it cannot complete in time)

The pattern catalogue

PatternPreventsCost
Timeoutunbounded waiting; resource exhaustionnone — this is always correct
Retry + backoff + jittertransient failures reaching the useramplification if unbudgeted; only safe for idempotent calls
Circuit breakerhammering a failing dependencya window of rejected requests that might have succeeded
Bulkheadone dependency consuming all capacitylower peak utilisation; more configuration
Load sheddingcollapse under overloadsome users rejected — deliberately
Backpressureunbounded queues and memory growthproducers must handle being slowed
Fallbacka failure becoming user-visiblea degraded answer, which must be designed and tested
Hedged requesttail latencyextra load — only for a small percentage of requests
Outlier ejectiongray failureneeds peer comparison; can eject too aggressively

Health checks that do not make things worse

javascript · the distinction that prevents fleet-wide restarts
// LIVENESS: "is this process wedged?" — restart me if this fails.
// It must check NOTHING external. If it checked the database, a 30-second
// database failover would restart every instance simultaneously, losing all
// in-flight work and stampeding the database as it recovers.
app.get('/livez', (_req, res) => res.status(200).end());

// READINESS: "should I receive traffic right now?"
// Critical dependencies belong here — but cached, with a short timeout, and
// never so strict that a brief blip removes the whole fleet from rotation.
app.get('/readyz', async (_req, res) => {
  if (draining) return res.status(503).end();
  const ok = await cachedCheck(2_000, () => db.ping({ timeoutMs: 800 }));
  res.status(ok ? 200 : 503).end();
});

// DEPENDENCY STATUS: for humans and dashboards, never for automated ejection.
app.get('/status', async (_req, res) => res.json(await probeAll()));

Disaster recovery

RPO
Recovery point objective — how much data you can afford to lose, measured in time. Set by the business, not by engineering.
RTO
Recovery time objective — how long you can afford to be down. Determines the topology: backup-and-restore, warm standby, or active-active.
The rule
An untested backup is not a backup. Schedule restore drills, measure the actual RTO, and treat a failed drill as an incident.
What teams forget to back up

The database, always. Then: infrastructure definitions, secrets and their encryption keys, container images, DNS configuration, CI/CD pipeline definitions and third-party integration settings. A team that can restore its database into an environment it cannot rebuild has not tested recovery — it has tested one step of it.

Interview follow-up

“How would you make this system more reliable?” The weak answer adds replicas. The strong answer starts by asking what the largest correlated failure domain is and how many dependencies sit in the critical path, then proposes removing one from that path entirely by making it optional with a designed fallback — because that changes the multiplication rather than adding a decimal place to one term. Follow it with timeouts everywhere and a named degradation mode per dependency, and you have described a system that fails small.

11

Observability & operating at scale

Monitoring tells you the system is broken. Observability lets you work out why without deploying new code to find out. At scale the constraint is not what you can collect but what you can afford to store and query.

Three signals, three jobs

MetricsLogsTraces
Answersis the system healthy?what happened to this request?where did the latency go?
Cardinalitymust be lowhighhigh, but sampled
Costcheap per series, expensive per label combinationexpensive per GB ingestedexpensive; sample it
Retentionmonths to yearsdays to weeksdays
Alertableyesrarely — use it for contextno
Cardinality is the thing that takes down your monitoring

A metric labelled with user_id creates one time series per user. Ten million users is ten million series, and Prometheus will fall over long before that. The rule is absolute: labels must be bounded and known in advance — route pattern, status class, region, tenant tier. Never an id, a URL, an email, a full path or an error message. When you need per-entity detail, that is what logs and traces are for.

What to measure

RED — for every request-driven service Rate requests/second, by route and status Errors failed requests/second, and the ratio Duration latency distribution — HISTOGRAM, never an average USE — for every resource (CPU, memory, disk, pool, queue) Utilisation % of time busy Saturation how much work is queued and waiting Errors error events Saturation is the leading indicator and the one people omit. Utilisation at 100% tells you it is full now; queue depth rising tells you it will be full soon, which is the only one you can act on. Plus, per system: one or two BUSINESS metrics. A checkout success rate that drops while every technical dashboard is green is a real and common failure — a bad deploy that breaks a validation rule looks perfectly healthy.

Traces, and why sampling strategy matters

A trace is a tree of spans across services, joined by a propagated context: trace 7c1d POST /checkout 820 ms ├─ auth.verify 12 ms ├─ cart.get 30 ms ├─ pricing.quote 240 ms │ └─ tax.calculate 210 ms ◀── the answer ├─ inventory.reserve 45 ms └─ payment.authorise 480 ms └─ (external) 470 ms ◀── and this HEAD sampling decide at the first span (e.g. keep 1%) cheap, but you keep a random 1% — which is almost never the slow ones TAIL sampling buffer the whole trace, decide when it completes keep 100% of errors, 100% of traces over p99, 1% of the rest this is what you want — the interesting traces are the rare ones cost: the collector must buffer spans until the trace finishes
javascript · context propagation is the whole game
// A trace only exists if the context crosses every boundary. The three that
// are routinely forgotten:
//   1. queue messages   — inject the trace context into the message headers
//   2. background jobs  — link the job's span to the request that enqueued it
//   3. batch operations — one span per batch, with an attribute for the count

// Enqueue: carry the context with the work
const carrier = {};
propagation.inject(context.active(), carrier);
await queue.add('send-receipt', { orderId, _trace: carrier });

// Consume: continue the SAME trace, days later if necessary
const parent = propagation.extract(context.active(), job.data._trace);
tracer.startActiveSpan('send-receipt', {}, parent, async (span) => { ... });

// Without this, an async flow appears as a request that ended at "queued"
// and an unrelated job that started from nothing — and you can never answer
// "what happened to this customer's receipt?"

Correlation

Request id
Generated at the edge, returned in a response header, present on every log line. The user's screenshot becomes a database query.
Trace id
In every log line too, so a log search jumps straight to the waterfall and back.
Exemplars
Attach a trace id to a histogram bucket, so clicking the p99 spike on a dashboard opens an actual slow trace. This is the single highest-value observability integration and it is frequently unconfigured.
Version and deploy id
On every metric and log. "Did this start at the deploy?" should be answerable in one query, not by comparing timestamps by eye.

Cost control without going blind

LeverSavingWhat you lose
Drop high-cardinality labelsvery largeper-entity slicing in metrics — use logs instead
Tail-sample traces at 1–5%largealmost nothing, if errors and slow traces are always kept
Log identifiers, not bodieslargethe ability to replay a request — keep it behind a debug flag
Tier log retention (7d hot, 30d cold)moderatequery speed on old data
Recording rules for dashboard queriesmoderatenothing — this is strictly a win
Sample debug/info logsmoderatesome context; keep 100% of warn and error
The observability bill routinely exceeds the compute bill

Logging every request and response body at a few thousand requests per second produces terabytes a month, and ingestion is priced per gigabyte. Teams discover this on an invoice rather than a dashboard. Decide deliberately what each signal is for, and treat log volume as a resource with a budget like any other.

Interview follow-up

“How would you debug a latency regression in a distributed system?” Name the sequence and the signal at each step: RED metrics to find which service and whether it is p50 or p99; USE to find whether a resource is saturated; a trace waterfall from a slow request to find where the time went; logs filtered by the trace id for the detail. Then mention exemplars, because being able to click from a latency histogram straight into a representative slow trace is what turns that sequence from twenty minutes into two.

12

Security & privacy by design

Security decisions that belong on the architecture diagram, not in a hardening sprint six months later. Most of them are cheap when designed in and structurally expensive to retrofit.

Trust boundaries

Draw them on the diagram. A trust boundary is any line where data crosses from something you control to something you do not — and every one needs authentication, authorisation, validation and rate limiting. internet ┃ edge ┃ services ┃ data stores ▲ ▲ ▲ │ │ └─ least-privilege DB users; no shared admin creds │ └─ service identity (mTLS / signed tokens), NOT network trust └─ authn, authz, validation, rate limits, TLS The flat-network mistake: assuming that anything inside the VPC is trustworthy. One compromised service, one SSRF, one leaked internal endpoint, and there is no second line of defence. Every service should authenticate its callers even when they are internal.

Authentication and authorisation

ModelDecides onFitsWatch out
RBACthe subject's rolesmall, fixed permission setsrole explosion as exceptions accumulate
Scopestoken capabilitiesAPIs, OAuth clientscoarse; composes with RBAC rather than replacing it
ABACattributes of subject, resource, environment"a manager may approve under £5 000 in their own department"hard to audit; policies drift
ReBACthe relationship graphsharing, folders, org hierarchiesneeds a purpose-built store to be fast
Broken object-level authorisation is the most exploited API flaw there is

The endpoint checks that you are logged in and then fetches by id — so any authenticated user can read any record by changing a number. It is not caught by penetration tests that only check for login, it is not visible in a schema, and it appears the moment someone adds an endpoint without thinking. The structural fix is that the tenant or owner is part of the query, applied by a repository the handler cannot bypass, plus a generated test per endpoint that asserts a cross-tenant read returns 404. Discipline alone fails at the twentieth endpoint.

Secrets and keys

  • Short-lived over long-lived. A workload identity that mints a fifteen-minute credential is categorically safer than a static key, because a leak has an expiry. OIDC federation from CI and workload identity in the cluster remove the static secret entirely.
  • Rotation must be routine. Accept an array of valid secrets — verify against all, sign with the first — so rotation is two deploys with no user-visible event. A rotation procedure that has never been run does not work.
  • Envelope encryption for data at rest: a data key per record or tenant, encrypted by a master key in a KMS. Rotating the master then does not require re-encrypting the data.
  • Never in the image, the repo, the log, or a URL. Environment variables are readable by any code in the process, including a compromised dependency, and they appear in crash dumps.

Privacy as an architectural constraint

The problem: "delete my data" is easy in a normalised database and very hard in a system with backups, event logs, analytics warehouses, search indexes, caches and third-party processors. You cannot rewrite an immutable event log, and you should not be deleting from backups. CRYPTO-SHREDDING makes it tractable: · encrypt each user's personal data with a per-user data key · store the data key in a key store, referenced by user id · on erasure, destroy the key The ciphertext remains everywhere — backups, logs, warehouse, replicas — and is now permanently unreadable. One key deletion satisfies erasure across every system that ever received the data. Combine with: · minimisation do not collect what you do not need · separation PII in one store, behavioural data keyed by a pseudonymous id in another · retention a TTL per data class, enforced by the store · residency a tenant's home region decided at signup, not retrofitted
Residency cannot be added later

"EU customer data must stay in the EU" is a routing and schema decision that touches every service. Retrofitting it into a system that shards by user id and replicates globally is a migration of everything. If there is any prospect of the requirement, pin tenants to a home region from the first day — it costs almost nothing then, and it is a rewrite later.

Threat modelling, briefly and usefully

Spoofing
Can someone claim to be another user or service? → authn, mTLS, signed tokens.
Tampering
Can data be modified in transit or at rest? → TLS, signatures, integrity checks.
Repudiation
Can someone deny an action? → append-only audit log the operator cannot edit.
Information disclosure
What leaks? → authz, encryption, and the 404-not-403 rule for existence.
Denial of service
What is expensive to request? → rate limits, quotas, complexity caps, load shedding.
Elevation of privilege
Can a user become an admin? → least privilege, no client-side authorisation.

Run this against the trust boundaries on your diagram, not against the codebase. Fifteen minutes in a design review catches more than a scanner does later, because the expensive flaws are architectural.

Interview follow-up

“Where would you put security in this design?” The strong answer refuses the premise that it is a layer: authentication at every trust boundary including internal ones, authorisation checked against the object and enforced by the data-access layer rather than by handlers, secrets that are short-lived rather than merely well-stored, and privacy handled by crypto-shredding so erasure does not require rewriting immutable history. Then name the one thing that must be decided now because it cannot be retrofitted — data residency, or the tenancy model.

13

Cost, capacity & evolution

Two constraints that never appear in the requirements and always decide the outcome: what the design costs to run, and how much of it you can change once it is running.

Where the money goes

LineDriverThe usual surprise
Storage + retentiondata volume × replication × retentionkeeping raw events for years because nobody set a TTL
Data transferegress, cross-AZ, cross-regioncross-AZ chatter between microservices; NAT gateway charges
Computepeak provisioningnon-production running 24/7; autoscaling that never scales in
Observabilityingestion volumefrequently exceeds the compute bill
Managed servicesconvenience premiumreal, and usually worth it — but measure it rather than assuming
Unit economics change decisions; totals only change moods

"We spend £180 000 a month" produces a meeting. "This endpoint costs £0.004 per call and is called nine million times a day by one customer on a £200 plan" produces a decision. Cost per request, per tenant and per feature are the numbers that let engineering and product argue about the same thing.

Capacity planning

Provision for: peak + failure domain loss + headroom peak measured, not guessed — yesterday's graph − one AZ if you run in 3 AZs, you must survive on 2 ⇒ each AZ runs at ≤ 66% of its capacity at peak + headroom 20-30% for spikes, deploys, and being wrong Which means steady-state utilisation of roughly 50% is not waste; it is the cost of surviving the failure you designed for. Little's Law L = λ × W concurrency = arrival rate × latency 500 rps × 200 ms = 100 concurrent requests ⇒ halving latency halves the concurrency you must provision for. Latency work is capacity work, and it is usually cheaper.

Reversible and irreversible decisions

Reversible — decide fast
  • Which cache library
  • Instance sizes and counts
  • Internal service boundaries
  • Language for a new service
  • Queue technology
Irreversible — spend your design budget here
  • Shard key — changing it means rewriting the dataset
  • Public API contract — clients you do not control depend on it
  • Tenancy model — retrofitting isolation is a rewrite
  • Data residency — touches routing and schema everywhere
  • Event schemas — consumers you cannot coordinate with
  • Primary key format — referenced by everything, forever

Almost all design-review time should go to the right-hand column. A wrong cache library costs a week; a wrong shard key costs a quarter and a migration nobody wants to run.

Evolutionary architecture

Start with a modular monolith. One deployable, clear internal module boundaries, one database with per-module schemas. You get: · transactions across modules while the boundaries are still wrong · refactoring without a network hop and a version negotiation · one deploy, one trace, one log stream Extract a service when there is a specific reason, and name it: · genuinely different scaling profile (a transcoder, an ML scorer) · genuinely different availability need (payments vs recommendations) · a team boundary that is causing real merge and release contention · a compliance boundary requiring separation Not a reason: "microservices are best practice", or "it will be easier later" — extraction is easier later, from a well-modularised monolith, than it is now from a design nobody has validated. Strangler fig for the extraction: 1. put a facade in front of the module 2. build the new service alongside; dual-write or shadow-read 3. move read traffic, compare results, then move writes 4. delete the old path only when traffic is zero Start with the DATA boundary. If two modules share tables, they are one service wearing two names, and splitting the code first just adds a network call to a distributed transaction you did not want.

What to present in a design review

  1. Requirements and numbers first. Scale, latency, consistency, the failure you must survive. Everything after this is only judgeable against them.
  2. The simple version. Draw the design you would build if the numbers were 10× smaller. It is often surprisingly close to correct.
  3. Where it breaks, and what you would change. This is the part that demonstrates judgement rather than recall.
  4. The two or three decisions that are irreversible, with the alternatives you rejected and why.
  5. What you have deliberately not built, and the signal that would make you build it.
  6. Cost and operational burden. Who carries the pager, and what does it cost per month.
The closing thought

The strongest answer to "how would you scale this to ten times the load" is rarely a bigger diagram. It is: here is the specific component that saturates first and the measurement that tells me; here is the change I would make; here is what that change costs and what it makes harder. A design is a sequence of justified changes over time, not a picture that is correct once. Interviewers are assessing whether you can reason about the sequence — and so is anyone who has to operate what you build.

S01

Ride-hailing (Uber-style)

Two hard problems sit inside this system and everything else is plumbing: indexing a million moving objects so you can ask "who is near this point" in milliseconds, and running a matching decision that is good globally rather than greedy locally.

Functional
  • Rider requests a ride from A to B; sees an ETA and a fare estimate
  • System matches a nearby available driver; driver accepts or declines
  • Both parties see live position for the duration of the trip
  • Trip lifecycle: requested → matched → arriving → in progress → completed
  • Fare computed and charged at completion; receipt issued
Non-functional
  • Match decision within 5 s of request
  • Driver location freshness under 5 s
  • A trip must never be lost — survives app kill, crash, network loss
  • A driver must never be dispatched to two trips
  • Regional isolation: a failure in one city must not affect another
Out of scope
  • Payments internals (see the payments case study)
  • Driver onboarding and background checks
  • Pool/shared rides
  • Routing engine internals — treated as a service

Back-of-envelope

Monthly users
100 M~5 M rides/day
Ride requests
~60/s avg×5 peak = 300/s
Active drivers
1 M concurrentthe number that shapes everything
Location writes
250 k/s1 M drivers ÷ 4 s update interval
Trip storage
~2 GB/day5 M × ~400 B, plus location traces

250 000 writes per second of ephemeral, immediately-stale data is the defining number. It rules out a general-purpose database as the location store: these writes never need durability, never need transactions, and are worthless four seconds later. They belong in memory, sharded by geography.

API

plaintext · the endpoints that matter
POST /rides                    { pickup, dropoff, productType }
  → 202 { rideId, state: "matching" }        — never block on matching

GET  /rides/{id}               → { state, driver?, eta?, route? }
WS   /rides/{id}/track         ← server pushes driver position + state changes

POST /driver/location          { lat, lng, heading, speed, accuracy }
  → 204                                       — fire and forget, batched, UDP-like

POST /rides/{id}/offer/{offerId}/accept   → 200 | 409 (already taken)
POST /rides/{id}/cancel        { reason }  → 200 { cancellationFeeCents }
POST /rides/{id}/complete      { endLocation, distanceM, durationS }

High-level architecture

rider app driver app │ HTTPS │ location every 4s (batched) ▼ ▼ ┌─────────────────────── API gateway ─────────────────────────┐ │ auth · rate limit · route by city_id — every request is regional │ └───────┬──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┘ ▼ ▼ trip service location service state machine, durable in-memory geo index, sharded by cell Postgres/Mongo per region NOT a general database │ │ │ "find drivers near P" │ writes: 250k/s, TTL 30s └──────────────┬───────────────────┘ ▼ dispatch service batched matching window (2-5 s) ├── supply/demand per cell → pricing service (surge) ├── travel time → ETA service (road graph) └── offer → driver → accept/decline/timeout │ ▼ events Kafka ─▶ trip archive · analytics · payments · notifications
Fig. S01.1 — city-scoped services; the location store is deliberately not a database

Deep dive 1 — indexing moving drivers

Rejected · lat/lng B-tree
  • WHERE lat BETWEEN … AND lng BETWEEN … gives a square, then you filter by true distance
  • Two independent ranges: the index can only serve one efficiently
  • 250 k writes/s against a B-tree is hopeless
Rejected · quadtree
  • Adapts beautifully to density — good for static points
  • But rebalancing under constant movement is expensive
  • Harder to shard across machines than a flat cell id
Chosen · hierarchical cell ids (H3 / S2)
  • A location becomes a single 64-bit integer — a plain hash-map key
  • Hexagons (H3) have uniform neighbour distance; a "ring" query is exact
  • Cell id is the natural shard key; a city is a set of cells
  • Resolution is a tunable: coarse cells in sparse areas, fine in dense
javascript · the location store, in memory
// Sharded by cell. Each node owns a contiguous set of cells for one city.
// No durability: a node restart loses positions, and drivers re-report in 4 seconds.
class LocationIndex {
  #byCell = new Map();      // cellId -> Map<driverId, {lat,lng,heading,ts,state}>
  #byDriver = new Map();    // driverId -> cellId, so we can move it out of the old cell

  update(driverId, lat, lng, meta) {
    const cell = h3.latLngToCell(lat, lng, RES_9);   // ~174 m edge
    const prev = this.#byDriver.get(driverId);
    if (prev !== undefined && prev !== cell) this.#byCell.get(prev)?.delete(driverId);

    let bucket = this.#byCell.get(cell);
    if (!bucket) this.#byCell.set(cell, (bucket = new Map()));
    bucket.set(driverId, { lat, lng, ...meta, ts: Date.now() });
    this.#byDriver.set(driverId, cell);
  }

  // "Who is available within ~2 km of this point?"
  nearby(lat, lng, radiusM, { limit = 50 } = {}) {
    const origin = h3.latLngToCell(lat, lng, RES_9);
    const rings = Math.ceil(radiusM / 174);          // hex edge length at res 9
    const out = [];
    // gridDisk expands outward ring by ring — cheap, and no boundary problem,
    // which is the failure mode geohash prefixes have
    for (const cell of h3.gridDisk(origin, rings)) {
      for (const [id, d] of this.#byCell.get(cell) ?? []) {
        if (Date.now() - d.ts > 30_000) continue;    // stale: treat as offline
        if (d.state !== 'available') continue;
        const m = haversine(lat, lng, d.lat, d.lng); // exact distance, cheap at this count
        if (m <= radiusM) out.push({ id, ...d, distanceM: m });
      }
      if (out.length >= limit * 4) break;            // enough candidates, stop expanding
    }
    return out.sort((a, b) => a.distanceM - b.distanceM).slice(0, limit);
  }
}
Why not geohash

Geohash prefixes are convenient because any key-value store can range-scan them, but two points a metre apart either side of a cell boundary share no prefix at all. You must query the eight neighbouring cells and merge — doable, but the neighbour computation is fiddly and the cells are rectangles that distort badly near the poles. H3's gridDisk gives you the ring directly, with uniform distance to every neighbour. If you are constrained to Redis, its GEOADD/GEOSEARCH commands implement the geohash approach for you and are a perfectly reasonable starting point.

Deep dive 2 — batched matching

GREEDY — match each request to its nearest driver, immediately request A at 10:00:00 → takes driver D1 (200 m away) request B at 10:00:01 → D1 gone, takes D2 (900 m away) total wait: 200 m + 900 m BATCHED — collect requests for a 2-5 s window, then solve together A: D1 200 m, D2 400 m assignment that minimises TOTAL wait: B: D1 250 m, D2 900 m A → D2 (400 m), B → D1 (250 m) total wait: 650 m — better for both riders and for driver utilisation The window is a real trade: longer windows produce better assignments and worse perceived latency. 2-5 s is where most systems land, and it is why the ride request returns 202 matching rather than a driver.
javascript · the dispatch loop
// Runs per city, every WINDOW_MS. One process owns a city (leader-elected),
// so the assignment problem is small and never spans regions.
async function dispatchTick(cityId) {
  const requests = await pending.drain(cityId);          // ~10-100 in a busy city
  if (!requests.length) return;

  // Build the cost matrix. Cost is ETA, not straight-line distance — a driver
  // 300 m away across a river is much further than one 800 m away on the same road.
  const candidates = requests.map((r) => locations.nearby(r.pickup.lat, r.pickup.lng, 3_000));
  const etas = await etaService.batch(flatten(requests, candidates));

  const cost = buildMatrix(requests, candidates, etas, {
    // Weighting is a product decision, not a technical one:
    etaWeight: 1.0,
    acceptanceRateWeight: 0.3,   // prefer drivers likely to accept
    idleTimeWeight: 0.2,         // fairness: the driver waiting longest gets priority
  });

  // Hungarian algorithm is O(n³) — fine at n ≈ 100. Above that, use a greedy
  // approximation with local swaps; the quality difference is small.
  const assignment = hungarian(cost);

  for (const [reqIdx, drvIdx] of assignment) {
    const offer = await offers.create({
      rideId: requests[reqIdx].id,
      driverId: candidates[reqIdx][drvIdx].id,
      expiresAt: Date.now() + 15_000,
    });
    push.toDriver(offer);                                 // 15 s to accept
  }
}

// Acceptance is a compare-and-swap: the FIRST accept wins, the rest get 409.
// This is what makes double-dispatch structurally impossible.
async function accept(offerId, driverId) {
  const claimed = await offers.updateOne(
    { _id: offerId, driverId, state: 'offered', expiresAt: { $gt: new Date() } },
    { $set: { state: 'accepted', acceptedAt: new Date() } },
  );
  if (claimed.modifiedCount === 0) throw new Conflict('offer no longer available');

  const trip = await trips.updateOne(
    { _id: offer.rideId, state: 'matching' },             // the trip guard
    { $set: { state: 'matched', driverId, matchedAt: new Date() } },
  );
  if (trip.modifiedCount === 0) throw new Conflict('ride already matched');
  await locations.setState(driverId, 'on_trip');
}

Deep dive 3 — the trip state machine

requested ──▶ matching ──▶ matched ──▶ arriving ──▶ in_progress ──▶ completed │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ └─▶ payment_pending ─▶ paid ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ cancelled no_drivers cancelled cancelled (fee applies after `arriving`) Rules that make it safe: · every transition is a conditional update guarded by the CURRENT state · therefore every transition is idempotent — a retry matches nothing · every state has a TIMEOUT and an owner: matching 60 s → no_drivers arriving 10 min → auto-cancel + fee matched 15 s → re-dispatch in_progress 4 h → flag for review · the trip document is the single source of truth; the app is a cache of it · on app restart, GET /rides/{id} restores everything — the client holds no state
The failure that must never happen

Two trips assigned to one driver. It cannot be prevented by checking availability before assigning — that is a read-then-write race. It is prevented by the offer accept being a conditional update on state: 'offered', and the trip transition being a conditional update on state: 'matching'. Both guards must hold, and both are single-document atomic operations. No distributed lock is required, and none would be more reliable.

Live tracking

The rider's app needs the driver's position, but pushing 250 000 updates per second to riders is neither necessary nor affordable. Three reductions, in order:

  1. Only during an active trip. Positions are pushed only to the one rider on that trip — the fan-out is 1:1, not broadcast.
  2. Throttle to ~1 Hz on the wire, and interpolate client-side between updates. The marker moves smoothly on a 1 Hz feed; it does not need 4 Hz.
  3. Snap to the road server-side before sending. Raw GPS jitters across buildings; a snapped position is both smaller to send and visibly more correct.

Scaling & regional isolation

ComponentPartitioned byBottleneckFix
Location indexH3 cell rangea dense city centre in one shardfiner resolution in dense cells; split the hot cell range
Dispatchcitymatrix size in a huge city at peaksub-divide the city into dispatch zones with overlap at the borders
Trip storecity → regionwrite throughputshard on {cityId, tripId}; archive completed trips out
ETA serviceroad-graph tileCPU on routingcache by (origin cell, dest cell) for 60 s — hit rates are very high
WebSocketsgateway nodeconnections per nodeconnection registry keyed by tripId; see the real-time chapter

City is the isolation unit. Every service is deployed per region, holds only that region's data, and shares nothing across regions except the user account. An outage in one region is invisible in another, and a bad deploy can be rolled out city by city.

Failure modes

FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Location shard diesdrivers in those cells invisibleno durability needed — drivers re-report within 4 s; keep shards small
Driver app offline mid-triprider sees a frozen markerbuffer positions on device, replay on reconnect; show "reconnecting" honestly
GPS drift / tunnelwrong position, wrong faresnap to road; reject implausible jumps; fare from the route, not raw points
Driver declines / times outrider waits longeroffer expiry re-enters the next dispatch window; widen the radius each round
Payment fails after the riderevenue, not serviceride completes regardless; debt on the account, retried out of band
Dispatch leader diesthat city stops matchingshort lease; requests remain queued; a new leader drains the backlog
Surge feedback looprunaway pricingcap multiplier; smooth over a window; require a floor of demand samples
Interview · what they are really testing

Three things. First, whether you reach for a database for the location store — the strong answer identifies that 250 k writes/s of data that is worthless in four seconds does not belong in one. Second, whether you understand that greedy matching is a product mistake, not just a technical one, and can explain the batching window trade. Third, whether double-dispatch prevention is stated as a conditional update rather than "we'd check if the driver is free" — that check is the bug.

S02

Seat booking (BookMyShow-style)

This is the correctness case study. A ride can be re-dispatched and a feed can be stale, but a seat sold twice is a person turned away at the door. Everything here is subordinate to one invariant.

Functional
  • Browse cities, cinemas, films, showtimes
  • View a live seat map for a show
  • Select seats; hold them while paying
  • Pay; receive tickets with a scannable code
  • Cancel within policy; seats return to inventory
Non-functional
  • A seat is never sold twice. Non-negotiable.
  • Seat map loads in under 300 ms
  • Survive a blockbuster on-sale: 100 k concurrent users on one show
  • Hold expires predictably; no seat is stranded
  • Payment ambiguity never results in a lost seat or a double charge
Out of scope
  • Seat-map editing tools for venues
  • Dynamic pricing
  • Food and beverage ordering
  • Recommendation

Back-of-envelope

Bookings
2 M/day~25/s average
Browse : book
100 : 1~2 500 reads/s average
On-sale spike
100 k in 60 sthe number that dictates the design
Seats per show
~200a show is a small, bounded object
Shows live
~500 ka fortnight of listings nationally

Two observations shape everything. First, browse traffic is a hundred times booking traffic and is almost entirely cacheable — so the read path and the write path are different systems. Second, a show has only about two hundred seats, so the contended object is tiny; the whole seat map fits in a single document or a single Redis hash.

API

plaintext
GET  /shows/{id}/seatmap        → { seats: [{id, row, num, class, state}], version }
                                  cached 2-5 s at the edge; state is ADVISORY

POST /shows/{id}/holds          { seatIds[], idempotencyKey }
  → 201 { holdId, expiresAt, totalCents }   — authoritative; this is the real check
  → 409 { conflictingSeatIds[] }            — someone beat you to specific seats

POST /holds/{id}/confirm        { paymentIntentId }
  → 201 { bookingId, tickets[] }
DELETE /holds/{id}              → 204        — explicit release on back-navigation
The seat map is advisory, the hold is authoritative

Trying to make the displayed map perfectly live is both impossible and unnecessary — the user takes twenty seconds to choose. Serve a cached map, accept that it is a few seconds stale, and make the hold the moment of truth. A clean 409 naming exactly which seats were taken, with the map refreshed underneath, is a better experience than a map that flickers as it live-updates.

Architecture

READ PATH (100× the traffic, ~100% cacheable) browser ─▶ CDN ─▶ listings service ─▶ read replicas │ cities, cinemas, films, showtimes: TTL minutes └ seat map: TTL 2-5 s, invalidated on booking WRITE PATH (small, contended, must be exactly right) browser ─▶ waiting room ─▶ booking service ─▶ inventory admission control hold → confirm the seat store (on-sale only) │ ├─▶ payment service (idempotent) └─▶ Kafka ─▶ tickets · email · analytics expiry sweeper — releases holds whose TTL passed, every second
Fig. S02.1 — a cached read plane and a small, strongly consistent write plane

Deep dive — the hold, and why the obvious design is wrong

Rejected · read then write
  • if (seat.state === 'free') seat.state = 'held'
  • Two requests both read free, both write held
  • Fails under exactly the load it needs to survive
  • Adding a transaction helps only if the read is inside it and the isolation level forbids the anomaly
Rejected · distributed lock per show
  • Correct, and it serialises the entire show
  • 100 k users on one show now form a single queue
  • Lock held across a payment redirect is a guaranteed outage
  • A GC pause longer than the TTL breaks the guarantee anyway
Chosen · conditional write + unique constraint
  • The database decides, atomically, with no lock to hold
  • Concurrency is per seat, not per show
  • Idempotent: a retry matches nothing and changes nothing
  • Survives process death — there is no lock to leak
javascript · the whole invariant, in one query
// One document per show holds all ~200 seats. Updates to a single document are
// atomic, so this needs no transaction, no lock and no retry loop.
//
// { _id: showId, version, seats: { "A1": {state:'free'},
//                                  "A2": {state:'held', holdId, expiresAt},
//                                  "A3": {state:'sold', bookingId} } }

async function hold(showId, seatIds, userId, idempotencyKey) {
  const holdId = new ObjectId();
  const now = new Date();
  const expiresAt = new Date(Date.now() + HOLD_MS);   // 8 minutes

  // The filter asserts EVERY requested seat is currently takeable.
  // An expired hold counts as free — that is what the $or expresses — so a
  // crashed sweeper degrades throughput, never correctness.
  const filter = { _id: showId };
  const set = {};
  for (const s of seatIds) {
    filter[`seats.${s}.state`] = { $in: ['free', 'held'] };
    filter.$and ??= [];
    filter.$and.push({
      $or: [
        { [`seats.${s}.state`]: 'free' },
        { [`seats.${s}.expiresAt`]: { $lt: now } },     // someone else's hold lapsed
      ],
    });
    set[`seats.${s}`] = { state: 'held', holdId, userId, expiresAt };
  }

  const res = await shows.updateOne(filter, { $set: set, $inc: { version: 1 } });

  if (res.modifiedCount === 0) {
    // Nothing changed ⇒ at least one seat was unavailable. Tell them WHICH.
    const show = await shows.findOne({ _id: showId }, { projection: { seats: 1 } });
    const taken = seatIds.filter((s) => {
      const seat = show.seats[s];
      return seat.state === 'sold' || (seat.state === 'held' && seat.expiresAt > now);
    });
    throw new Conflict('seats unavailable', { conflictingSeatIds: taken });
  }

  await holds.insertOne({ _id: holdId, showId, seatIds, userId, expiresAt,
                          idempotencyKey, state: 'active' });
  return { holdId, expiresAt };
}
All-or-nothing is a requirement, not a nicety

A family booking four adjacent seats must get four or zero — three seats and an apology is worse than a clean failure. Because all seats live in one document and the filter asserts all of them, the update is atomic across the whole set for free. Had seats been separate documents, this would need a transaction and a rollback path, which is a strong argument for the single-document layout even before considering performance.

Confirm, and the payment ambiguity problem

hold created (8 min) │ ├─ user pays ─▶ provider ─▶ succeeded ─▶ confirm: seats held → sold ✔ │ ├─ user pays ─▶ provider ─▶ timeout / unknown │ ↳ do NOT release the seats │ ↳ do NOT retry the charge │ ↳ POLL the provider for the intent's real state │ ↳ extend the hold while polling │ ├─ user abandons ─▶ hold expires ─▶ sweeper frees the seats │ └─ payment succeeded but confirm crashed ↳ the reconciler finds paid intents with no booking and completes them ↳ this is why the hold outlives the payment window
javascript · confirm is a guarded transition, twice
async function confirm(holdId, paymentIntentId) {
  const held = await holds.findOne({ _id: holdId, state: 'active' });
  if (!held) throw new NotFound('hold', holdId);
  if (held.expiresAt < new Date()) throw new Conflict('hold expired');

  // Verify with the PROVIDER, never with the client. The client saying
  // "payment succeeded" is a claim, not evidence.
  const intent = await payments.retrieve(paymentIntentId);
  if (intent.status !== 'succeeded') throw new Conflict('payment not complete');
  if (intent.metadata.holdId !== holdId) throw new Forbidden('intent mismatch');

  const bookingId = new ObjectId();
  const set = {};
  const filter = { _id: held.showId };
  for (const s of held.seatIds) {
    filter[`seats.${s}.holdId`] = holdId;       // still OUR hold — nobody swept it
    set[`seats.${s}`] = { state: 'sold', bookingId };
  }

  const res = await shows.updateOne(filter, { $set: set, $inc: { version: 1 } });
  if (res.modifiedCount === 0) {
    // Swept between payment and confirm. The money is real; the seats are gone.
    // Refund automatically and say so plainly — never leave this to support.
    await payments.refund(paymentIntentId, { reason: 'seats_released' });
    throw new Conflict('seats were released before payment completed');
  }

  await bookings.insertOne({ _id: bookingId, ...held, paymentIntentId, confirmedAt: new Date() });
  await holds.updateOne({ _id: holdId }, { $set: { state: 'confirmed' } });
  return bookingId;
}
Make the hold outlive the payment

If the hold is eight minutes and the payment redirect can take nine, you have designed the refund path above into the normal case. Size the hold from the p99 of the actual payment flow, extend it when the provider reports the intent is processing, and treat the automatic refund as an exception you monitor rather than a routine outcome.

Deep dive — surviving the on-sale

100 000 users arrive in 60 seconds for a show with 200 seats. 99.8% of them cannot possibly succeed. The design must reflect that. 1 WAITING ROOM admission control at the edge signed token, position in queue, admit N/sec matched to backend capacity ↳ the queue is the product: an honest position beats a spinning failure 2 PRE-WARM seat map in cache before the sale opens; static assets on CDN ↳ never let 100 k cold reads hit the origin at t=0 3 SHED EARLY reject at the edge with a clear message, not at the database ↳ a 429 in 5 ms costs nothing; a timeout at the database costs everything 4 PARTITION THE HOT DOC one show = one document = one write lock at extreme contention, split by seat block: show:A, show:B, show:C ↳ only worth it above ~1 000 writes/s on a single show
javascript · admission control
// A token bucket per show, refilled at the rate the booking service can absorb.
// Users without a token wait in a queue and are told their position honestly.
const ADMIT_LUA = `
  local tokens = tonumber(redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) or ARGV[1])
  if tokens <= 0 then return 0 end
  redis.call('DECR', KEYS[1])
  return 1
`;
// Refilled by a ticker at, say, 200/s — matched to measured backend capacity,
// not guessed. The waiting room turns a stampede into a queue, and a queue
// is something users tolerate when the position is visible and moving.

The expiry sweeper

javascript · lazy expiry plus a background sweep
// Correctness does NOT depend on the sweeper: the hold filter already treats an
// expired hold as free. The sweeper exists so the displayed map is accurate.
setInterval(async () => {
  const expired = await holds.find({ state: 'active', expiresAt: { $lt: new Date() } })
                             .limit(500).toArray();
  for (const h of expired) {
    const filter = { _id: h.showId };
    const set = {};
    for (const s of h.seatIds) {
      filter[`seats.${s}.holdId`] = h._id;      // only free seats STILL held by us —
      set[`seats.${s}`] = { state: 'free' };    // never stomp a seat that got confirmed
    }
    await shows.updateOne(filter, { $set: set, $inc: { version: 1 } });
    await holds.updateOne({ _id: h._id }, { $set: { state: 'expired' } });
    await cache.del(`seatmap:${h.showId}`);
  }
}, 1_000).unref();
The sweeper must be safe to be late, and safe to run twice

Guarding on holdId is what makes both true. If it runs late, the hold filter already treats the seat as available, so nothing is stranded. If it runs concurrently with a confirm, the confirm has already changed holdId to a sold state and the sweeper's filter matches nothing. A sweeper that blindly sets expired seats to free would occasionally erase a paid booking — and would do so rarely enough to reach production.

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Read scaleCDN + replicas; the seat map is the only short-TTL object
Write scaleshard by showId — perfect partitioning, since no operation spans shows
Hot showadmission control first; seat-block partitioning only if measurements demand it
Archivemove shows past their date out of the hot collection nightly
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Payment provider slowholds expire during checkoutextend on processing; poll for the true state; auto-refund with a clear message
Sweeper stoppedmap shows seats as held that are freecorrectness unaffected (lazy expiry); alert on oldest un-swept hold
Cache serves a stale mapuser picks a taken seat409 names the seats; client refreshes and re-selects
Booking service crashes mid-confirmpaid, no bookingreconciler matches succeeded intents to bookings; completes or refunds
Venue changes the seat map latebookings reference dead seatsversion the map; block edits once any seat is sold
Duplicate submittwo holds by one useridempotency key on the hold request
Interview · the answer that scores

“The invariant is that a seat has at most one owner, and I want the database to enforce it rather than my code. I'd keep all seats for a show in one document so a multi-seat hold is a single atomic conditional update — all-or-nothing for free, and concurrency scoped per show rather than globally. The filter treats an expired hold as free, which means the expiry sweeper is a cosmetic optimisation rather than part of the correctness argument. I would not use a distributed lock: it serialises a whole show, it cannot safely span a payment redirect, and it fails silently under a GC pause. For the on-sale spike I'd put a waiting room in front, because 99.8% of that traffic cannot succeed and the honest thing to do is queue it visibly rather than fail it slowly.”

S03

Video streaming (Netflix/YouTube-style)

Two systems wearing one name: an offline pipeline that turns an upload into hundreds of files, and an online path that is almost entirely a caching problem. The interesting engineering is in the first; the money is in the second.

Functional
  • Upload a video, resumably, from an unreliable connection
  • Transcode to an adaptive bitrate ladder
  • Stream with quality that adapts to bandwidth
  • Resume playback across devices
  • Search and browse a catalogue
Non-functional
  • Start-up latency under 2 s to first frame
  • Rebuffer ratio under 0.5% of playback time
  • Survive a synchronised release to millions
  • Bandwidth cost is the dominant operating expense
  • Upload never blocks on transcoding
Out of scope
  • Live streaming (a materially different pipeline)
  • Recommendation model internals
  • DRM cryptography details
  • Content moderation

Back-of-envelope

Users
500 M1 B watch-hours/month
Concurrent peak
~10 Mevening, regionally staggered
Egress at peak
~50 Tbps10 M × 5 Mbps average bitrate
Uploads
500 h/min≈ 8 h of video per second
Storage/hour
~5 GBafter the full ABR ladder

Fifty terabits per second is the number that decides the architecture. No origin serves that. The entire online design exists to ensure that a request almost never reaches your servers — a 99%+ CDN offload is not an optimisation here, it is the only viable operating point.

The upload and transcode pipeline

creator ──▶ resumable upload (pre-signed, chunked) ──▶ object storage ↳ client never touches the API with bytes │ raw master ↳ resumes from the last acked chunk ▼ ingest event │ ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┤ ▼ ▼ ▼ probe segment thumbnails codec, duration split into 2-6 s GOPs sprite sheet resolution, audio │ preview scrub │ ▼ │ ┌───── fan out per rung ─────┐ │ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ │ 240p 480p 720p 1080p 4K │ └────────┴────┬───┴────────┘ │ ▼ │ package HLS + DASH manifests │ ▼ └────────────▶ publish ──▶ CDN pre-position (popular titles) ▼ catalogue: status = ready
Fig. S03.1 — segment first, then transcode segments in parallel
Segment before transcoding, not after

Transcoding a two-hour film as one job takes hours on one machine and cannot be retried cheaply. Splitting into 2–6 second segments first turns it into thousands of independent jobs: they run in parallel across a fleet, a failed segment retries in seconds rather than restarting the film, and progress is measurable. The constraint is that segments must split on keyframe boundaries, which is why the probe step runs first.

javascript · the job graph, with idempotency
// Every job is keyed deterministically, so a retry — or a duplicate delivery —
// recomputes the same output to the same location and is harmless.
const jobId = `${assetId}:${rung.name}:${segmentIndex}`;
const outputKey = `assets/${assetId}/${rung.name}/${segmentIndex}.m4s`;

async function transcodeSegment({ assetId, segmentIndex, rung }) {
  // Cheap idempotency: if the output already exists, this ran before.
  if (await storage.exists(outputKey)) return { skipped: true };

  const src = await storage.get(`assets/${assetId}/segments/${segmentIndex}.mp4`);
  const out = await ffmpeg(src, {
    videoCodec: 'libx264', crf: rung.crf, maxrate: rung.maxrate, bufsize: rung.bufsize,
    scale: rung.height, preset: 'veryfast',
    // Identical GOP structure across every rung — otherwise the player cannot
    // switch bitrate at a segment boundary without a visible stall.
    keyint: SEGMENT_FRAMES, minKeyint: SEGMENT_FRAMES, sceneCut: 0,
  });
  await storage.put(outputKey, out);
  await progress.increment(assetId);          // publish when all rungs × segments done
}

const LADDER = [
  { name: '240p',  height: 240,  maxrate: '400k',  crf: 26 },
  { name: '480p',  height: 480,  maxrate: '1200k', crf: 24 },
  { name: '720p',  height: 720,  maxrate: '2800k', crf: 23 },
  { name: '1080p', height: 1080, maxrate: '5000k', crf: 22 },
  { name: '2160p', height: 2160, maxrate: '16000k', crf: 21 },
];
// 2h film ÷ 4s segments = 1,800 segments × 5 rungs = 9,000 jobs.
// At 200 workers and ~4s per job, the film is ready in about 3 minutes.

Deep dive — adaptive bitrate

The manifest lists every rung. The player chooses — the server never does. #EXTM3U #EXT-X-STREAM-INF:BANDWIDTH=400000,RESOLUTION=426x240 240p/index.m3u8 #EXT-X-STREAM-INF:BANDWIDTH=1200000,RESOLUTION=854x480 480p/index.m3u8 #EXT-X-STREAM-INF:BANDWIDTH=2800000,RESOLUTION=1280x720 720p/index.m3u8 Each variant playlist lists its segments: #EXTINF:4.0, seg00001.m4s #EXTINF:4.0, seg00002.m4s Selection logic (buffer-based, which is what modern players use): buffer < 5 s → step DOWN aggressively, even two rungs avoid a stall at all costs buffer > 20 s → step UP one rung never jump multiple 5-20 s → hold hysteresis stops oscillation Why buffer and not measured throughput: throughput estimates are noisy and lag reality, and a player that chases them oscillates visibly. Buffer level is a direct measurement of whether you are winning or losing.
The first segment decides the impression

Start-up latency dominates perceived quality far more than steady-state resolution. Players therefore request the lowest rung first to fill the buffer fast, then climb. Getting this backwards — starting at 1080p and stalling for four seconds — is the difference between a product that feels instant and one that feels broken, even though the steady state is identical.

Delivery, and why bandwidth is the design

player ──▶ CDN edge (hit: ~99%) ──▶ done │ miss ▼ mid-tier / shield ──▶ collapses many edge misses into one origin fetch │ miss ▼ origin (object storage) Cost arithmetic that drives every decision: 50 Tbps at peak. Origin egress is the expensive tier. 99% offload → 500 Gbps to origin. 95% offload → 2.5 Tbps to origin — 5× the cost for a 4-point hit-rate drop. Hence: · long TTLs (segments are immutable — fingerprinted, never rewritten) · shield tier so a cold edge does not multiply origin fetches · pre-position popular titles BEFORE the release, not on demand · ISP-embedded caches for the largest catalogues
Immutable segments make caching trivial

Because a segment file never changes — a re-encode produces a new asset version with new URLs — every segment can be cached for a year with no invalidation logic anywhere. All the cache complexity collapses into the manifest, which is small, short-TTL and cheap to serve. Designing content to be immutable is what turns a hard caching problem into a solved one.

Playback session

javascript · resume, concurrency limits, and heartbeats
// Resume position: written often, read rarely, tolerant of loss.
// Never a synchronous write on the playback path.
POST /playback/heartbeat  { sessionId, assetId, positionS, rung, bufferS, stalls }
// every 10 s → queue → batched upsert. Losing 10 s of position is invisible;
// blocking playback on a database write is not.

// Concurrent stream limits are a CLAIM, not a count.
async function startSession(userId, assetId, deviceId) {
  const key = `streams:${userId}`;
  const active = await redis.zcount(key, Date.now() - 60_000, '+inf');
  if (active >= plan.maxStreams) throw new Conflict('stream limit reached');
  await redis.zadd(key, Date.now(), deviceId);      // heartbeat refreshes the score
  await redis.expire(key, 300);
  // A sorted set with time-based scores self-heals: a device that crashes
  // simply stops refreshing and ages out. No cleanup job, no stuck sessions.
}

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Synchronised releasepre-position to every edge hours ahead; the first million viewers must all hit warm caches
Transcode backlogpriority queue — a creator's first upload jumps a re-encode; autoscale on queue depth
Catalogue readsheavily cached, denormalised read models; the catalogue is not the video path
Storage growthdrop unused rungs for cold titles; keep the master in cold storage; re-encode on demand
View countsapproximate and batched — never a synchronous increment per play
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Transcode job failsone segment missing → playback stalls at that pointidempotent retry; block publish until every segment exists; verify before marking ready
CDN region outageviewers in that regionmulti-CDN with health-based steering; the player retries the alternate host on failure
Origin overloadrebuffering everywhereshield tier; request collapsing; serve stale manifests rather than erroring
Manifest cached too longnew episodes invisibleshort manifest TTL, long segment TTL — the split that makes this safe
Ladder mismatch across rungsstalls on every quality switchidentical GOP alignment enforced in the encoder settings and verified in QA
Heartbeat pipeline backs upresume positions staledegrade silently — playback must never depend on it
Interview · what separates a strong answer

Naming the two systems separately and giving each its own reasoning. For the pipeline: segment-then-transcode for parallelism and cheap retries, idempotent jobs keyed by output path, and GOP alignment across rungs because otherwise switching stalls. For delivery: recognising that this is a caching-economics problem, that immutable fingerprinted segments make long TTLs free, and that the difference between 95% and 99% offload is a five-fold change in the largest line on the bill. Candidates who spend the whole answer on the database have missed where the system actually lives.

S04

Chat & messaging (WhatsApp/Telegram-style)

Fifty million idle TCP connections and a hundred billion small writes a day. The scale is unusual, but the genuinely hard parts are ordering without trustworthy clocks, delivering to a device that is currently offline, and doing both when the server cannot read the messages.

Functional
  • 1:1 and group messaging, groups up to 100 k
  • Delivery and read receipts; typing indicators
  • Offline delivery and multi-device sync
  • Media attachments
  • End-to-end encryption for 1:1 and small groups
Non-functional
  • Send-to-deliver under 500 ms when both parties are online
  • No message is ever lost once acknowledged
  • Per-conversation ordering is identical for every participant
  • Duplicates are tolerated by the protocol, invisible to the user
  • 50 M concurrent connections
Out of scope
  • Voice and video calls
  • Cryptographic protocol design (Signal protocol assumed)
  • Spam and abuse systems
  • Channels/broadcast at millions of subscribers

Back-of-envelope

Messages
100 B/day~1.2 M/s average
Connections
50 Mat ~40 kB each = 2 TB of RAM across the fleet
Gateway nodes
~1 00050 k connections each
Storage
~10 TB/day100 B × ~100 B/message + metadata
Fan-out peak
hugea 100 k group message is 100 k deliveries

Architecture

device ══ persistent WebSocket ══▶ gateway (stateful: holds N connections) │ │ 1. persist FIRST, then deliver ▼ message service │ ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ message store session registry fan-out append-only user → gateway per recipient device by conversation (Redis, TTL) │ │ ▼ │ online? ─▶ route to that gateway │ offline? ─▶ push notification ▼ (content stays on server) sync cursor per device "give me everything after seq N"
Fig. S04.1 — persist before deliver; the store is the truth, the socket is an optimisation
Persist first, always

The temptation is to deliver over the open socket and write to storage asynchronously — it is faster and the socket is right there. It is also how messages are lost: the gateway dies between the send and the write, the sender saw a tick, and the message never existed. Write to the durable log, acknowledge the sender from that write, and treat delivery over the socket as an optimisation on top of a sync protocol that would have worked anyway.

Deep dive 1 — ordering without trusting clocks

WRONG order by client timestamp Device clocks are wrong by minutes to hours. Two participants see two different orders of the same conversation, and neither is fixable later. WRONG order by server receive time Better, but two servers' clocks differ by milliseconds, and NTP skew means concurrent messages can order differently for different readers. RIGHT a monotonic per-conversation sequence number Every message gets seq = previous + 1, assigned by ONE writer per conversation. Ordering is then total, identical for everyone, and it doubles as the sync cursor. conv:9f3a seq 1 "hey" seq 2 "you there?" seq 3 "yes" ← "give me everything after seq 2" is exact How one writer per conversation, at 1.2 M messages/s? Partition by conversation id. Each partition has a single writer; conversations are independent, so this parallelises perfectly across thousands of partitions.
javascript · send, with client dedup and server ordering
// The client generates the id. This is what makes retries safe: the client
// resends on any uncertainty, and the server recognises it.
async function send({ conversationId, clientMsgId, senderId, ciphertext }) {
  // Single-document atomic increment: allocate the next sequence number.
  const { value: conv } = await conversations.findOneAndUpdate(
    { _id: conversationId, participants: senderId },   // authorisation IS the filter
    { $inc: { lastSeq: 1 }, $set: { lastActivityAt: new Date() } },
    { returnDocument: 'after' },
  );
  if (!conv) throw new Forbidden('not a participant');

  try {
    await messages.insertOne({
      _id: `${conversationId}:${clientMsgId}`,   // unique index = dedup for free
      conversationId,
      seq: conv.lastSeq,
      senderId,
      ciphertext,
      serverTs: new Date(),
    });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === 11000) {
      // A retry of a message we already stored. Return the ORIGINAL — never
      // a second copy with a new sequence number.
      return messages.findOne({ _id: `${conversationId}:${clientMsgId}` });
    }
    throw e;
  }

  await fanOut(conv, seq);      // asynchronous; the sender is already acknowledged
  return { seq: conv.lastSeq };
}
The sequence gap

Incrementing lastSeq and then failing to insert leaves a hole in the sequence. Readers must treat a gap as "not yet visible" rather than "end of conversation", or a single failed insert truncates the conversation for everyone. Either allocate the sequence inside the same atomic write as the message, or make the reader tolerant of gaps with a short grace period before declaring the tail reached.

Deep dive 2 — delivery and offline sync

javascript · route to the right gateway, or fall back to push
// The registry maps a DEVICE (not a user) to the gateway holding its socket.
// A user with a phone, a laptop and a tablet has three independent cursors.
async function fanOut(conv, seq) {
  for (const userId of conv.participants) {
    const devices = await devices.find({ userId, active: true }).toArray();

    for (const d of devices) {
      const gateway = await redis.get(`sess:${d.id}`);       // TTL-refreshed by heartbeat

      if (gateway) {
        // Direct routing beats broadcasting to 1,000 gateways.
        await gatewayBus.publish(gateway, { deviceId: d.id, conversationId: conv._id, seq });
      } else {
        // Offline. The message is already durable; push only wakes the app.
        // Never put content in the push payload — it leaks, and with E2EE the
        // server does not have the plaintext anyway.
        await pushQueue.add('wake', { deviceId: d.id, badge: true });
      }
    }
  }
}

// On reconnect the device does not ask "what did I miss" vaguely — it states
// exactly where it is, per conversation.
async function sync(deviceId, cursors) {
  const out = [];
  for (const { conversationId, afterSeq } of cursors) {
    const batch = await messages
      .find({ conversationId, seq: { $gt: afterSeq } })
      .sort({ seq: 1 }).limit(500).toArray();
    out.push({ conversationId, messages: batch, hasMore: batch.length === 500 });
  }
  return out;
}
Why the registry rather than broadcast

Broadcasting every message to all 1 000 gateways so each can check its local sockets means every gateway processes 1.2 M messages per second — the fan-out cost is multiplied by the fleet size, and adding gateways makes it worse. A registry lookup turns delivery into one targeted publish. The cost is that the registry must be kept accurate: entries expire on a heartbeat TTL, so a crashed gateway's entries age out and those devices fall back to push until they reconnect.

Deep dive 3 — group fan-out

Rejected · copy per recipient
  • One message to a 100 k group = 100 k stored rows
  • Storage multiplied by group size
  • An edit or delete becomes 100 k updates
Chosen · one log, per-member cursor
  • The message is stored once in the conversation log
  • Each member has a cursor: lastReadSeq
  • Delivery is a notification carrying a sequence number, not content
  • Storage is O(messages), not O(messages × members)

The fan-out still costs one notification per online device, but notifications are tiny and can be batched and rate-limited. For very large groups, delivery receipts must be dropped entirely — see below.

Receipts, and their write amplification

In a 1:1 chat, receipts are cheap: 1 message → 2 receipt writes. In a 100 k group, "delivered" and "read" per member per message means 200 000 writes for one message — 200× the cost of the message itself. Therefore, by group size: ≤ 10 members full per-member delivered + read receipts ≤ 500 members read receipts aggregated ("seen by 43") on a 5 s debounce > 500 members no receipts at all — and say so in the UI Typing indicators follow the same rule, and are never persisted: fire-and-forget over pub/sub with a 3 s TTL. Losing one is invisible.

End-to-end encryption, and what it costs the server

javascript · the server stores ciphertext it cannot read
// Each DEVICE has its own key pair. A message to a user with 3 devices is
// encrypted 3 times — once per device — because the server cannot re-encrypt.
{
  _id: 'conv123:cm_88f2',
  conversationId: 'conv123',
  seq: 4211,
  senderId: 'u_9',
  senderDeviceId: 'd_2',
  // one envelope per recipient device
  envelopes: [
    { deviceId: 'd_7',  ciphertext: '...', ephemeralKey: '...' },
    { deviceId: 'd_11', ciphertext: '...', ephemeralKey: '...' },
  ],
  serverTs: ISODate(),
}

// What the server LOSES by not holding the plaintext:
//   · no server-side search — the client indexes locally
//   · no server-side spam or abuse detection on content
//   · no content in push notifications; the client decrypts on wake
//   · no re-encryption for a NEW device: history cannot be handed over,
//     which is why adding a device shows "messages before this point are
//     unavailable" unless the user explicitly transfers an encrypted backup
//   · group fan-out cost scales with DEVICES, not members
E2EE is a product decision with architectural consequences

It is not a feature you add later. Search, multi-device history, backup, moderation and push previews all change shape, and several become impossible server-side. The strong interview answer states the trade explicitly rather than treating encryption as a checkbox: you gain a guarantee that a server compromise does not expose message content, and you give up every server-side capability that requires reading it.

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Message storepartition by conversationId; time-ordered within a partition; archive cold conversations
Gatewaysstateless except for the sockets; scale on connection count; connection draining on deploy
Medianever through the message path — pre-signed upload, message carries a key and a decryption key
Hot grouprate-limit sends per group; drop receipts; batch notifications
Reconnect stormjittered client backoff; connection rate limit at the edge; a deploy must not reconnect 50 M devices at once
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Gateway node dies50 k devices disconnectedregistry entries expire; devices reconnect elsewhere and sync from their cursor — nothing is lost
Duplicate deliverymessage appears twiceclient dedups on clientMsgId; the unique index prevents duplicate storage
Out-of-order arrivaljumbled conversationclient orders by seq, never by arrival or timestamp
Push provider outageoffline users not wokenmessages remain durable; delivered on next foreground; retry with backoff
Sequence gapclient thinks it is caught uptreat a gap as "wait", not "end"; re-request after a grace period
Clock skew—irrelevant by construction: nothing orders by time
Interview · the three questions behind the question

“How do you guarantee ordering?” — a per-conversation monotonic sequence assigned by a single writer per partition, never a timestamp. “How do you avoid duplicates?” — client-generated message ids with a unique index, so at-least-once delivery is safe and the client dedups. “How do you scale WebSockets?” — a session registry mapping device to gateway, so delivery is a targeted publish rather than a fleet-wide broadcast. If you can also say why receipts are dropped above a group-size threshold, you have shown you understand write amplification rather than just the happy path.

S05

Food delivery (Zomato/Swiggy-style)

Three independent parties, none of whom you control, must be coordinated inside a forty-minute window against a perishable product. The state machine is the system; everything else supports it.

Functional
  • Discover restaurants that deliver to my address, with live availability
  • Build a cart from a versioned menu; place and pay for an order
  • Restaurant accepts and prepares; courier collects and delivers
  • Live ETA and tracking throughout
  • Cancellation and partial refunds
Non-functional
  • Search results in under 300 ms
  • Survive a 5× dinner peak lasting ninety minutes
  • An order is never silently lost between the three parties
  • A courier is never double-assigned
  • Money movement is exactly once
Out of scope
  • Restaurant point-of-sale integration
  • Courier payroll and gig compliance
  • Menu photography and content
  • Loyalty programmes

Back-of-envelope

Orders
5 M/day~58/s average
Dinner peak
~300/stwo 90-minute windows carry most of the day
Restaurants
500 kavailability changes constantly
Couriers
200 k activelocation every 5 s at peak
Search : order
50 : 1~3 000 searches/s at peak

Traffic is not merely peaky, it is predictably peaky — the same two windows every day, in every time zone. That means scheduled scaling rather than reactive autoscaling, and it means capacity planning is a solved problem if anyone bothers to look at yesterday's graph.

Architecture

customer restaurant tablet courier app │ │ │ ▼ ▼ ▼ ┌──────────────────── API gateway ─────────────────────┐ └──┬─────────────┬──────────────┬───────────────┬─────────┘ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ discovery menu order dispatch geo + rank versioned state machine batching + assignment cached 60 s immutable (the core) │ │ snapshots │ ├─▶ location (in-memory geo) │ │ └─▶ ETA (prep + travel) ▼ ▼ search index Kafka ─▶ payments · notifications · analytics (availability │ as a filter) └─▶ timeout watchdog — every state has a deadline
Fig. S05.1 — the order state machine is the spine; other services feed it

Deep dive 1 — the order state machine

created ─▶ paid ─▶ placed ─▶ accepted ─▶ preparing ─▶ ready ─▶ picked_up ─▶ delivered │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ └─ 60 min → alert │ │ │ └─ courier assigned by now │ │ └─ prep ETA drives courier dispatch timing │ └─ 120 s timeout → auto-cancel + full refund └─ restaurant offline / closed → reject before charging Cancellation windows differ by state, and that IS the business logic: before `accepted` → free cancellation, full refund `accepted`..`ready` → partial charge; the food is being made after `picked_up` → no refund by default; support override Every transition: · a conditional update guarded by the current state (idempotent by construction) · emits an event (the audit trail, and what drives notifications) · has a DEADLINE with an owner and an automatic action on expiry
javascript · transitions and the watchdog
const TRANSITIONS = {
  placed:    { next: ['accepted', 'rejected'], timeoutMs: 120_000, onTimeout: 'autoReject' },
  accepted:  { next: ['preparing', 'cancelled'], timeoutMs: 60_000, onTimeout: 'nudge' },
  preparing: { next: ['ready'], timeoutMs: 45 * 60_000, onTimeout: 'escalate' },
  ready:     { next: ['picked_up'], timeoutMs: 15 * 60_000, onTimeout: 'redispatch' },
  picked_up: { next: ['delivered', 'failed'], timeoutMs: 60 * 60_000, onTimeout: 'alert' },
};

async function transition(orderId, to, actor, meta = {}) {
