  const from = Object.keys(TRANSITIONS).filter((s) => TRANSITIONS[s].next.includes(to));
  if (!from.length) throw new BadRequest(`no transition into ${to}`);

  // The guard is the concurrency control. A duplicate tap, a retried webhook
  // and a racing watchdog all resolve to "matched nothing", which is success.
  const res = await orders.findOneAndUpdate(
    { _id: orderId, state: { $in: from } },
    {
      $set: { state: to, [`timestamps.${to}`]: new Date(), ...meta },
      $push: { history: { from: '$state', to, actor, at: new Date() } },
    },
    { returnDocument: 'after' },
  );

  if (!res) {
    const current = await orders.findOne({ _id: orderId }, { projection: { state: 1 } });
    if (!current) throw new NotFound('order', orderId);
    if (current.state === to) return current;          // already there — a retry
    throw new Conflict(`cannot go ${current.state} → ${to}`);
  }

  await outbox.insertOne({ type: `order.${to}`, payload: { orderId } });
  await watchdog.schedule(orderId, to, TRANSITIONS[to]?.timeoutMs);
  return res;
}
Every state needs a deadline and an owner

The failure that defines this domain is an order sitting in placed because a restaurant tablet is in a drawer. Without a timeout the customer waits indefinitely, discovers at 9pm that no food is coming, and never returns. A 120-second auto-reject with an immediate full refund and an apology is a far better outcome than a silent stall — and it is only possible if every state has an explicit deadline, not just the ones someone remembered.

Deep dive 2 — dispatch and batching

Dispatch is not "find the nearest courier". It is a scheduling problem with a time dimension the ride-hailing version does not have. A courier assigned too early idles at the restaurant. A courier assigned too late means cold food and a late delivery. target pickup time = order.acceptedAt + prepEstimate − travelToRestaurant So dispatch runs on a rolling window, considering orders whose predicted ready time falls inside the courier's arrival horizon. BATCHING — one courier, two orders, if and only if: · both restaurants within ~500 m of each other · both drop-offs within ~1 km of each other · the detour adds < 6 minutes to the FIRST order · neither order is already late ↳ raises courier earnings per hour and cuts cost per delivery ~30% ↳ but a bad batch makes both customers late, so the constraints are hard limits
javascript · assignment with a time-aware cost
function assignmentCost(order, courier, now) {
  const toRestaurant = eta(courier.position, order.restaurant.location);
  const readyAt = order.timestamps.accepted.getTime() + order.prepEstimateMs;
  const arriveAt = now + toRestaurant;

  // Waiting at the restaurant is wasted courier time; arriving late is worse.
  const idleMs = Math.max(0, readyAt - arriveAt);
  const lateMs = Math.max(0, arriveAt - readyAt);

  return (
    toRestaurant * 1.0 +
    idleMs * 0.4 +                      // idling is bad
    lateMs * 2.5 +                      // being late is much worse
    courier.idleSinceMs * -0.3 +        // fairness: longest-waiting courier first
    (courier.batchedOrders * 5 * 60_000) // discourage over-batching
  );
}

// Same shape as ride-hailing: build the matrix over a short window, solve,
// then offer with an expiry. Acceptance is a conditional update, so a
// courier can never end up on two orders.
async function acceptAssignment(assignmentId, courierId) {
  const claimed = await assignments.updateOne(
    { _id: assignmentId, courierId, state: 'offered', expiresAt: { $gt: new Date() } },
    { $set: { state: 'accepted' } },
  );
  if (claimed.modifiedCount === 0) throw new Conflict('assignment no longer available');
  await couriers.updateOne({ _id: courierId }, { $inc: { activeOrders: 1 } });
}

Deep dive 3 — menu versioning

The bug that costs real money

A restaurant raises a price at 19:05. A customer has a cart built at 19:02. If the order reprices at checkout, the customer is charged more than they agreed; if it does not, the restaurant is paid less than they now charge. Both are real disputes with real support cost.

javascript · immutable menu snapshots
// Menus are never edited in place. An edit publishes a NEW version; the old
// one remains readable forever, because orders reference it.
{ _id: 'menu_v_88213', restaurantId, version: 41, publishedAt, items: [...] }

// The cart pins the version it was built from.
{ _id: cartId, restaurantId, menuVersionId: 'menu_v_88213', lines: [...] }

// At checkout, price from the PINNED version, then check for drift.
async function priceCart(cart) {
  const menu = await menus.findOne({ _id: cart.menuVersionId });   // always resolvable
  const priced = computeTotals(cart, menu);

  const current = await menus.findOne({ restaurantId: cart.restaurantId, isCurrent: true });
  if (current._id !== cart.menuVersionId) {
    const changed = diffAffecting(cart, menu, current);
    if (changed.length) {
      // Do not silently reprice. Show the customer exactly what changed
      // and make them confirm — this converts a dispute into a decision.
      throw new Conflict('menu changed', { changed, newTotalCents: computeTotals(cart, current).total });
    }
  }
  return priced;      // unaffected changes (a different dish) are ignored
}

ETA, and why it is three predictions

customer-facing ETA = prep time + courier travel to restaurant + travel to customer (restaurant-specific, (road graph + (road graph + time-of-day, item mix) live traffic) drop-off difficulty) Each is a separate model with its own error, and they compound. Two rules that matter more than model quality: 1. Quote a range, not a point. "25-35 min" is honest and almost always right; "28 min" is precise and frequently wrong, which reads as a lie. 2. Never let the ETA go backwards. A quote that moves from 30 to 45 minutes destroys trust more than an initially pessimistic 40 would have. Pad the initial quote; tighten it as certainty increases.

Peak handling and failure

PressureLeverEffect
More demand than couriersextend quoted ETAsself-limiting: some customers do not order
Still oversubscribedhide distant restaurantsshrinks the delivery radius, raises courier throughput
Restaurant at capacitymark it busy; extend its prep estimateprotects the restaurant from an unservable queue
Severesurge feeattracts couriers, damps demand — use last, it is unpopular
Extremestop accepting in that zonean honest "unavailable" beats a two-hour delivery
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Restaurant never acceptsone order stalls120 s auto-reject, full refund, notify, suppress the restaurant from search
Restaurant rejects after paymentcustomer charged for nothingrefund is automatic and immediate; never require a support ticket
Courier cancels mid-tripfood is at the restaurantre-dispatch with a priority boost; compensate the restaurant if it goes cold
Courier app offlinetracking frozenbuffer positions locally; show "last seen"; the order state is server-side
Payment captured, order rejectedmoney heldauthorise at placement, capture at accepted — the ordering matters
Dispatch service downno assignments in that cityorders queue in ready; extend ETAs; manual dispatch fallback
Missing item on deliverypartial dissatisfactionpartial refund flow with photo evidence; charge back to the restaurant
Interview · what makes this different from ride-hailing

Three things worth naming. It is a three-sided marketplace, so the state machine spans parties you do not control and every state needs a timeout with an automatic action. Dispatch has a time dimension — the courier should arrive as the food is ready, not as soon as possible — which turns a nearest-neighbour problem into a scheduling one. And the menu must be versioned and pinned to the cart, because repricing a customer mid-checkout is a dispute rather than a bug. Candidates who describe it as "Uber for food" usually miss all three.

S06

Social feed (Twitter/X-style)

One decision dominates: build each timeline when someone posts, or when someone reads. The arithmetic says neither works alone, and the interesting part is the threshold where you switch between them.

Functional
  • Post text and media; follow and unfollow
  • Home timeline of posts from followed accounts
  • Likes, reposts, replies with counts
  • Profile timelines; search and trending
  • Block and mute, honoured everywhere
Non-functional
  • Timeline p99 under 200 ms
  • New post visible to followers within ~5 s
  • Reads outnumber writes 100 : 1
  • Accounts with 10 M+ followers must not degrade the system
  • A deleted or blocked post never appears
Out of scope
  • Ranking model training
  • Direct messages (see the chat case study)
  • Advertising and monetisation
  • Content moderation policy

Back-of-envelope

Users
500 M200 M daily active
Posts
500 M/day~6 000/s average, ~20 000/s peak
Timeline reads
~600 k/s200 M × 20 loads ÷ 86 400, ×2 peak
Follow edges
~50 Bmedian 150; tail in the millions
Fan-out writes
~900 k/s6 000 × 150 median, celebrities excluded

The decision, with the numbers

PUSH (fan-out on write) — precompute every follower's timeline read O(1) one indexed range scan ✔ write O(followers) 6 000 posts/s × 150 = 900 k writes/s ✔ tolerable one celebrity post = 10 M writes ✘ PULL (fan-out on read) — query followees at read time write O(1) ✔ read O(following) 600 k reads/s × 150 lookups = 90 M/s ✘ HYBRID followers < 10 000 → PUSH (99.9% of accounts, 900 k writes/s) followers ≥ 10 000 → PULL (a few thousand accounts) read = precomputed list ⊕ small pull for followed large accounts Why 10 000: above it, the fan-out cost exceeds the read cost it saves, because the overwhelming majority of those followers are not online and will never read the row you just wrote for them.

Data model

javascript · four collections, and the indexes that matter
// posts — source of truth. Immutable except deletedAt.
{ _id: SnowflakeId, authorId, text, mediaIds, replyTo, repostOf, createdAt, deletedAt }
// index: { authorId: 1, _id: -1 }        ← profile timeline AND the pull path
// Snowflake ids are time-sortable, so _id alone gives chronological order:
// no secondary sort, and the cursor is just an id.

// edges — both directions are different queries, so both are indexed
{ followerId, followeeId, createdAt }
// index: { followerId: 1, followeeId: 1 } unique   ← "who do I follow"
// index: { followeeId: 1, followerId: 1 }          ← "who follows X" (fan-out)

// accounts — the celebrity flag, denormalised so the read path never counts
{ _id, handle, followerCount, isLarge }
// isLarge maintained by a job when followerCount crosses the threshold

// timeline — precomputed, IDS ONLY, capped
{ _id: { userId, postId }, authorId }
// index: { '_id.userId': 1, '_id.postId': -1 }
// shards perfectly on _id.userId: a read touches exactly one user's rows
Snowflake ids remove a whole class of problem

A time-sortable 64-bit id means the timeline needs no createdAt index, the cursor is a single value rather than a compound tuple, and pagination is a plain $lt on the id. It also makes the id generation itself coordination-free — each node has its own worker id and never talks to anyone. Choosing this at the start saves a great deal of pagination complexity later.

Write path

javascript · post returns immediately; fan-out is somebody else's problem
async function createPost(authorId, input) {
  const post = { _id: snowflake.next(), authorId, ...input, createdAt: new Date(), deletedAt: null };

  await session.withTransaction(async () => {
    await posts.insertOne(post, { session });
    await outbox.insertOne({ type: 'post.created', payload: { postId: post._id, authorId } }, { session });
  });
  return post;        // p99 of the write path is one insert, regardless of followers
}

// ---- fan-out worker ----
async function fanOut({ postId, authorId }) {
  const account = await accounts.findOne({ _id: authorId }, { projection: { isLarge: 1 } });
  if (account.isLarge) return;                      // celebrities are pulled, not pushed

  const cursor = edges.find({ followeeId: authorId }, { projection: { followerId: 1 } })
                      .batchSize(1_000);
  let batch = [];
  for await (const e of cursor) {
    batch.push({ insertOne: { document: { _id: { userId: e.followerId, postId }, authorId } } });
    if (batch.length === 1_000) { await flush(batch); batch = []; }
  }
  if (batch.length) await flush(batch);
}

async function flush(ops) {
  await timeline.bulkWrite(ops, { ordered: false })
    .catch((e) => { if (!e.writeErrors?.every((w) => w.code === 11000)) throw e; });
  await rateLimiter.consume(ops.length);            // global cap on fan-out write rate
}
Rate-limit the fan-out, or it becomes a self-inflicted outage

An account with 9 999 followers — just under the threshold — posting ten times a minute generates 100 000 writes. A dozen such accounts at once and fan-out is issuing over a million writes a minute against the same cluster that serves reads, and the timeline slows for everyone because a few people were prolific. Give fan-out its own connection pool, cap its global write rate, and let the queue absorb the backlog. A timeline arriving three seconds late is invisible; a degraded cluster is not.

Read path — merge push and pull

javascript · the assembly
async function homeTimeline(userId, { cursor, limit = 25 }) {
  const before = cursor ?? snowflake.max();

  const [pushed, largeFollows] = await Promise.all([
    timeline.find({ '_id.userId': userId, '_id.postId': { $lt: before } })
            .sort({ '_id.postId': -1 }).limit(limit + 10).toArray(),
    largeFollowsCache.get(userId),                  // small, changes rarely, cached
  ]);

  // The pulled slice is IDENTICAL for every follower of that account, so it
  // caches extremely well — one query serves millions of readers.
  const pulled = largeFollows.length
    ? await celebritySliceCache.get(largeFollows, before, limit)
    : [];

  const merged = [...pushed.map((t) => t._id.postId), ...pulled]
    .sort((a, b) => (b > a ? 1 : -1))
    .slice(0, limit + 10);

  // Hydrate bodies in ONE batched, cache-first read
  const bodies = await postCache.mget(merged);

  // Filter at READ time — not at fan-out time
  const visible = merged
    .map((id) => bodies.get(id))
    .filter((p) => p && !p.deletedAt && !blocked.has(p.authorId) && !muted.has(p.authorId))
    .slice(0, limit);

  return { data: visible, nextCursor: visible.at(-1)?._id ?? null };
}
Why filtering happens at read, not at fan-out

Deletes, blocks, mutes and moderation removals all happen after the id has been copied into millions of timelines. Chasing those copies is an O(followers) cleanup that races every reader and can never be complete. Filtering during hydration is O(page size), always correct, and needs no cleanup at all — the row simply resolves to nothing. Over-fetch by a small margin so filtering does not return a short page, and reclaim the dead rows lazily in the background.

Counters

javascript · sharded, approximate, reconciled
// A viral post takes 50 k likes/second. One document, one lock, one bottleneck.
const SHARDS = 64;
await counters.updateOne(
  { _id: `${postId}:${randomInt(SHARDS)}` },
  { $inc: { likes: 1 } },
  { upsert: true },
);

// Read: sum the shards once, cache for a few seconds. Nobody needs a like
// count exact to the millisecond, and pretending otherwise builds a bottleneck
// into the most-read number on the page.
const total = await cache.getOrSet(`lc:${postId}`, 5, async () => {
  const [r] = await counters.aggregate([
    { $match: { _id: { $gte: `${postId}:`, $lt: `${postId};` } } },
    { $group: { _id: null, n: { $sum: '$likes' } } },
  ]).toArray();
  return r?.n ?? 0;
});
// Exactness comes from a nightly reconciliation against the likes collection,
// which has a unique index on (postId, userId) and is the actual record.

Storage, and the optimisation that halves it

plaintext
NAIVE
  500 M users × 800 timeline rows × ~40 B = 16 TB
  and most of it is for users who have not opened the app in a year.

ACTIVE-ONLY
  fan out to users seen in the last 30 days: ~200 M
  200 M × 800 × 40 B = 6.4 TB       ← 60% saved, no user-visible change
  a dormant user's timeline is built on demand at next login (one pull, ~1 s)

POSTS
  500 M/day × ~300 B = 150 GB/day, ~55 TB/year
  hot: last 7 days in cache and on fast storage; older tiers out

CACHE
  post bodies for the last 24 h: 500 M × 300 B ≈ 150 GB
  hit rate is very high because feeds concentrate on recent, popular posts
Do not materialise timelines for dormant users

This is the single largest saving available in the design, and it costs nothing perceptible. Most registered accounts are inactive; writing rows they will never read is pure waste. Fan out only to recently-active users, and build a returning user's timeline on demand at login — a one-off pull that takes a second, after which they are back on the fast path.

Scaling and failure

Breaks firstSymptomFix
Fan-out backlogtimelines minutes behindrate-limit and bulkhead the worker; lower the celebrity threshold
Timeline collection sizestorage and index costcap rows per user; active-only fan-out; TTL dormant timelines
Hot post readsorigin load spike on a viral postcache bodies by id — the hit rate is highest for exactly the posts causing load
Counter contentionwrite latency on popular postsshard counters, cache totals, reconcile nightly
Celebrity merge costslow feeds for heavy followerscache the pulled slice per author — it is identical for all their followers
Single-cluster writesglobal write ceilingshard timeline on _id.userId; posts on _id
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Fan-out worker diesnew posts not in timelinesoutbox replays; posts are durable; only visibility is delayed
Deleted post still in timelinesghost entriesread-time filter makes it invisible immediately; rows reclaimed lazily
Cache cold after deployorigin read spikestaggered rollout; request collapsing; serve stale on miss
Account crosses the thresholdfan-out spike then stopsflip isLarge ahead of the boundary with hysteresis to avoid flapping
Unfollow after fan-outstale posts remainfilter at read against the current follow set for recent entries
Interview · the answer that scores

“Push for the 99.9% of accounts below roughly ten thousand followers, because reads outnumber writes a hundred to one and a precomputed timeline turns the read into a single index scan. Pull for the few thousand accounts above it, because fanning out to ten million mostly-inactive followers costs far more than it saves. Merge the two at read time and filter deletes, blocks and mutes during hydration rather than chasing copies through millions of rows. The two things I would insist on beyond that are rate-limiting the fan-out worker so an enthusiastic near-threshold account cannot degrade the cluster, and only fanning out to recently-active users, which is the largest storage saving available and is invisible to users.”

S07

File sync & share (Dropbox/Drive-style)

The blobs are the easy part — object storage solves that. The system is a metadata problem: representing a mutable tree, shared by several people, edited concurrently on devices that are frequently offline.

Functional
  • Sync a folder across a user's devices
  • Upload only what changed, not the whole file
  • Share a folder or file with others, with permissions
  • Version history and restore; trash with recovery
  • Work offline; reconcile on reconnect
Non-functional
  • A change propagates to other devices within seconds
  • Never lose a user's edit — a conflict is acceptable, silent loss is not
  • Sync resumes correctly after any interruption
  • Bandwidth proportional to the change, not the file
  • 10 M devices syncing continuously
Out of scope
  • Real-time collaborative editing (a different problem — see CRDTs)
  • Document preview rendering
  • Full-text search of contents
  • Enterprise compliance and eDiscovery

Back-of-envelope

Users
100 M10 M devices online at once
Files
~1 Baverage 1 MB, heavily skewed small
Blob storage
~1 PBbefore deduplication
Metadata ops
~100 k/sthe real workload
Dedup saving
30–50%shared documents, repeated installers

Note the asymmetry: a petabyte of blobs is a solved problem you can buy, while a hundred thousand metadata operations per second against a mutable tree is where all the engineering goes. Design the metadata service first and treat blob storage as a commodity.

Architecture

device ──▶ metadata service ──▶ metadata store (sharded by namespace) │ │ file tree, versions, block lists, ACLs, CURSOR │ │ │ └──▶ notify ──▶ other devices ("namespace 9f3a changed") │ ↳ they then PULL the delta by cursor │ └──▶ block service ──▶ object storage (content-addressed by hash) ↳ "do you already have these hashes?" → upload only the misses Two independent planes. The metadata plane is small, transactional and chatty. The block plane is large, immutable and dumb. They fail separately, scale separately, and only the metadata plane needs to be consistent.
Fig. S07.1 — metadata is the system; blocks are storage

Deep dive 1 — chunking and deduplication

Rejected · fixed-size chunks
  • Split every 4 MB
  • Insert one byte at the start and every subsequent chunk shifts
  • A one-byte edit re-uploads the whole file
  • Fine for append-only logs, wrong for documents
Chosen · content-defined chunking
  • A rolling hash over a sliding window; cut where the hash matches a pattern
  • Boundaries follow content, so an insertion shifts only the local chunk
  • A one-byte edit re-uploads one chunk, typically ~4 MB → ~0.004% of a 100 GB file
  • Also the basis of cross-file dedup: identical content chunks the same way
javascript · the upload protocol
// Client side: chunk, hash, ask, upload only the misses.
const chunks = contentDefinedChunks(fileBuffer, { min: 2 << 20, avg: 4 << 20, max: 8 << 20 });
const hashes = chunks.map((c) => sha256(c));

// One round trip decides how much we actually have to send.
const { missing } = await api.post('/blocks/check', { hashes });
// Typical edit: 400 chunks, 1 missing. We upload 4 MB instead of 1.6 GB.

await Promise.all(missing.map((h) => api.put(`/blocks/${h}`, chunks[hashes.indexOf(h)])));

// Commit is a METADATA operation. The blocks already exist; this makes them a file.
await api.post('/files/commit', {
  path: '/work/report.pdf',
  blockList: hashes,              // ordered — this IS the file
  parentRev: currentRev,          // optimistic concurrency, see below
  size, mtime,
});
Global dedup leaks information

If "do you have this hash?" is answered across all users, an attacker who guesses a file's contents can confirm whether anyone on the platform holds it — and can do so for a sensitive document by trying candidate versions. Scope dedup per user or per team, or add a per-namespace salt to the hash. The storage saving from global dedup is real, but so is the confirmation oracle.

Deep dive 2 — the sync protocol

Every namespace (a user's root, or a shared folder) has a monotonically increasing cursor. Every metadata change increments it. namespace 9f3a cursor 4108 /work/report.pdf rev 12 cursor 4109 /work/notes.md rev 3 (deleted) cursor 4110 /photos/ created A device stores only its last cursor. Sync is then one question: GET /delta?namespace=9f3a&cursor=4108 → { entries: [...], cursor: 4110, hasMore: false } Properties that make this correct: · resumable — interrupted at any point, resume from the last cursor · idempotent — replaying a delta is harmless; entries are absolute state · compact — a device offline for a month gets only what changed · no diffing — the client never has to compare trees Notification is just a nudge: "namespace 9f3a moved". It carries no content, so it can be lossy, unordered and duplicated without harming correctness — the device pulls the authoritative delta anyway.
javascript · delta, and why entries are absolute
// Each entry describes the CURRENT state of a path, not an operation.
// "created/renamed/modified" would require the client to have seen every
// prior entry in order; absolute state does not.
{
  path: '/work/report.pdf',
  rev: 12,
  type: 'file',              // or 'folder', or 'deleted'
  blockList: ['a3f...', ...],
  size: 1_048_576,
  modifiedBy: 'u_88',
  serverModified: '2026-04-11T10:14:02Z',
}

// A rename appears as a delete of the old path plus a file at the new path.
// Slightly more entries, dramatically simpler client logic — and immune to
// a client that missed an intermediate state.

Deep dive 3 — conflicts

javascript · optimistic concurrency, and what happens when it fails
async function commit({ namespaceId, path, blockList, parentRev, deviceId, userId }) {
  const res = await files.findOneAndUpdate(
    { namespaceId, path, rev: parentRev },        // the rev IS the lock
    {
      $set: { blockList, size, modifiedBy: userId, serverModified: new Date() },
      $inc: { rev: 1 },
    },
    { returnDocument: 'after' },
  );

  if (res) { await bumpCursor(namespaceId, path); return res; }

  const current = await files.findOne({ namespaceId, path });
  if (!current) throw new Conflict('file was deleted');

  // Divergence. We do NOT merge and we do NOT overwrite.
  // Both versions are preserved and the user decides.
  const conflictPath = conflictName(path, deviceName(deviceId));
  //   /work/report.pdf → /work/report (Ada's conflicted copy 2026-04-11).pdf
  const copy = await files.insertOne({
    namespaceId, path: conflictPath, blockList, rev: 1,
    modifiedBy: userId, serverModified: new Date(), conflictOf: path,
  });
  await bumpCursor(namespaceId, conflictPath);
  return { conflict: true, path: conflictPath, serverRev: current.rev };
}
Never merge a binary file, and never silently pick a winner

Last-write-wins on a 200 MB video project discards someone's afternoon with no trace and no notification — and because it is silent, nobody discovers it until much later. A conflicted copy is ugly, visible and completely safe: both versions exist, the filename explains what happened, and the user resolves it with full information. Automatic merging is only appropriate for formats you genuinely understand, and even then only when the merge is provably lossless.

Sharing and permissions

javascript · namespaces make permission checks O(1)
// A shared folder becomes its OWN namespace, mounted into each member's tree.
// This is the key modelling decision: it means a permission check never walks
// a path upward looking for an inherited ACL.
{ _id: 'ns_root_u88',  ownerId: 'u_88', type: 'root' }
{ _id: 'ns_proj_4a2',  type: 'shared', name: 'Project X' }

{ userId: 'u_88', namespaceId: 'ns_proj_4a2', role: 'editor', mountPath: '/Project X' }
{ userId: 'u_91', namespaceId: 'ns_proj_4a2', role: 'viewer', mountPath: '/Shared/Project X' }

// Authorisation is a single membership lookup, cached, regardless of tree depth.
// Sync also gets simpler: a device subscribes to a SET of namespaces and
// tracks one cursor per namespace, entirely independently.

// Link sharing is a scoped, revocable, expiring token — never a guessable path.
{ token: 'sha256(...)', namespaceId, path, role: 'viewer',
  expiresAt, passwordHash, maxDownloads, downloads }

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Metadata scaleshard by namespaceId — no operation spans namespaces, so it partitions cleanly
Notification fan-outa device subscribes per namespace; a shared folder with 500 members is 500 nudges, not 500 deltas
Large folderspaginate the delta; never return 100 k entries in one response
Block storagecontent-addressed and immutable, so caching and replication are trivial; reference-count for deletion
Version historyblocks are shared across versions — history costs only the changed chunks
LAN syncpeers exchange blocks directly after checking hashes with the server; large saving in offices
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Upload interruptedpartial blocksblocks are independent and content-addressed; resume uploads only the missing ones
Commit crashes after block uploadorphaned blocksharmless — a reference-count sweeper reclaims unreferenced blocks after a grace period
Client clock wrongbad orderingserver assigns rev and cursor; client time is metadata only
Rename storm (folder move)thousands of delta entriesrepresent a folder move as one namespace-level entry where possible; paginate otherwise
Two devices edit offlinedivergenceconflicted copy — both preserved, user resolves
Corrupt local stateone device wrongcursor reset to 0 forces a full re-sync; verify by block hash, not by mtime
Accidental mass deleteuser datatrash with retention, plus a "many files deleted" prompt before it propagates
Interview · the three things being tested

Whether you identify that this is a metadata problem rather than a storage problem. Whether you reach for content-defined chunking and can say why fixed chunks fail — the insertion shift is the whole argument. And whether your conflict story is "conflicted copy" rather than "last write wins", because silent data loss is the one outcome this product cannot have. The strong answer also mentions the cursor: one monotonic number per namespace makes sync resumable, idempotent and compact, and removes any need for the client to diff trees.

S08

E-commerce & inventory (Amazon-style)

A read-heavy catalogue bolted to a strongly-consistent inventory count, joined by a checkout that spans five services and must never take money without reserving stock. The inventory decrement is the whole problem.

Functional
  • Browse and search a catalogue of 100 M products
  • Cart that survives login and device changes
  • Checkout: price, apply promotions, reserve stock, take payment, create order
  • Order tracking and fulfilment status
  • Returns and refunds
Non-functional
  • Product page under 200 ms; search under 300 ms
  • Never sell stock that does not exist — or have a deliberate policy if you do
  • Never take money without a reservation
  • Survive a flash sale: 500 k users, one product, sixty seconds
  • Price shown is the price charged
Out of scope
  • Warehouse robotics and logistics
  • Seller onboarding for a marketplace
  • Recommendation model internals
  • Tax calculation rules by jurisdiction

Back-of-envelope

Products
100 M~2 TB of catalogue data
Orders
10 M/day~115/s average, ~1 000/s peak
Browse : buy
200 : 1~200 k page views/s at peak
Flash sale
500 k/60 s~8 000/s on ONE inventory row
Catalogue reads
~99% cacheableproduct data changes rarely

Architecture

READ PLANE (200× the traffic, almost entirely cacheable) browser ─▶ CDN ─▶ catalogue ─▶ read models (denormalised, per-locale) search ─▶ inverted index (Elasticsearch) ↳ stock shown here is ADVISORY: "In stock" / "Only 3 left" WRITE PLANE (small, transactional, must be exactly right) ─▶ cart ─▶ checkout orchestrator (saga) │ ┌──────────────┼───────────────┬──────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ▼ ▼ pricing inventory payment order promotions RESERVE authorise create authoritative (the hard part) then capture then fulfil │ │ │ │ └──────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────┘ │ compensations run in reverse on failure ▼ Kafka ─▶ fulfilment · email · analytics · ledger
Fig. S08.1 — advisory stock on the read plane, authoritative reservation on the write plane

Deep dive 1 — inventory without overselling

Rejected · read then decrement
  • if (stock > 0) stock--
  • Two requests read 1, both decrement, stock is −1
  • Fails precisely under flash-sale load
Rejected · lock the row
  • Correct, and serialises 8 000 requests/s onto one row
  • Held across a payment call is an outage
  • Lock contention becomes the bottleneck, not the stock
Chosen · conditional decrement + reservation
  • One atomic conditional update — the database arbitrates
  • Reservation has a TTL, so an abandoned checkout self-heals
  • Idempotent via a reservation id
  • No lock to leak, no transaction to hold open
javascript · reserve, confirm, release
// { _id: sku, available: 412, reserved: 18, version: 9931 }

async function reserve({ sku, qty, reservationId, ttlMs = 15 * 60_000 }) {
  // Idempotent: a retry with the same id returns the existing reservation
  // rather than reserving a second time.
  const existing = await reservations.findOne({ _id: reservationId });
  if (existing) return existing;

  // The filter IS the check. Nothing can interleave between test and decrement.
  const res = await inventory.updateOne(
    { _id: sku, available: { $gte: qty } },
    { $inc: { available: -qty, reserved: qty }, $set: { updatedAt: new Date() } },
  );
  if (res.modifiedCount === 0) throw new Conflict('insufficient stock', { sku });

  await reservations.insertOne({
    _id: reservationId, sku, qty, state: 'held',
    expiresAt: new Date(Date.now() + ttlMs), createdAt: new Date(),
  });
  return { reservationId, expiresAt };
}

// Payment succeeded: the reservation becomes a real decrement.
async function confirm(reservationId) {
  const r = await reservations.findOneAndUpdate(
    { _id: reservationId, state: 'held' },        // guard: idempotent
    { $set: { state: 'confirmed', confirmedAt: new Date() } },
  );
  if (!r) return;                                  // already confirmed — fine
  await inventory.updateOne({ _id: r.sku }, { $inc: { reserved: -r.qty } });
}

// Abandoned or failed: put the stock back. Guarded, so it cannot double-release.
async function release(reservationId, reason) {
  const r = await reservations.findOneAndUpdate(
    { _id: reservationId, state: 'held' },
    { $set: { state: 'released', reason, releasedAt: new Date() } },
  );
  if (!r) return;
  await inventory.updateOne({ _id: r.sku }, { $inc: { available: r.qty, reserved: -r.qty } });
}

// Sweeper releases expired holds. As with seat booking, correctness does not
// depend on it being timely — it only affects how quickly stock returns.
Some retailers oversell deliberately

Strict reservation costs conversions: stock held by abandoned carts is unsellable for fifteen minutes, and during a sale that can be most of your inventory. Some businesses therefore accept a small oversell rate and apologise with a refund and a discount, because the extra completed orders outweigh the cost of the exceptions. That is a legitimate business decision, not a technical shortcut — but it must be explicit, measured, and have a funded apology path. Drifting into it by accident is how you end up cancelling a thousand Christmas orders.

Deep dive 2 — checkout as a saga

There is no distributed transaction across five services. There is a sequence of local commits, each with a compensating action. 1 price recompute from the catalogue — never trust the cart's price 2 reserve inventory conditional decrement ↩ release 3 authorise payment hold, not a capture ↩ void 4 create order the durable record ↩ mark cancelled 5 capture take the money ↩ refund 6 confirm inventory reserved → sold (no compensation needed) Failure at step N runs compensations N−1 … 1, in reverse. Why authorise before create, and capture after: an authorisation is reversible and free; a capture is not. If order creation fails, voiding an authorisation is invisible to the customer, whereas refunding a capture appears on their statement and generates a support contact.
javascript · the orchestrator, with compensation
async function checkout(cart, idempotencyKey) {
  const done = [];
  const undo = async () => {
    for (const step of done.reverse()) {
      // Compensations must never throw — log and continue, or a failed rollback
      // leaves the system in the worst possible state.
      await step.compensate().catch((e) => logger().error({ e, step: step.name }, 'compensation failed'));
    }
  };

  try {
    const priced = await pricing.quote(cart);                  // authoritative price
    if (priced.totalCents !== cart.expectedTotalCents) {
      throw new Conflict('price changed', { newTotalCents: priced.totalCents });
    }

    const reservationId = `${idempotencyKey}:inv`;
    await inventory.reserveAll(priced.lines, reservationId);
    done.push({ name: 'inventory', compensate: () => inventory.releaseAll(reservationId) });

    const auth = await payments.authorise({
      amount: priced.totalCents, idempotencyKey: `${idempotencyKey}:auth`,
    });
    done.push({ name: 'auth', compensate: () => payments.void(auth.id) });

    const order = await orders.create({ ...priced, authId: auth.id, idempotencyKey });
    done.push({ name: 'order', compensate: () => orders.cancel(order.id, 'checkout_failed') });

    await payments.capture(auth.id, { idempotencyKey: `${idempotencyKey}:cap` });
    await inventory.confirmAll(reservationId);
    await outbox.insert({ type: 'order.placed', payload: { orderId: order.id } });

    return order;
  } catch (err) {
    await undo();
    throw err;
  }
}
Price at checkout, never from the cart

The cart holds what the user saw; it is a display artefact and it is client-influenced. Prices, promotions, tax and shipping must all be recomputed server-side at checkout from the current catalogue. If the total has changed, stop and show the difference rather than silently charging the new amount — that is both a trust issue and, in many jurisdictions, a legal one. The cart's total is an expectation you validate, not an input you trust.

Deep dive 3 — the flash sale

8 000 requests/second against ONE inventory document. A single document has a single write lock; this is a hot-row problem, not a scale problem. 1 ADMISSION CONTROL a waiting room in front of the sale most of that traffic cannot succeed — queue it honestly at the edge 2 PARTITION THE ROW split 1 000 units into 10 buckets of 100 { _id: 'sku:0', available: 100 } … { _id: 'sku:9', available: 100 } a request picks a random bucket; on failure, tries another ↳ 10× the write concurrency, and the total is the sum ↳ the tail is awkward: when most buckets are empty, retries rise → collapse remaining stock into one bucket below a threshold 3 PRE-DECREMENT IN REDIS an atomic DECRBY as the gate, the database as the durable record written asynchronously ↳ fastest; requires reconciliation, and Redis must not be the only truth 4 SHED EARLY once reservations exceed stock, reject instantly at the edge ↳ a 5 ms "sold out" is a far better experience than a 30 s timeout

Catalogue read models

javascript · denormalise for the page, not for the database
// The product page needs one document, not eight joins. A projection worker
// consumes catalogue events and maintains this shape per locale.
{
  _id: 'B08N5WRWNW:en-GB',
  title, description, brand,
  images: [...],
  price: { currentCents, wasCents, currency },
  rating: { average: 4.4, count: 12_884 },     // precomputed, not aggregated on read
  variants: [{ sku, colour, size, inStock: true }],
  breadcrumbs: [...],
  updatedAt,
}
// Cache-Control: public, s-maxage=300, stale-while-revalidate=3600
// Stock is deliberately NOT in this document beyond a boolean — it changes
// far too often to cache, and it is advisory here anyway.

// Stock badge comes from a separate, tiny, short-TTL endpoint:
// GET /stock?skus=a,b,c → { a: 'in', b: 'low', c: 'out' }   TTL 10s
// This split is what lets the expensive page be cached for five minutes.

Failure modes

FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Payment captured, inventory gonecustomer charged, nothing to shipreserve before authorising; the saga ordering prevents it structurally
Order created, capture failsorder with no moneycompensate: cancel the order, release stock, notify
Duplicate submittwo ordersidempotency key threaded through every step of the saga
Compensation failsinconsistent statepersist saga state; a reconciler retries compensations; alert on stuck sagas
Promotion misconfiguredlarge financial losscap discount per order and in aggregate; require approval above a threshold; kill switch
Search index staleout-of-stock items shownacceptable — the reservation is authoritative; refresh availability on the page
Reservation sweeper stopsstock strandedlazy expiry in the reserve filter; alert on oldest held reservation
Interview · the load-bearing answers

Three. Inventory is a conditional decrement with a TTL'd reservation, never a read-then-write and never a lock held across a payment. Checkout is a saga with explicit compensations, ordered so that the irreversible step (capture) comes last and the reversible one (authorise) comes first. And the catalogue and the inventory are different systems with different consistency requirements — treating stock as cacheable page data is the mistake that produces overselling at exactly the moment it matters most.

S09

Stay marketplace (Airbnb-style)

Search here is not "find listings near me" — it is "find listings near me that are free for these seven specific nights, at a price computed per night, ranked". Availability over a date range is what makes this hard.

Functional
  • Search by location, date range, guests and facets
  • Hosts manage a calendar, rates, minimum stays and blocks
  • Book instantly, or request with host approval
  • Payment on booking; payout after check-in
  • Cancellation policies; double-blind reviews
Non-functional
  • Search under 500 ms with availability applied
  • A listing is never double-booked for overlapping dates
  • The price shown is the price charged
  • Calendar edits are visible in search within a minute
  • 500 M searches/day
Out of scope
  • Host onboarding and identity verification
  • Insurance and damage claims
  • Messaging (see the chat case study)
  • Photography and listing content

Back-of-envelope

Listings
10 Meach with a 500-day calendar
Searches
500 M/day~6 000/s, ~20 000/s peak
Bookings
2 M/day~250 : 1 search-to-book
Calendar cells
5 B10 M × 500 days
Calendar writes
~50 k/shosts, bookings, channel-manager syncs

Five billion calendar cells is the number that rules out the obvious design. A row per listing per night, joined against a geo query at 20 000 searches per second, is not a query plan — it is a wish.

Deep dive 1 — why availability search is hard

The query is: near London, free 12–19 June, ≥4 guests, has wifi, under £200/night NAIVE join listings to a calendar table SELECT l.* FROM listings l JOIN calendar c ON c.listing_id = l.id WHERE ST_DWithin(l.geo, :point, 20000) AND c.date BETWEEN '2026-06-12' AND '2026-06-18' AND c.available = true GROUP BY l.id HAVING COUNT(*) = 7 ← 7 rows per candidate listing 50 k candidates in London × 7 nights = 350 k calendar rows per search, at 20 k searches/second. 7 billion row reads per second. No. TWO-STAGE stage 1 — cheap candidate generation (search engine) geo + facets + price band + a COARSE availability signal → ~500 candidates in ~20 ms stage 2 — exact filtering (availability service) precise per-night check on 500 listings, from a compact representation → ~50 survivors in ~10 ms then rank, then hydrate. The whole design is about making stage 2 operate on 500 listings, not 50 000.

Deep dive 2 — the availability bitmap

javascript · 500 nights as 63 bytes
// One bit per night from a fixed epoch. 500 nights = 63 bytes per listing.
// 10 M listings × 63 B = 630 MB — the ENTIRE availability index fits in memory.
// That single fact is what makes stage 2 possible at this query rate.

const EPOCH = Date.UTC(2026, 0, 1);
const dayIndex = (d) => Math.floor((d - EPOCH) / 86_400_000);

class AvailabilityIndex {
  #bits = new Map();                        // listingId -> Uint8Array(63)

  isFree(listingId, checkIn, checkOut) {
    const bits = this.#bits.get(listingId);
    if (!bits) return false;
    // Nights occupied are [checkIn, checkOut) — the checkout day is free again.
    for (let d = dayIndex(checkIn); d < dayIndex(checkOut); d++) {
      if ((bits[d >> 3] >> (d & 7)) & 1) return false;   // 1 = booked/blocked
    }
    return true;
  }

  // Whole-range check on 500 candidates: ~500 × 7 bit tests. Microseconds.
  filter(candidateIds, checkIn, checkOut) {
    return candidateIds.filter((id) => this.isFree(id, checkIn, checkOut));
  }

  set(listingId, from, to, booked) {
    const bits = this.#bits.get(listingId) ?? new Uint8Array(63);
    for (let d = dayIndex(from); d < dayIndex(to); d++) {
      if (booked) bits[d >> 3] |= 1 << (d & 7);
      else        bits[d >> 3] &= ~(1 << (d & 7));
    }
    this.#bits.set(listingId, bits);
  }
}
// Rebuilt from the durable calendar on start; updated from a change stream.
// It is a CACHE — the booking write below is the authoritative check.
The coarse signal in stage 1

Stage 1 still needs to avoid returning candidates that are obviously unavailable, or stage 2 filters away 95% of its input. A cheap trick works well: index a small set of precomputed booleans per listing — "has any availability in June", "has a 7-night gap in June" — which the search engine can filter on directly. It is approximate and occasionally lets through a listing that stage 2 rejects, which is exactly the right trade.

Deep dive 3 — booking without double-booking

javascript · the durable check is a range overlap, not a bitmap read
// The bitmap is fast and approximate. The BOOKING must consult the durable
// record, and it must be a single atomic operation.
//
// bookings: { _id, listingId, checkIn, checkOut, state, guestId }
// A partial unique index cannot express "no overlapping range" in most
// document stores, so the atomicity comes from a per-listing calendar document.
//
// calendars: { _id: listingId, nights: { '2026-06-12': bookingId, ... }, version }

async function book({ listingId, checkIn, checkOut, guestId, idempotencyKey }) {
  const nights = eachNight(checkIn, checkOut);          // ['2026-06-12', ... ]
  const bookingId = new ObjectId();

  // Assert EVERY night is currently free, and claim them all, atomically.
  const filter = { _id: listingId };
  const set = {};
  for (const n of nights) {
    filter[`nights.${n}`] = { $exists: false };
    set[`nights.${n}`] = bookingId;
  }

  const res = await calendars.updateOne(filter, { $set: set, $inc: { version: 1 } });
  if (res.modifiedCount === 0) {
    const cal = await calendars.findOne({ _id: listingId }, { projection: { nights: 1 } });
    const taken = nights.filter((n) => cal.nights?.[n]);
    throw new Conflict('dates unavailable', { unavailableNights: taken });
  }

  await bookings.insertOne({
    _id: bookingId, listingId, checkIn, checkOut, guestId,
    state: 'pending_payment', idempotencyKey, createdAt: new Date(),
  });
  return bookingId;
}

// Release on payment failure or host rejection — guarded by bookingId so it
// can never clear a night that has since been claimed by someone else.
async function release(bookingId) {
  const b = await bookings.findOne({ _id: bookingId });
  const filter = { _id: b.listingId };
  const unset = {};
  for (const n of eachNight(b.checkIn, b.checkOut)) {
    filter[`nights.${n}`] = bookingId;                 // only OUR nights
    unset[`nights.${n}`] = '';
  }
  await calendars.updateOne(filter, { $unset: unset, $inc: { version: 1 } });
}
Minimum stays and gap rules are part of the check

A host with a three-night minimum should not end up with an unbookable one-night gap between two reservations. The availability check therefore is not merely "are these nights free" but "does this booking leave a valid calendar" — which means evaluating the host's rules against the resulting state, not the current one. Getting this wrong produces calendars full of dead nights that nobody can book and the host cannot explain.

Pricing

A nightly rate is not a number on the listing. It is a computation: base rate for that specific date (weekday/weekend, season) + demand adjustment (local events, occupancy) − length-of-stay discount (weekly, monthly) + cleaning fee (once, not per night) + service fee (percentage) + taxes (jurisdiction, sometimes per person) = total for the stay Therefore: · search shows an ESTIMATE from a precomputed per-night rate table · the listing page computes the exact total for the selected dates · checkout recomputes and pins it — the quote is stored with the booking · a quote has a short expiry, and the user is shown any change explicitly Never let the displayed total and the charged total be computed at different times from mutable inputs without an explicit confirmation step.

Booking flow and state

quote ─▶ pending_payment ─▶ confirmed ─▶ checked_in ─▶ completed ─▶ reviewed │ (nights held) │ │ └─ instant book: straight to confirmed ├─ request-to-book ─▶ awaiting_host ─(24 h)─▶ expired → release │ └─ host declines → release + refund └─ payment fails ─▶ released Money timing, which is a policy decision with real consequences: · authorise at booking, capture at check-in − 24 h (common) · payout to host 24 h after check-in (protects the guest) · cancellation refunds follow the listing's policy, computed from the booking's PINNED policy version, not the host's current one

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Search scaleshard the index by geography; replicate for query throughput; cache popular (city, date-range) results for 60 s
Calendar writesshard by listingId — no operation spans listings
Bitmap freshnessupdated from a change stream; a stale bitmap only costs a wasted candidate, never a double booking
Channel managershosts syncing from other platforms cause bursty calendar writes; rate-limit per host and reconcile
Long date rangescap search span (e.g. 28 nights); beyond that the bitmap check is still cheap but ranking is not
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Search shows an unavailable listinguser frustrationexpected — bitmap is a cache; the booking check is authoritative and the error names the nights
Host edits calendar during checkoutbooking fails latenights are held at quote time, so an edit cannot take them; host sees "held"
Payment authorised, host declinesmoney heldvoid the authorisation immediately; never capture before confirmation
Double booking via two channelsa real guest is turned awaythe calendar document is the single source of truth for all channels; external syncs go through the same conditional write
Bitmap rebuild lag after restartsearches miss listingsserve from the durable calendar for stage 2 until the bitmap is warm
Currency or tax change mid-quoteprice mismatchquotes are pinned and short-lived; a change forces an explicit re-confirm
Interview · the insight being looked for

That availability cannot be a join. The strong answer separates candidate generation from exact filtering, then observes that if availability is represented compactly enough — a bit per night — the exact filter runs in memory over the whole corpus and the problem disappears. The second insight is that this in-memory structure is a cache: it makes search fast, but the booking must still be a single conditional write against the durable calendar, because that is the only thing that can arbitrate between two simultaneous guests.

S10

Payments & ledger (Stripe-style)

The only system in this book where "eventually consistent" is not an acceptable answer to any question. Money must not be created or destroyed, every movement must be explicable months later, and the external processor will regularly tell you nothing at all.

Functional
  • Accept a payment: authorise, capture, or both
  • Refund fully or partially; handle disputes
  • Maintain balances for merchants; pay out on a schedule
  • Emit webhooks to merchants for every state change
  • Reconcile against processor settlement files
Non-functional
  • Money is never created or destroyed. The ledger always balances.
  • Every operation is idempotent and replay-safe
  • Full auditability: no record is ever mutated or deleted
  • 10 k payments/second peak
  • An ambiguous processor response never results in a double charge
Out of scope
  • Card network protocols and acquiring
  • Fraud scoring models
  • PCI scope reduction details beyond tokenisation
  • Tax and invoicing

Deep dive 1 — double-entry, and why you never store a balance

A balance is not a field. It is the sum of entries, and that distinction is what makes the system auditable and impossible to silently corrupt. Every transaction has ≥2 entries. They MUST sum to zero. £100 payment from a customer, £3 fee: txn_88f2 "payment" entry account: customer_receivable −10000 (they owe us less) entry account: merchant_9f3a_pending +9700 entry account: platform_revenue +300 ─────── 0 ✔ Refund £40 later — a NEW transaction, never an edit: txn_9a01 "refund" (references txn_88f2) entry account: merchant_9f3a_pending −4000 entry account: customer_receivable +4000 ─────── 0 ✔ Nothing is ever updated. Nothing is ever deleted. A mistake is corrected by posting a reversing transaction, exactly as in accounting — so the history shows both the error and the correction, which is the point.
javascript · the ledger write, with the invariant enforced
// entries: append-only. { _id, txnId, accountId, amount, currency, seq, createdAt }
// index: { accountId: 1, _id: 1 }   ← balance derivation
// index: { txnId: 1 } unique-ish     ← all entries of a transaction

async function post({ txnId, type, entries, idempotencyKey, metadata }) {
  // 1. The invariant, checked before anything touches the database.
  const byCurrency = groupBy(entries, (e) => e.currency);
  for (const [currency, group] of byCurrency) {
    const sum = group.reduce((a, e) => a + e.amount, 0);
    if (sum !== 0) throw new Error(`unbalanced transaction in ${currency}: ${sum}`);
  }
  // Amounts are integers in minor units. There is no float anywhere in this system.
  if (entries.some((e) => !Number.isInteger(e.amount))) throw new Error('non-integer amount');

  // 2. All entries land together, or none do. This is the one place a
  //    multi-document transaction is genuinely non-negotiable.
  const session = client.startSession();
  try {
    await session.withTransaction(async () => {
      // Idempotency lives in the same transaction as the effect.
      await transactions.insertOne(
        { _id: txnId, type, idempotencyKey, metadata, createdAt: new Date() },
        { session },
      );
      await entriesCol.insertMany(
        entries.map((e, i) => ({ _id: `${txnId}:${i}`, txnId, ...e, createdAt: new Date() })),
        { session, ordered: true },
      );
    }, { readConcern: { level: 'snapshot' }, writeConcern: { w: 'majority' } });
  } catch (e) {
    if (e.code === 11000) return transactions.findOne({ _id: txnId });   // replay
    throw e;
  } finally {
    await session.endSession();
  }
}

// Balance = sum of entries. Correct by construction, and provable at any time.
async function balance(accountId, asOf = new Date()) {
  const [r] = await entriesCol.aggregate([
    { $match: { accountId, createdAt: { $lte: asOf } } },
    { $group: { _id: '$currency', amount: { $sum: '$amount' } } },
  ]).toArray();
  return r ?? { amount: 0 };
}

// For accounts with millions of entries, snapshot periodically —
// but the snapshot is a CACHE derived from entries, never the source of truth.
// balance(t) = snapshot(t0).amount + sum(entries where t0 < createdAt <= t)
Why a mutable balance field is a bug waiting to happen

A stored balance can drift from its entries through a partial failure, a race, a bad migration or a well-meaning manual fix — and once it has drifted, there is no way to tell which number is right. Deriving the balance means the two can never disagree, because there is only one of them. Snapshots are fine as an optimisation precisely because they are reproducible: you can recompute any snapshot from the entries and compare.

Deep dive 2 — the payment intent state machine

requires_payment_method ▼ requires_confirmation ▼ requires_action ──────────▶ (3DS challenge, bank app) ▼ processing ─────────────────▶ UNKNOWN ← the state that defines the system │ ↳ never retry; POLL the processor ├──▶ succeeded ──▶ capture ──▶ ledger posted └──▶ failed / canceled Every transition: · guarded by the current state → idempotent, replay-safe · appended to an immutable event log on the intent · triggers exactly one ledger transaction, keyed by (intentId, transition) The rule that prevents double charges: the intent id is the idempotency key at the processor. A retry with the same intent returns the SAME result rather than charging again — and if the processor does not support this, you cannot safely retry at all.
javascript · handling the ambiguous response
async function confirm(intentId) {
  const intent = await intents.findOneAndUpdate(
    { _id: intentId, status: { $in: ['requires_confirmation', 'processing'] } },
    { $set: { status: 'processing', lastAttemptAt: new Date() } },
    { returnDocument: 'after' },
  );
  if (!intent) return intents.findOne({ _id: intentId });    // already resolved

  try {
    const result = await processor.charge({
      amount: intent.amountCents,
      currency: intent.currency,
      source: intent.tokenisedSource,
      idempotencyKey: intentId,          // THE critical line
    }, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(30_000) });

    return applyResult(intentId, result);
  } catch (err) {
    // Timeout or network failure. We do NOT know whether the money moved.
    // Retrying the charge could double-charge. Doing nothing could lose it.
    if (isAmbiguous(err)) {
      await intents.updateOne(
        { _id: intentId },
        { $set: { status: 'processing', needsReconciliation: true },
          $push: { events: { type: 'ambiguous_response', at: new Date(), err: String(err) } } },
      );
      // A poller resolves it from the PROCESSOR's own record — the only
      // authority on whether the charge happened.
      await reconcileQueue.add('poll-intent', { intentId },
        { jobId: `poll:${intentId}`, delay: 5_000, attempts: 20,
          backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 5_000 } });
      throw new AppError('payment processing', { status: 202 });
    }
    throw err;
  }
}

// The poller is the resolution mechanism, not the retry.
async function pollIntent({ intentId }) {
  const remote = await processor.retrieve(intentId);     // by OUR idempotency key
  if (remote.status === 'pending') throw new Error('still pending — retry');
  return applyResult(intentId, remote);                   // posts the ledger entries
}
Never retry a charge on an ambiguous response

A timeout means you did not receive an answer, not that nothing happened. The processor may have taken the money and lost the reply. Retrying the charge risks a second debit; the correct action is to retry the query. This is the single most important rule in payment integration, and the reason every serious processor supports idempotency keys on the write path and a retrieve endpoint on the read path.

Deep dive 3 — reconciliation

Your ledger says what you BELIEVE happened. The processor's settlement file says what ACTUALLY happened. They will differ, and the differences are the interesting part. Daily, for each settlement file: for each processor transaction: match on your intent id (embedded as processor metadata) · match, amounts agree → mark reconciled · match, amounts differ → investigate (FX, partial capture, fee change) · in processor, not in ours → ORPHAN CHARGE — money we did not record · in ours, not in processor → PHANTOM — we recorded a charge that never landed Orphan charges are the dangerous class: a customer was debited and your system has no record, so no goods were delivered and no refund is possible without manual work. They come almost entirely from ambiguous responses that were never polled to resolution — which is why the poller above is not optional. Every discrepancy becomes a ticket with an owner. A reconciliation process that produces a number nobody reads is theatre.

Webhooks to merchants

javascript · signed, retried, and explicitly not ordered
async function deliver(endpoint, event) {
  const body = JSON.stringify(event);
  const ts = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
  // Sign the timestamp WITH the body so a captured request cannot be replayed later.
  const signature = hmacSha256(endpoint.secret, `${ts}.${body}`);

  const res = await fetch(endpoint.url, {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: {
      'content-type': 'application/json',
      'webhook-id': event.id,                 // receiver dedups on this
      'webhook-timestamp': String(ts),
      'webhook-signature': `v1,${signature}`,
    },
    body,
    signal: AbortSignal.timeout(10_000),
  });

  if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`endpoint returned ${res.status}`);
}
// Retry schedule spans days: 5s, 30s, 2m, 10m, 1h, 6h, 24h, then disabled
// with an email. Delivery is at-least-once and UNORDERED — a `succeeded`
// can arrive before a `processing`. The receiver must treat each event as a
// state assertion and ignore ones older than what it has already seen.

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Ledger write throughputappend-only inserts partition well; shard entries by accountId, keeping a transaction's entries co-located
Balance readsperiodic snapshots plus a delta scan; never a stored mutable field
Hot merchant accountentries are appends, not updates, so there is no hot row — this is a benefit of the design
Multi-currencyentries carry a currency; the zero-sum invariant is enforced per currency; FX is an explicit transaction with its own rate record
Historical queriestime-travel is free: balance(accountId, asOf) is a filter on the same entries
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Ambiguous processor responsepossible orphan chargenever retry the charge; poll to resolution; reconcile daily
Duplicate requestdouble chargeidempotency key at our API and at the processor
Ledger transaction partially writtenunbalanced booksall entries in one transaction; a nightly job asserts every txn sums to zero
Webhook endpoint downmerchant out of syncretry over days; a polling API as the fallback; alert the merchant
Bug produced wrong entriesincorrect balancespost correcting transactions — never edit history; the audit shows both
Payout while a dispute landsnegative balancehold a reserve; delay payouts; allow negative balances rather than blocking the ledger
Rounding in splitspennies lost or createdallocate the remainder deterministically to a designated account; the sum must still be zero
Interview · the three answers that matter

“How do you store balances?” — you do not; you derive them from immutable double-entry records, with snapshots as a reproducible cache. “What happens when the processor times out?” — you never retry the charge, you poll the processor for the true state, because a timeout means unknown rather than failed. “How do you fix a bug that produced wrong entries?” — you post a reversing transaction, because an append-only ledger is only auditable if nothing is ever edited. Candidates who describe UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 have not yet met the class of bug this design exists to prevent.

S11

Notification platform

An internal platform whose users are other engineering teams. The interesting requirements are not technical — they are that no product team should be able to spam a user, and that a one-time passcode must never queue behind a marketing campaign.

Functional
  • Producers request a notification by template id, recipient and variables
  • Deliver via push, email, SMS or in-app, choosing per user preference
  • Honour consent, quiet hours, frequency caps and unsubscribes
  • Localise and personalise from templates
  • Track delivery, bounces and engagement
Non-functional
  • Transactional notifications delivered within seconds, always
  • A campaign of 10 M must not delay a passcode
  • A user never receives the same notification twice
  • Legal compliance on consent is absolute
  • 10 B notifications/month
Out of scope
  • Campaign authoring UI
  • Marketing segmentation and audience building
  • Email reputation and IP warming
  • Rich in-app messaging surfaces

Back-of-envelope

Volume
10 B/month~4 000/s average
Campaign burst
10 M in 10 min~17 000/s on top of baseline
Users
500 M~3 devices each for push
Transactional share
~15%but 100% of the urgency

Architecture

producers (any product team) │ POST /notifications { templateId, userId, vars, priority } ▼ ingest ─▶ validate · resolve recipient · DEDUPE · consent · frequency cap │ ↳ most drops happen HERE, before any work is done ▼ priority queues ┌─ critical OTP, security, payment — dedicated capacity ├─ transactional receipts, alerts └─ marketing campaigns, digests │ ▼ render ─▶ template + locale + variables → channel-specific payload │ ▼ channel adapters push(APNs/FCM) · email(SES) · SMS · in-app │ per-provider rate limits, retries, failover ▼ delivery tracking ─▶ bounces, complaints, opens → back into preferences
Fig. S11.1 — separate queues per priority class, with dedicated capacity for critical
Priority is capacity, not ordering

Putting a priority field on a single queue does not help: if 10 million campaign messages are already in flight against a provider rate limit, a high-priority passcode still waits for a slot. Critical notifications need their own queue, their own workers and their own share of the provider quota — physically separate capacity, reserved and idle most of the time. That idle capacity is the product requirement, not waste.

Deep dive — the preference and suppression engine

javascript · every check that can drop a notification, in order of cost
// Ordered cheapest-first: an early drop costs nothing downstream.
async function shouldSend({ userId, templateId, category, priority, dedupeKey }) {
  // 1. Global suppression — legal. Never overridable, not even by "critical".
  if (await suppressions.has(userId)) return drop('globally_unsubscribed');

  // 2. Hard bounces and complaints — sending again damages deliverability
  if (await bounces.isHard(userId, channel)) return drop('hard_bounce');

  // 3. Deduplication — the same logical notification from two producers,
  //    or a retry of the same request. TTL is per category.
  if (!(await dedupe.claim(dedupeKey, DEDUPE_TTL[category]))) return drop('duplicate');

  // 4. Category consent — the user turned THIS kind off
  const prefs = await preferences.get(userId);
  if (prefs.categories[category]?.enabled === false) return drop('category_off');

  // Transactional and critical bypass everything below this line.
  if (priority === 'critical' || category === 'transactional') return { send: true };

  // 5. Quiet hours, in the USER's timezone — not the server's
  if (inQuietHours(prefs, DateTime.now().setZone(prefs.timezone))) {
    return defer(nextWindow(prefs));        // deferred, not dropped
  }

  // 6. Frequency cap — the single most effective anti-annoyance control
  const sent = await counters.get(`freq:${userId}:${category}:${today()}`);
  if (sent >= prefs.caps[category] ?? DEFAULT_CAP[category]) return drop('frequency_cap');

  return { send: true };
}

// Dedupe claim is atomic — two producers racing produce exactly one send.
const claim = (key, ttlSec) => redis.set(`dedupe:${key}`, 1, 'EX', ttlSec, 'NX');
// dedupeKey is DERIVED, not supplied: hash(userId, templateId, entityId, bucket)
// so two teams notifying about the same order collapse into one message.
Critical bypasses preferences — but never suppression

A security alert or a login passcode must reach the user regardless of quiet hours or frequency caps, because the user's safety depends on it. It must not bypass a global unsubscribe or a hard bounce: those are legal and deliverability constraints respectively, and violating them has consequences beyond annoyance. Encode that distinction explicitly in the code, because the temptation to add one more bypass is constant.

Channel adapters and provider limits

javascript · rate limiting, failover and token hygiene
const channels = {
  push: {
    limiter: { max: 10_000, duration: 1_000 },       // provider's documented limit
    providers: [apns, fcm],                           // by platform, not failover
    async send(msg) {
      const res = await provider(msg.deviceToken.platform).send(msg);
      // Providers tell you when a token is dead. ACT ON IT — a fleet of dead
      // tokens wastes quota and degrades your sender reputation.
      if (res.error === 'Unregistered' || res.error === 'InvalidRegistration') {
        await devices.deleteOne({ token: msg.deviceToken.value });
        return { dropped: 'invalid_token' };
      }
      return res;
    },
  },
  email: {
    limiter: { max: 500, duration: 1_000 },
    providers: [ses, sendgrid],                       // genuine failover
    async send(msg) {
      for (const p of this.providers) {
        try { return await p.send(msg); }
        catch (e) { if (!isTransient(e)) throw e; providerFailover.inc({ from: p.name }); }
      }
      throw new Unavailable('all email providers failed');
    },
  },
  sms: { limiter: { max: 100, duration: 1_000 }, providers: [twilio] },
};

// Per-provider circuit breakers: a failing provider must not consume the
// entire worker pool retrying. See the resilience chapter.

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Campaign burstingest at full speed into the marketing queue; drain at a fixed rate — the queue absorbs, the providers are protected
Fan-out to devicesone notification per user becomes N device sends; batch per provider (APNs and FCM both accept multicast)
Preference lookupshot path — cache aggressively with short TTL; a stale opt-in is tolerable, a stale opt-out is not, so invalidate on unsubscribe synchronously
Template renderingprecompile templates; render is CPU-bound, so it scales horizontally and belongs off the ingest path
Tracking volumeopens and clicks are analytics-scale; batch and roll up rather than writing per event
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Campaign sent twice10 M duplicate messages, reputationaldedupe key derived from campaign id + user id, claimed atomically before send
Provider outageone channel downfailover for email/SMS; push has no alternative, so queue and retry
Retry storm after outageprovider rate limits exceededjittered backoff; the limiter is the hard gate, not the retry policy
Sending to unsubscribed userslegal exposuresuppression checked at send time, not at campaign build time — audiences go stale
Template variable missing"Hi {{firstName}}" deliveredrender is validated in CI against a schema; a missing required variable fails the request, not the send
Timezone bug3am marketing pushquiet hours computed in the user's zone; a test suite over every zone offset
Interview · the non-obvious insight

That this is fundamentally a policy engine with a delivery pipeline attached, not a delivery pipeline with some filters. Most of the design effort goes into deciding whether to send at all — suppression, consent, dedup, quiet hours, frequency caps — and most of the value is in the drops. The second insight is that priority must be separate capacity rather than a queue ordering field, because a shared provider rate limit means a campaign already in flight will otherwise delay a passcode no matter how it is prioritised.

S12

Search & typeahead

Two systems with different shapes. Search is an inverted index answering complex queries in a hundred milliseconds; typeahead is a prefix structure answering a much simpler question in twenty, on every keystroke.

Functional
  • Full-text search over 10 B documents with ranking
  • Filters and facets; pagination
  • Autocomplete on every keystroke
  • Spelling correction and synonyms
  • New and updated documents searchable within minutes
Non-functional
  • Search p99 under 100 ms
  • Typeahead p99 under 50 ms
  • 100 k queries/second
  • Index updates must not degrade query latency
Out of scope
  • Crawling (see the crawler case study)
  • Learning-to-rank model training
  • Personalisation infrastructure
  • Image and video search

Deep dive 1 — the inverted index

A forward index maps document → terms. Searching it means scanning every document. An inverted index maps term → documents, so a query reads only the postings for the terms it mentions. "distributed" → [ 12, 88, 401, 9002, ... ] posting list, doc ids ascending "systems" → [ 88, 401, 7734, ... ] Query "distributed systems" = INTERSECT the two lists. Because both are sorted, intersection is a linear merge with skip pointers — and skip pointers let you jump past long runs, so a rare term paired with a common one costs roughly the length of the RARE list, not the common one. Each posting carries more than an id: { docId, termFrequency, positions[], fieldMask } ↳ termFrequency → scoring ↳ positions → phrase queries ("distributed systems" adjacent) ↳ fieldMask → title matches weighted above body matches Segments: the index is built as immutable segments, periodically merged. Immutability is what makes concurrent search and indexing possible without locking — a searcher holds a consistent set of segments while new ones are being written alongside.
javascript · the analysis pipeline decides what is searchable
// Indexing and querying MUST use the same analyser, or terms never match.
// This is the single most common cause of "search returns nothing".
function analyse(text, locale) {
  return text
    .toLowerCase()
    .normalize('NFKC')                       // unicode: café === café
    .split(/\P{L}+/u).filter(Boolean)        // tokenise on non-letters
    .filter((t) => !STOPWORDS[locale].has(t))
    .map((t) => stem(t, locale));            // running → run
}
// Trade-off: stemming raises recall and lowers precision. "university" and
// "universe" stem alike in some algorithms, which surprises users. Index BOTH
// a stemmed and an exact field, and weight the exact match higher.

Deep dive 2 — partitioning

Rejected · partition by term
  • Each shard owns a subset of terms and their full posting lists
  • A two-term query touches only two shards — appealing in theory
  • But a common term's list is enormous and lands on one shard
  • Hot-shard problem is severe, and intersection requires shipping huge lists between nodes
Chosen · partition by document
  • Each shard holds a complete index over its slice of documents
  • Every query goes to every shard (scatter), results merged (gather)
  • Load is uniform; adding shards adds capacity linearly
  • Replicate shards for query throughput and availability
javascript · scatter-gather with a two-stage rank
async function search(query, { limit = 10, offset = 0 }) {
  const terms = analyse(query);

  // STAGE 1 — cheap retrieval on every shard, in parallel.
  // Each returns its own top (limit + offset) by a fast scoring function.
  const perShard = await Promise.all(
    shards.map((s) => s.topK(terms, limit + offset, { scorer: 'bm25' })
                       .catch(() => [])),          // a dead shard degrades, never fails
  );

  // Merge: k sorted lists → one. Take enough for re-ranking.
  const candidates = mergeTopK(perShard, (limit + offset) * 5);

  // STAGE 2 — expensive re-ranking on ~250 documents, not 10 billion.
  // This is where a learned model, personalisation and freshness live.
  const reranked = await ranker.score(candidates, { query, user: ctx.user });

  return reranked.slice(offset, offset + limit);
}
Two-stage ranking is the whole trick

You cannot run an expensive model over ten billion documents. You can run a cheap, index-native score (BM25) to find a few hundred plausible candidates, then spend real computation on those. Every large search system has this shape, and the interesting engineering is in making stage 1 cheap enough and its recall good enough that the right answer is nearly always in the candidate set.

Deep pagination is a scatter-gather trap

Page 500 with 10 results per page means every shard must return its top 5 010 documents so the merge can find the global 5 001st. Cost grows linearly with offset across the whole fleet. Cap the reachable depth (most engines do, around 10 000 results) and offer a cursor or "search within these results" instead. Users who genuinely want result 5 000 want a better query, not a deeper page.

Deep dive 3 — typeahead

Different problem: a query on every keystroke, so latency budget ~20 ms, but the query is only a prefix and the answer set is small. TRIE with cached top-K at each node (root) / | \ d s m | | | di sy mo | dis ──▶ topK: ["distributed systems", "distance", "discord"] | dist ──▶ topK: ["distributed systems", "distance", "distil"] Storing the answer AT each node turns the query into a single traversal of length |prefix| followed by a memory read. No ranking at query time. Memory: ~10 M distinct prefixes × 10 completions × ~40 B ≈ 4 GB. Shard by first 2-3 characters; each shard is independent and replicated. Built offline from query logs — the corpus is what people SEARCHED FOR, weighted by frequency and recency, not what documents contain. Rebuilt hourly; a delta layer in front handles trending terms in real time.
javascript · the query path, and the client's half of the job
// Server: one traversal, one read. No scoring, no index intersection.
function complete(prefix, k = 10) {
  let node = root;
  for (const ch of prefix.toLowerCase()) {
    node = node.children.get(ch);
    if (!node) return [];                    // no completions for this prefix
  }
  return node.topK.slice(0, k);              // precomputed at build time
}

// Client: the server can be fast and still feel slow if the client is naive.
//  · debounce ~120 ms — do not query on every keystroke
//  · CANCEL the in-flight request when a newer keystroke arrives, or a slow
//    response for "dis" overwrites a fast one for "distri"
//  · keep a local cache keyed by prefix — backspacing must never hit the network
//  · render optimistically from the local cache while the request is in flight

Freshness

Immutable segments make search fast and updates awkward. The standard resolution: new documents ──▶ in-memory buffer (searchable immediately, small, slow-ish) │ every ~1 s ▼ small segment on disk │ background merge ▼ large segments (fast, immutable, most of the corpus) A query searches the buffer AND all segments, merging results. So a document is searchable ~1 second after indexing, without ever mutating a segment. Merges are the operational hazard: merging two 50 GB segments consumes IO and CPU and shows up as a p99 latency spike. Throttle merge IO, schedule large merges off-peak, and alert on segment count — an index with thousands of small segments is one where merging has fallen behind.

Failure modes

FailureBlast radiusMitigation
One shard slow or downpartial resultstimeout per shard and return what you have, flagged as partial — degraded beats failed
Analyser mismatchzero results for valid queriessame analyser at index and query time, asserted by a test that indexes and searches a known document
Hot queryrepeated identical workcache results by (query, filters) for seconds; the head of the distribution is very concentrated
Segment merge stormp99 spikethrottle merge IO; schedule off-peak; monitor segment count
Reindex requiredhours of rebuildbuild into a new index alias, then flip the alias atomically — never rebuild in place
Typeahead index stalemissing trending termsa small real-time delta layer merged over the hourly-built trie
Deep pagination abusefleet-wide loadhard cap on offset; cursor-based alternative
Interview · what a strong answer includes

Document partitioning with the reason term partitioning loses (hot shards and shipping huge posting lists). Two-stage ranking, because you cannot score ten billion documents. The immutable-segment model and how freshness is achieved without mutation. And for typeahead, recognising that it is a different data structure built from query logs rather than documents, with the answers precomputed at each node so the query does no ranking at all. Mentioning that a search engine must never be the system of record is a good closing note.

S13

URL shortener & analytics
