The classic warm-up, and a genuine masterclass if you treat it seriously. Everything interesting is in key generation, the read path's extreme cacheability, and capturing analytics without slowing the redirect.

Functional
  • Shorten a long URL; optionally choose a custom alias
  • Redirect to the original, fast, from anywhere
  • Expiry and deletion
  • Click analytics: volume, referrer, geography, device
Non-functional
  • Redirect p99 under 30 ms globally
  • 500 : 1 read-to-write
  • Keys are not guessable or enumerable
  • Analytics never delays a redirect
Out of scope
  • Link-in-bio pages and branded domains
  • A/B testing and deep-link routing
  • QR generation

Back-of-envelope

New URLs
100 M/month~40/s average
Redirects
10 B/month~4 000/s, ~20 000/s peak
Storage
~600 GB/5 yr6 B rows × ~100 B
Hot set
~1%a few GB — fits entirely in cache

Five years of links is only a few hundred gigabytes, and the actively-clicked subset is a rounding error on that. This is a key-value lookup with a tiny working set — which means the read path should almost never touch a database.

Deep dive 1 — key generation

ApproachCollision handlingGuessable?Verdict
Hash the URL, truncatecheck-and-retry on collisionnoa read before every write; the same URL always maps to the same key, which leaks that someone shortened it
Random 7 charsunique index, retry on duplicatenofine, but write amplification rises as the space fills
Global counter → base62impossible by constructionyes — sequentialrejected: anyone can enumerate every link ever created
Counter ranges + shuffleimpossible by constructionnochosen — no collisions, no reads, not enumerable
javascript · range allocation, and why it needs no coordination
// base62: [0-9a-zA-Z]. 62^7 = 3.5 trillion keys — 5 years at 100M/month
// uses 6 billion, or 0.17% of the space.
const ALPHABET = '0123456789abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyzABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ';

// Each node claims a block of 10,000 ids ONCE, then allocates from memory.
// One coordination round trip per 10,000 URLs — effectively free.
class KeyAllocator {
  #next = 0; #end = 0;
  async #claim() {
    const { value } = await counters.findOneAndUpdate(
      { _id: 'urlKeys' },
      { $inc: { next: 10_000 } },
      { upsert: true, returnDocument: 'after' },
    );
    this.#next = value.next - 10_000;
    this.#end = value.next;
  }
  async next() {
    if (this.#next >= this.#end) await this.#claim();
    return encode(scramble(this.#next++));
  }
}

// Sequential ids are not enumerable once passed through a reversible
// permutation. Feistel is a keyed bijection over the id space: distinct ids
// map to distinct outputs (so still no collisions), but consecutive ids look
// unrelated. XOR-with-a-secret would NOT do this — the pattern survives.
function scramble(n) {
  let l = n >>> 16, r = n & 0xffff;
  for (let i = 0; i < 4; i++) {
    const t = r;
    r = l ^ (roundFn(r, i) & 0xffff);
    l = t;
  }
  return ((l << 16) | r) >>> 0;
}

function encode(n) {
  let s = '';
  do { s = ALPHABET[n % 62] + s; n = Math.floor(n / 62); } while (n > 0);
  return s.padStart(7, '0');
}
Why sequential keys are a real vulnerability

A shortener with sequential keys lets anyone walk the entire corpus with a loop. That corpus contains password-reset links people shortened, unlisted documents, internal dashboards and private invitations — all of which their owners believed were unguessable. This has happened repeatedly in production systems. The permutation costs nothing and removes the entire class of problem.

Deep dive 2 — the read path

20 000 redirects/second. Almost none should reach a database. client ─▶ edge / CDN ─ hit ─▶ 302 + Location ~5 ms │ miss ▼ app + local LRU ─ hit ─▶ 302 ~1 ms │ miss ▼ Redis ─ hit ─▶ 302 ~2 ms │ miss (cold, or expired) ▼ database ─▶ 302, populate caches ~10 ms 301 vs 302 — a real product decision, not a detail: 301 permanent → browsers cache it forever. Fastest possible repeat redirect, but you never see the click again and you can never change or revoke the destination for that user. 302 found → every click reaches you. Analytics work, links stay revocable. Choose 302 unless analytics genuinely do not matter, and set a short Cache-Control alongside it. Negative caching matters too: an unknown key should be cached as "unknown" for a minute, or a scanner probing random keys becomes a database load test.

Deep dive 3 — analytics without slowing the redirect

javascript · fire-and-forget, then batch
app.get('/:key', async (req, res) => {
  const target = await resolve(req.params.key);        // cache-first
  if (!target) return res.status(404).render('not-found');
  if (target.expiresAt && target.expiresAt < new Date()) return res.status(410).render('expired');

  // Respond FIRST. The redirect is the product; analytics is a side effect.
  res.redirect(302, target.url);

  // Then record, without awaiting. A failure here must never affect the user.
  clickBuffer.push({
    key: req.params.key,
    ts: Date.now(),
    ref: req.get('referer')?.slice(0, 255),
    ua: req.get('user-agent')?.slice(0, 255),
    country: req.get('cf-ipcountry'),                  // from the edge, not a GeoIP lookup
    // Never store the raw IP: hash it with a rotating daily salt so unique-visitor
    // counting still works but the data is not personally identifying.
    visitor: hash(req.ip + dailySalt()),
  });
});

// Flush in batches — one write per click at 20k/s would dominate everything.
setInterval(() => {
  if (!clickBuffer.length) return;
  const batch = clickBuffer.splice(0, clickBuffer.length);
  producer.send({ topic: 'clicks', messages: batch.map(toMessage) }).catch(() => {
    clicksDropped.inc(batch.length);                    // drop rather than block
  });
}, 1_000).unref();

// Real-time counter for the dashboard's headline number — approximate, cheap:
redis.incr(`clicks:${key}:${hourBucket()}`);
// Exact counts come from the batch pipeline rolling up the Kafka topic.
// Two numbers, two purposes, and it is worth saying which is which in the UI.
Approximate now, exact later

A dashboard headline wants a number immediately and does not need it to be exact. A billing or reporting figure must be exact and can take minutes. Serving both from the same pipeline forces you to make one of them worse. Increment a Redis counter for the live view, roll up the event stream for the authoritative figure, and reconcile — HyperLogLog gives unique visitors at ~1.6% error in 12 kB per key, which is far cheaper than a set of visitor hashes.

Abuse, which is not optional

  • Scan the target on creation against a malware and phishing reputation service. A shortener is a laundering tool for malicious links, and yours will be used as one.
  • Rate-limit creation per account and per IP; a burst of thousands of shortenings from one source is almost never legitimate.
  • Re-scan asynchronously. A URL that was clean on Monday can be compromised by Thursday, and the short link keeps working.
  • Interstitial warning for links that later score badly, rather than a silent redirect.
  • Block redirects to your own admin paths and to private address ranges, or the shortener becomes an SSRF primitive.

Failure modes

FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Viral linkone key, enormous trafficedge caching makes this trivial — the hot key is the easiest case, not the hardest
Cache cold after deploydatabase load spikerequest collapsing; staggered rollout; the working set is small enough to prewarm
Key scannerdatabase load from missesnegative caching plus per-IP rate limiting
Analytics pipeline backs updelayed statsdrop events rather than block; the redirect must never depend on it
Counter node loses its rangea block of ids unusedharmless — the space is 3.5 trillion; never try to reclaim ranges
Custom alias collision—unique index; return 409 and suggest alternatives
Malicious targetreputational and legalscan on create and periodically; interstitial; takedown path
Interview · why this question is asked

Because it is simple enough that there is nowhere to hide. Interviewers look for: a key scheme with no read-before-write and no enumerability (and the ability to explain why sequential is a security problem, not an aesthetic one); recognition that this is a caching problem where the hot key is the easy case; the 301-versus-302 trade stated as a product decision; and analytics captured off the response path. Candidates who spend the time on database sharding have solved a problem the numbers say does not exist.

S14

Nearby & geo search

"What is near me" looks trivial and is not. The naive query asks for a rectangle when you wanted a circle, and asks a B-tree to satisfy two independent range conditions — which it cannot do.

Functional
  • Find places within a radius, ranked by distance and relevance
  • Filter by category, rating, open-now
  • Support both static places and moving objects
  • Bounding-box search for a map viewport
Non-functional
  • Results in under 50 ms
  • 100 k queries/second
  • Static places change rarely; moving objects update constantly
  • Correct near cell boundaries and across the antimeridian
Out of scope
  • Routing and turn-by-turn navigation
  • Map tile rendering
  • Address geocoding

Why the obvious query fails

SELECT * FROM places WHERE lat BETWEEN 51.49 AND 51.53 AND lng BETWEEN -0.14 AND -0.10 Three separate problems: 1 A composite B-tree on (lat, lng) can only range-scan the FIRST column. It reads every row in the latitude band — a strip around the entire planet — then filters by longitude. Selectivity collapses. 2 You asked for a square. Corners are ~1.41× the radius, so results include places outside the circle and you must post-filter by true distance. 3 A degree of longitude is 111 km at the equator and 0 km at the pole. A fixed degree offset is a different real distance everywhere. The fix: reduce two dimensions to ONE ordered key that preserves locality, so a single index range scan retrieves a compact region of space.

The options

StructureHow it worksGood atWeak at
Geohashinterleave lat/lng bits → a base32 string; shared prefix = nearbyworks in any KV or B-tree store; trivially shardable; human-readableboundary problem; cells are rectangles that distort with latitude
Quadtreerecursively subdivide until a cell holds ≤ N pointsadapts to density; efficient for skewed datain-memory; rebalancing under movement; harder to shard
R-treebounding boxes in a balanced treepolygons, ranges, "within this shape"; what PostGIS usesheavier writes; not a simple key
S2project the sphere onto a cube, Hilbert curve per faceuniform cell area globally; excellent locality; region coveringmore complex; cells are quadrilaterals
H3hexagonal hierarchical griduniform neighbour distance (6 equidistant); clean ring querieshexagons do not subdivide perfectly; approximate parent/child
Static places → geohash or S2
  • Store a cell id column, index it, range-scan
  • Works in the database you already have
  • Updates are rare, so write cost is irrelevant
Moving objects → H3 in memory
  • Position is a hash-map key; update is O(1)
  • No durability needed — re-reported in seconds
  • gridDisk gives an exact ring with no boundary logic

Deep dive — geohash, and the boundary problem

Geohash interleaves latitude and longitude bits, then base32-encodes: 51.5074, -0.1278 → gcpvj0duy precision 5 gcpvj ±2.4 km precision 6 gcpvj0 ±0.61 km precision 7 gcpvj0d ±0.076 km Nearby points usually share a prefix. Usually. THE BOUNDARY PROBLEM Two shops 10 m apart, either side of a cell edge: shop A gcpvj0d... shop B gcpvj0e... They share only "gcpvj0" — and at a cell edge they may share nothing. A prefix query for shop A's cell simply misses shop B entirely. THE FIX: query the cell AND its 8 neighbours, then filter by true distance. ┌───┬───┬───┐ │ NW│ N │ NE│ 9 range scans instead of 1. ├───┼───┼───┤ Still far cheaper than a lat/lng scan, and now correct. │ W │ ● │ E │ ├───┼───┼───┤ This is why H3's gridDisk is attractive: the ring is │ SW│ S │ SE│ a primitive rather than something you compute. └───┴───┴───┘
javascript · a correct geohash proximity search
// Choose precision from the radius, so cells are comparable to the search area.
// Too coarse: you scan far too many candidates. Too fine: you need many rings.
const PRECISION_FOR_RADIUS = [
  { maxM: 20,      p: 8 }, { maxM: 150,   p: 7 }, { maxM: 1_200, p: 6 },
  { maxM: 5_000,   p: 5 }, { maxM: 40_000, p: 4 },
];

async function nearby(lat, lng, radiusM, filters = {}) {
  const { p } = PRECISION_FOR_RADIUS.find((x) => radiusM <= x.maxM) ?? { p: 3 };
  const centre = geohash.encode(lat, lng, p);

  // The 8 neighbours plus the centre. Handles the boundary problem, and the
  // library handles the antimeridian and pole wrapping so you do not have to.
  const cells = [centre, ...geohash.neighbours(centre)];

  // One indexed range scan per cell — a prefix match is a range on a B-tree.
  const candidates = await places.find({
    $or: cells.map((c) => ({ geohash: { $gte: c, $lt: c + '~' } })),
    ...buildFilters(filters),          // category, openNow, minRating
  }).limit(2_000).toArray();

  // Exact distance filter and ranking on a few hundred candidates — cheap.
  return candidates
    .map((pl) => ({ ...pl, distanceM: haversine(lat, lng, pl.lat, pl.lng) }))
    .filter((pl) => pl.distanceM <= radiusM)
    .sort((a, b) => score(b, radiusM) - score(a, radiusM))
    .slice(0, 50);
}

// Ranking is rarely pure distance: a 4.8-star restaurant 400 m away usually
// beats a 3.1-star one 100 m away. Make the weighting explicit and tunable.
const score = (p, radiusM) =>
  (1 - p.distanceM / radiusM) * 0.5 + (p.rating / 5) * 0.35 + popularity(p) * 0.15;
Filter after the geo pass, not before

It is tempting to apply "open now" and "category = pizza" in the same index as the geo scan. Compound indexes cannot serve a range on the geohash plus equality on two other columns efficiently in both orders, and you end up with an index per filter combination. Retrieve a bounded candidate set geographically, then filter in memory — a few thousand candidates is nothing, and it keeps the index count sane.

Moving objects

javascript · a different store for a different workload
// 200k couriers × one update every 5s = 40k writes/s of data that is
// worthless in 30 seconds. This must not touch a durable database.
class MovingIndex {
  #cells = new Map();     // h3 cell -> Map<id, {lat,lng,ts,meta}>
  #where = new Map();     // id -> cell, so we can remove from the old cell

  upsert(id, lat, lng, meta) {
    const cell = h3.latLngToCell(lat, lng, 9);
    const prev = this.#where.get(id);
    if (prev !== undefined && prev !== cell) this.#cells.get(prev)?.delete(id);
    let b = this.#cells.get(cell);
    if (!b) this.#cells.set(cell, (b = new Map()));
    b.set(id, { lat, lng, ts: Date.now(), ...meta });
    this.#where.set(id, cell);
  }

  within(lat, lng, radiusM, predicate) {
    const rings = Math.ceil(radiusM / 174);            // res-9 edge ≈ 174 m
    const out = [];
    for (const cell of h3.gridDisk(h3.latLngToCell(lat, lng, 9), rings)) {
      for (const [id, o] of this.#cells.get(cell) ?? []) {
        if (Date.now() - o.ts > 30_000) continue;      // stale = gone
        if (predicate && !predicate(o)) continue;
        const d = haversine(lat, lng, o.lat, o.lng);
        if (d <= radiusM) out.push({ id, ...o, distanceM: d });
      }
    }
    return out.sort((a, b) => a.distanceM - b.distanceM);
  }
}
// Sharded by cell range across nodes. No replication needed: a lost shard
// refills within one reporting interval.

Scaling and failure

ConcernApproach
Dense citiescell-based sharding creates a hot shard in Manhattan; use finer precision there, or shard by cell hash and accept multi-shard fan-out
Sparse regionsa fixed precision returns nothing over an ocean; expand rings until enough candidates or a max radius
Viewport queriesa bounding box is a set of covering cells (S2's region coverer does this well) rather than a radius
Cachingquantise the query point to a coarse cell before caching, or every slightly-different coordinate is a cache miss
Antimeridian±180° wrap breaks naive range queries; use a library's neighbour function, and test at ±180 and the poles
FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Boundary missa nearby place never appearsalways query the neighbour ring; a test with points either side of a known edge
Hot cell shardslow queries in one citysplit the cell range; replicate the hot shard read-only
Stale moving positionsdispatch to someone goneTTL on entries; treat missing heartbeat as offline
Precision mismatchscanning far too many candidatesderive precision from radius; cap candidate count and widen only if needed
Index rebuildmoving index empty on restartno durability needed — it refills in one reporting interval; serve static results meanwhile
Interview · the one thing to get right

Say the boundary problem out loud before you are asked. Almost every candidate proposes geohash prefixes, and almost none mention that two points a metre apart can share no prefix at all — which means the naive implementation silently returns wrong results rather than failing. Following it with "so I query the eight neighbours and filter by true distance, or use H3 where the ring is a primitive" demonstrates you have actually built this rather than read about it.

S15

Web crawler & indexer

A crawler is a scheduling problem disguised as a networking problem. Fetching pages is easy; deciding which page to fetch next, from ten billion candidates, without hammering any single host, is where the design lives.

Functional
  • Crawl from a seed set, following links
  • Respect robots.txt and crawl-delay
  • Deduplicate URLs and near-duplicate content
  • Re-crawl by importance and change frequency
  • Hand parsed content to an indexing pipeline
Non-functional
  • 10 B pages, refreshed roughly monthly
  • 5 000 pages/second sustained
  • Never overload a host — politeness is non-negotiable
  • A fetcher crash loses no URLs
Out of scope
  • Ranking and the search index itself
  • JavaScript rendering farms
  • Paywalled and authenticated content

Back-of-envelope

Corpus
10 B pagesat ~80 kB compressed ≈ 800 TB
Rate
5 000/s10 B ÷ 30 days ≈ 3 900/s, so 5 000 with headroom
Links found
~50/page250 k URL candidates/s to dedupe
Seen-URL set
10 B entriesthe memory problem — see below
Fetchers
~5 000each ~1 page/s given politeness delays

Architecture

seeds ──▶ URL frontier ◀──────────────────── extracted links │ ▲ ▼ │ fetcher pool ──▶ content store ──▶ parser ──┘ │ (raw HTML, extract links, │ object storage) normalise, filter ▼ robots cache ──▶ dedupe (seen set + SimHash) per-host rules, TTL 24h │ ▼ indexer (see the search chapter) Partitioned by hash(host) — every URL for one host is handled by the same frontier partition, which is what makes politeness enforceable locally with no cross-node coordination at all.
Fig. S15.1 — host-partitioned so politeness needs no distributed locking

Deep dive 1 — the URL frontier

Two competing requirements: priority crawl important and stale pages first politeness never more than one connection per host, with a delay A single priority queue satisfies the first and violates the second: the top 1 000 URLs are frequently all from one large site. TWO-LEVEL DESIGN FRONT queues — one per priority band (importance × staleness) F1 ████████ highest F2 ██████ F3 ███ a biased random pick selects a band, F4 █ so high priority dominates but never starves the rest │ ▼ router: hash(host) → back queue BACK queues — one per host, each strictly FIFO B1 example.com ████ B2 bbc.co.uk ██ B3 wikipedia.org ███████ │ ▼ HEAP keyed by nextFetchAt (B2, 10:00:03) ← pop the earliest, fetch it, push back with (B1, 10:00:05) nextFetchAt = now + crawlDelay(host) (B3, 10:00:09) Every fetcher pops from the heap. A host is in the heap at most once, so one host can never be fetched concurrently — politeness is structural.
javascript · the frontier
class Frontier {
  #front = [[], [], [], []];         // priority bands
  #back = new Map();                 // host -> URL[]
  #heap = new MinHeap((a, b) => a.at - b.at);   // {host, at}

  enqueue(url, priority) {
    this.#front[priority].push(url);
  }

  // Called when a back queue empties: refill it from the front queues.
  #refill(host) {
    for (const band of this.#pickBands()) {      // biased random, high bands first
      const i = band.findIndex((u) => hostOf(u) === host);
      if (i >= 0) return band.splice(i, 1)[0];
    }
    return null;
  }

  async next() {
    const now = Date.now();
    const top = this.#heap.peek();
    if (!top || top.at > now) return null;        // nothing due yet — fetchers idle briefly
    this.#heap.pop();

    const queue = this.#back.get(top.host) ?? [];
    const url = queue.shift() ?? this.#refill(top.host);
    if (!url) return null;                        // host exhausted; drop it from the heap

    // Reschedule BEFORE returning, so no other fetcher can take this host.
    const delay = await robots.crawlDelay(top.host);   // default 1s, respect the directive
    this.#heap.push({ host: top.host, at: now + delay });
    return url;
  }

  // A fetcher that dies mid-request must not lose the URL. Leases, not deletes:
  // claim with a TTL, and a reaper re-enqueues anything whose lease expired.
  lease(url, ms = 60_000) { return leases.set(url, Date.now() + ms); }
  complete(url) { return leases.delete(url); }
}
Politeness is an obligation, not a tuning parameter

Crawling a small site at a hundred requests per second is a denial-of-service attack that happens to be well-intentioned. It gets your address blocked, your organisation complained about, and in some jurisdictions raises legal questions. Respect robots.txt, honour Crawl-delay, back off hard on 429 and 503, identify yourself in the user agent with a contact URL, and rate-limit per IP as well as per host — because a thousand hostnames can share one small server.

Deep dive 2 — deduplication at ten billion

javascript · URL normalisation, then a bloom filter
// Normalise FIRST — these are all the same page, and treating them as
// distinct is how a crawler wastes most of its capacity.
function normalise(raw) {
  const u = new URL(raw);
  u.hash = '';                                       // fragments never change the response
  u.hostname = u.hostname.toLowerCase().replace(/^www\./, '');
  u.protocol = 'https:';                             // after verifying the host supports it
  if ((u.protocol === 'https:' && u.port === '443')) u.port = '';
  // Strip tracking parameters — they multiply the URL space enormously
  for (const p of ['utm_source','utm_medium','utm_campaign','fbclid','gclid','sessionid','sid']) {
    u.searchParams.delete(p);
  }
  u.searchParams.sort();                             // ?a=1&b=2 === ?b=2&a=1
  if (u.pathname.endsWith('/index.html')) u.pathname = u.pathname.slice(0, -10);
  return u.toString();
}

// A hash set of 10 B URLs at ~16 bytes each is 160 GB — too much for memory
// on every node. A Bloom filter trades a small false-positive rate for a
// 25× reduction.
//
//   n = 10e9 entries, p = 1% false positive
//   m = -n·ln(p)/(ln2)²  ≈ 9.6 bits/entry ≈ 12 GB   ← fits in RAM
//   k = (m/n)·ln2 ≈ 7 hash functions
//
// FALSE POSITIVE = we wrongly believe we have seen a URL, so we skip it.
// At 1%, we miss ~1% of pages. For a web crawler that is an acceptable loss;
// for a payments dedup it would not be. Know which side of that line you are on.
// There are NO false negatives — a URL we have crawled is never re-crawled.
if (!seen.mightContain(url)) {
  seen.add(url);
  frontier.enqueue(url, priorityOf(url));
}
CONTENT dedup is a different problem. Two URLs, identical content: exact duplicate → SHA-256 of the normalised body, a hash set near-duplicate → same article with a different sidebar or ad a byte hash is useless here — one word changes everything SimHash: a locality-sensitive hash where SIMILAR documents produce hashes with a SMALL Hamming distance. 1. tokenise into shingles (overlapping n-grams) 2. hash each shingle, weight by frequency 3. sum the weighted bit vectors, take the sign of each position → a 64-bit fingerprint distance ≤ 3 bits ⇒ near-duplicate ⇒ index one, alias the rest Store fingerprints in tables permuted by bit-block so a near-neighbour lookup is a handful of exact-match probes rather than a scan.

Deep dive 3 — freshness and traps

javascript · adaptive re-crawl
// Crawling everything monthly wastes capacity on pages that never change and
// starves pages that change hourly. Learn each page's change rate.
function nextCrawl(page) {
  const base = 86_400_000;                              // 1 day
  // Exponential backoff on stability, capped; accelerate when it does change.
  const interval = page.unchangedCount > 0
    ? Math.min(base * 2 ** page.unchangedCount, 30 * base)
    : base / 4;
  // Importance pulls it forward: a homepage is re-crawled far more than a
  // deep archive page with the same change rate.
  return Date.now() + interval / Math.max(1, page.importance);
}

// Cheap freshness checks — do not download 80 kB to discover nothing changed
const res = await fetch(url, {
  headers: {
    'if-modified-since': page.lastModified,
    'if-none-match': page.etag,
    'user-agent': 'AcmeBot/1.0 (+https://acme.com/bot)',
  },
});
if (res.status === 304) {                               // free: no body transferred
  await pages.updateOne({ _id: url }, { $inc: { unchangedCount: 1 } });
  return;
}
Spider traps consume a crawler alive

An infinite calendar (/events?date=2099-12-31&next=…) generates unlimited unique URLs, all of which look new. So do session ids in paths, faceted-search parameter combinations, and deliberately generated link mazes. Defences: a depth limit per host, a hard cap on URLs per host per crawl cycle, detecting parameter patterns that generate unbounded variation, near-duplicate content detection (the pages are all nearly identical), and a per-host budget that simply stops when exceeded. Without these, one site can occupy your entire frontier.

Failure modes

FailureBlast radiusMitigation
Fetcher dies mid-requestin-flight URLs lostlease with TTL; a reaper re-enqueues expired leases
Spider trapfrontier filled with junkper-host budget and depth cap; near-duplicate detection; pattern blacklist
robots.txt changes mid-crawlcrawling disallowed pathscache with a 24 h TTL and re-check; honour immediately on refresh; drop queued URLs now disallowed
Host blocks uslost coverage of a siteback off exponentially; contact address in the user agent; a manual allowlist process
Bloom filter false positive~1% of pages never crawledaccepted trade; a secondary exact set for high-importance hosts
Frontier partition lostthose hosts stallpersist the frontier; rebuild a partition from the seen set and link graph
Content store fillscrawl haltscompress; tier cold pages out; retention by importance rather than uniformly
Interview · the design that distinguishes

The two-level frontier. Almost everyone proposes a priority queue; the follow-up is always "what if the top thousand URLs are all from one site?", and the answer is the front-queue/back-queue split with a next-fetch-time heap, which makes politeness structural rather than something you check. The second distinguishing answer is the Bloom filter with the arithmetic — 12 GB for ten billion URLs at 1% — and an explicit statement of what a false positive costs here, which is a page never crawled. Knowing when an approximate structure is acceptable is the actual skill being tested.

L01

The low-level design method

High-level design asks which boxes exist. Low-level design asks what is inside one box: the interfaces, the state, the concurrency, and what happens when each of them is wrong.

The method

  1. Clarify and bound. What is in scope, what is explicitly not, and what scale. "A parking lot" could be one barrier or a thousand sites; the designs share nothing.
  2. Nouns become candidate entities, verbs become behaviours. A crude heuristic, and a reliable starting point.
  3. Write the public interface first. Before any field, before any class. The interface is the contract; the implementation is negotiable.
  4. Model the state explicitly. If the object has modes, write the state machine down. Booleans that encode a state are the single most common source of impossible states.
  5. Find the concurrency. What can two callers do at the same time, and what breaks? This is where LLD interviews are won.
  6. Then the classes, and only then.
Interface first is not a style preference

Writing ParkingLot.park(vehicle) → Ticket | Rejection before choosing data structures forces you to decide what a failure looks like, whether the operation can be retried, and who owns the identifier. Starting from fields produces a design where the interface is whatever fell out — and that interface is the part your callers are stuck with.

SOLID, with the failure each principle prevents

PrinciplePreventsBackend example
Single responsibilitya class with several reasons to change, so every change risks unrelated breakagean Order that also renders emails and talks to Stripe
Open/closedediting tested code to add a caseadding a payment method by adding a class, not by extending a switch
Liskov substitutiona subtype that breaks callers' assumptionsReadOnlyRepository extends Repository that throws on save
Interface segregationimplementers forced to stub methods they do not needa 30-method Storage interface when a cache needs three
Dependency inversionbusiness logic that cannot be tested without infrastructurea service that constructs its own MongoClient

Make illegal states unrepresentable

javascript · the highest-leverage LLD technique
// BAD: four booleans = 16 combinations, of which 12 are impossible.
// Every consumer must defensively check combinations that should not exist,
// and eventually one of them will occur because nothing prevents it.
class Order {
  isPaid = false;
  isShipped = false;
  isCancelled = false;
  isRefunded = false;
}
// isShipped && isCancelled? isRefunded && !isPaid? The type permits both.

// GOOD: one state, with the data each state actually carries.
// A cancelled order HAS a reason. A shipped order HAS a tracking number.
// Neither can exist without it, and neither can carry the other's fields.
const OrderState = {
  Draft:     ()                    => ({ tag: 'draft' }),
  Paid:      (paymentId, at)       => ({ tag: 'paid', paymentId, at }),
  Shipped:   (trackingNo, carrier) => ({ tag: 'shipped', trackingNo, carrier }),
  Cancelled: (reason, by)          => ({ tag: 'cancelled', reason, by }),
  Refunded:  (refundId, amount)    => ({ tag: 'refunded', refundId, amount }),
};

// Transitions are total and explicit. An unknown transition is a bug that
// throws here, not a silent no-op discovered by a customer.
const ALLOWED = {
  draft:     ['paid', 'cancelled'],
  paid:      ['shipped', 'cancelled'],
  shipped:   ['refunded'],
  cancelled: [],
  refunded:  [],
};

class Order {
  #state = OrderState.Draft();
  get state() { return this.#state; }

  transition(next) {
    if (!ALLOWED[this.#state.tag].includes(next.tag)) {
      throw new IllegalTransition(this.#state.tag, next.tag);
    }
    this.#state = next;
  }
  // No setters. The only way to change state is through a checked transition.
}
Boolean flags are a state machine you refused to write

Every system with isActive, isDeleted, isPending and isVerified eventually produces a record where two of them are true and nobody knows how. The state machine was always there; the only question was whether it was written down and enforced, or implied and violated.

Value objects and invariants at construction

javascript · validate once, at the boundary
// If a type can only hold valid values, downstream code never re-validates.
class Money {
  #cents; #currency;
  constructor(cents, currency) {
    if (!Number.isInteger(cents)) throw new TypeError('Money must be integer minor units');
    if (!CURRENCIES.has(currency)) throw new TypeError(`unknown currency ${currency}`);
    this.#cents = cents; this.#currency = currency;
    Object.freeze(this);                       // immutable: safe to share, safe to cache
  }
  plus(other) {
    if (other.#currency !== this.#currency) throw new TypeError('currency mismatch');
    return new Money(this.#cents + other.#cents, this.#currency);
  }
  // Operations return NEW instances. There is no way to mutate a Money into
  // an invalid one, so "is this amount valid?" is answered by its existence.
  equals(o) { return o instanceof Money && o.#cents === this.#cents && o.#currency === this.#currency; }
  toString() { return format(this.#cents, this.#currency); }
}
// This is "parse, don't validate": convert untrusted input into a type that
// carries the guarantee, once, at the edge.

The patterns that earn their place

PatternUse whenBackend example
Strategyone operation, several interchangeable algorithmspricing rules, split methods, dispatch policies
Factoryconstruction depends on runtime inputbuilding a payment provider from a config string
Buildermany optional parameters with validation at the enda query builder; a complex request object
Adaptera third-party API does not match your interfacewrapping two payment providers behind one port
Decoratoradding behaviour without changing the subjectretry, caching, logging around a repository
Observerseveral parties react to one eventdomain events; hooks
Commandan operation must be queued, logged or undonejob payloads; undo stacks
Chain of responsibilityordered handlers that may stop the chainmiddleware
Repositoryhiding persistence from domain logicevery data-access layer in this book
Statebehaviour differs per statevending machine; order lifecycle
The over-applied ones

Singleton is global mutable state with a respectable name; it makes tests order-dependent and hides dependencies. Abstract factory is usually one interface and a switch. Visitor is largely obsolete where you have sealed types and pattern matching. Naming a pattern is not a design; the design is the interface and what happens when it fails.

How to present an LLD answer

  1. Requirements and assumptions, out loud, in thirty seconds.
  2. The interfaces — the two or three types a caller touches.
  3. The state model, if there is one.
  4. The hard method, written out properly. Usually the allocation, the matching, or the transition.
  5. Concurrency: what two callers can do simultaneously, and what makes it safe.
  6. Extension: the next requirement they will ask for, and where it plugs in.
What the interviewer is scoring

Not whether you recalled a pattern name. They are watching whether you asked clarifying questions before designing, whether your interfaces would be pleasant to call, whether you noticed the concurrency, and whether adding the obvious next feature requires editing existing code or adding a class. The most common failure is a beautiful class diagram with no answer to "what happens when two people do this at once".

L02

Concurrency & thread safety in design

Concurrency bugs are rare in testing and frequent in production, because they need timing you cannot reproduce on demand. The defence is structural: design so the dangerous interleaving cannot occur, rather than hoping it does not.

The failure modes

Race condition
The result depends on timing. The classic is read-modify-write: two threads read 5, both write 6, one increment is lost.
Lost update
A special case with a name because it is so common: two writers, last one wins, the first one's change vanishes silently.
Deadlock
A holds lock 1 and wants 2; B holds 2 and wants 1. Both wait forever.
Livelock
Both threads keep yielding to each other and neither progresses. Retry loops without backoff produce this.
Starvation
One thread never gets the lock because others keep taking it. Unfair locks under contention.
ABA
A value changes A→B→A between your read and your compare-and-swap, so the CAS succeeds although the world changed. Fixed with a version counter alongside the value.

The single-threaded event loop is not immune

javascript · a race with no threads at all
// Node runs one thread. This is still a lost update, because there is a
// suspension point in the middle and another request can interleave there.
async function addCredits(userId, amount) {
  const user = await db.get(userId);       // ← A reads balance 100
                                            //   B reads balance 100
  user.balance += amount;                   // ← A computes 150, B computes 120
  await db.put(user);                       // ← A writes 150, B writes 120
}                                           //   final: 120. A's 50 is gone.

// FIX 1 — atomic operation: the database does the arithmetic, no read needed.
await db.updateOne({ _id: userId }, { $inc: { balance: amount } });

// FIX 2 — compare-and-swap when the new value is not a simple function of the old.
async function setTier(userId, tier) {
  for (let attempt = 0; attempt < 3; attempt++) {
    const user = await db.get(userId);
    const next = computeTier(user, tier);
    const res = await db.updateOne(
      { _id: userId, version: user.version },        // the guard
      { $set: { tier: next }, $inc: { version: 1 } },
    );
    if (res.modifiedCount === 1) return next;
    await sleep(jitter(attempt));                     // backoff, or you livelock
  }
  throw new Conflict('too much contention');
}

// FIX 3 — serialise with an async lock when the work genuinely cannot be atomic.
const lock = new AsyncLock();
await lock.acquire(`user:${userId}`, async () => { /* read, compute, write */ });
"Single-threaded so it is safe" is wrong

Single-threaded means no data races — no torn reads of a half-written object. It does not mean no logical races. Every await is a point where another request's continuation can run, so any read-modify-write spanning an await is exactly as unsafe as it would be with threads. The interleaving is just coarser, which makes it rarer and therefore harder to find.

Locking, and how to avoid needing it

ApproachCostUse when
Atomic operationnonethe new value is a function the store can compute ($inc, DECRBY)
Compare-and-swapretries under contentionread-compute-write with low contention — the default choice
Immutabilityallocationshared read-mostly data; removes the problem entirely
Confinementa partitioning schemeone owner per key (actor model, partitioned queues, one writer per conversation)
Mutexserialisationgenuinely must be exclusive and cannot be expressed atomically
Read-write lockwriter starvation riskheavy reads, rare writes
Distributed lockcorrectness caveats — see belowcoordination across processes, when the work is also idempotent
javascript · a correct distributed lock, and its honest limits
async function withLock(key, ttlMs, fn) {
  const token = crypto.randomUUID();
  // NX: only if absent. PX: auto-expire so a crashed holder cannot deadlock.
  const acquired = await redis.set(`lock:${key}`, token, 'PX', ttlMs, 'NX');
  if (!acquired) throw new Conflict('lock held');

  try {
    return await fn();
  } finally {
    // Compare-and-delete. A plain DEL would release someone ELSE's lock if
    // ours had already expired — which is the bug that makes naive locks worse
    // than no lock, because it removes protection precisely when it is needed.
    await redis.eval(
      `if redis.call('get', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then return redis.call('del', KEYS[1]) end`,
      1, `lock:${key}`, token,
    );
  }
}
No distributed lock is safe on its own

Between acquiring the lock and doing the work, your process can be paused — a long garbage collection, a hypervisor stall, a network partition. The TTL expires, another process acquires the lock, and now two holders believe they have exclusivity. The only complete fix is a fencing token: the lock hands out a monotonically increasing number, and the resource being protected rejects any write carrying a token lower than the highest it has seen. Without that, treat a distributed lock as an optimisation that reduces contention, and make the protected operation idempotent so a double execution is harmless.

Deadlock, and the one rule that prevents it

Four conditions must ALL hold. Break any one and deadlock is impossible: 1 mutual exclusion 2 hold-and-wait 3 no preemption 4 circular wait Breaking (4) is the practical answer: acquire locks in a global order. transfer(from, to): WRONG lock(from); lock(to) A→B locks A then B; B→A locks B then A; deadlock. RIGHT const [first, second] = [from, to].sort() lock(first); lock(second) Every caller now acquires in the same order. No cycle can form. Also useful: lock timeouts convert a deadlock into a detectable error rather than a hang, and lock-free designs avoid the question entirely.

Bounded queues are the backpressure mechanism

javascript · producer-consumer with a real bound
class BoundedQueue {
  #items = []; #waitingConsumers = []; #waitingProducers = [];
  constructor(capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }

  async put(item) {
    // The whole point: when full, the PRODUCER waits. An unbounded queue
    // instead grows until the process runs out of memory — it does not
    // remove the backpressure, it converts it into an OOM kill.
    while (this.#items.length >= this.capacity) {
      await new Promise((r) => this.#waitingProducers.push(r));
    }
    this.#items.push(item);
    this.#waitingConsumers.shift()?.();
  }

  async take() {
    while (this.#items.length === 0) {
      await new Promise((r) => this.#waitingConsumers.push(r));
    }
    const item = this.#items.shift();
    this.#waitingProducers.shift()?.();
    return item;
  }
}

Testing concurrent code

  • Stress tests — run the operation from N callers simultaneously and assert the invariant. This is the test that catches the seat-booking and inventory races, and it must be concurrent: a sequential loop passes while the bug remains.
  • Deterministic interleaving — inject a controllable scheduler or explicit yield points, so a specific ordering can be reproduced in a test.
  • Invariant checking — after a chaotic run, assert the properties that must hold: the ledger sums to zero, no seat has two owners, the count matches the rows.
  • Accept the limitation. A passing concurrency test proves the bug did not occur in that run, not that it cannot. Prefer designs where the interleaving is impossible over designs you have tested.
Interview follow-up

“How would you make this thread-safe?” The ordered answer: can the operation be a single atomic primitive (best)? If not, can it be a compare-and-swap on a version (usually)? If not, can the state be confined to one owner so there is no sharing (often)? Only if all three fail do you reach for a lock — and then you name the lock order to prevent deadlock and the TTL to prevent a permanent one. Reaching for a mutex first is the answer of someone who has not looked for the cheaper options.

L03

LLD: rate limiter, cache & ID generator

Three problems that appear in almost every LLD interview, each with a naive answer that looks right and a real answer that survives concurrency.

1 · Rate limiter

Fixed window count per minute, reset at the boundary simple; but 100 requests at 10:00:59 and 100 at 10:01:00 = 200 in one second while never exceeding "100 per minute". 2× burst at every edge. Sliding window log store a timestamp per request, count those in range exact; O(n) memory per key. 10 k rps × 60 s = 600 k timestamps per key. Sliding window counter weight the previous window by overlap ~exact, O(1) memory. count = curr + prev × (overlap fraction) Token bucket capacity C, refill R/sec; a request takes 1 token allows a controlled BURST up to C, then settles to R. The usual choice for APIs, because real traffic is bursty and punishing that is hostile. Leaky bucket requests queue and drain at a fixed rate smooths OUTPUT — right for protecting a fragile downstream, not a caller.
javascript · distributed token bucket, atomically
// Must be one atomic operation. GET-then-SET across the network is a race:
// two nodes both read 1 token remaining and both allow the request.
const TOKEN_BUCKET = `
  local key      = KEYS[1]
  local capacity = tonumber(ARGV[1])
  local refill   = tonumber(ARGV[2])   -- tokens per second
  local now      = tonumber(ARGV[3])   -- passed in: Redis TIME avoids clock skew
  local cost     = tonumber(ARGV[4])

  local b = redis.call('HMGET', key, 'tokens', 'ts')
  local tokens = tonumber(b[1]) or capacity
  local ts     = tonumber(b[2]) or now

  -- Lazy refill: no background timer, no per-key job. Compute what has
  -- accrued since the last request. This is what makes it O(1) and stateless.
  tokens = math.min(capacity, tokens + (now - ts) * refill)

  if tokens < cost then
    local retryAfter = math.ceil((cost - tokens) / refill)
    return {0, 0, retryAfter}
  end

  tokens = tokens - cost
  redis.call('HMSET', key, 'tokens', tokens, 'ts', now)
  redis.call('EXPIRE', key, math.ceil(capacity / refill) * 2)   -- self-cleaning
  return {1, math.floor(tokens), 0}
`;

async function allow(key, { capacity = 100, refill = 10, cost = 1 } = {}) {
  const [ts] = await redis.time();                       // server clock, not ours
  const [ok, remaining, retryAfter] = await redis.eval(
    TOKEN_BUCKET, 1, `rl:${key}`, capacity, refill, ts, cost,
  );
  return { allowed: ok === 1, remaining, retryAfter };
}
// Cost-based limiting falls out for free: an expensive endpoint passes cost: 10.
The follow-ups you will get

"What if Redis is down?" Fail open or closed — a deliberate choice. Fail open for a general API limit (availability matters more), fail closed for a login limiter (abuse matters more). Say which and why. "Key growth?" The EXPIRE above makes buckets self-cleaning; without it, one key per IP per endpoint grows without bound. "Clock skew?" Take the time from Redis, not from the application node.

2 · LRU cache

javascript · hash map + doubly linked list, O(1) both ways
// The naive answer — "store a timestamp and evict the oldest" — is O(n) per
// eviction. The linked list gives O(1) by making recency a POSITION.
class LRUCache {
  #map = new Map();                         // key -> node
  #head = { };                              // sentinels remove all the null checks
  #tail = { };

  constructor(capacity) {
    this.capacity = capacity;
    this.#head.next = this.#tail;
    this.#tail.prev = this.#head;
  }

  #remove(node) { node.prev.next = node.next; node.next.prev = node.prev; }
  #addFront(node) {
    node.next = this.#head.next; node.prev = this.#head;
    this.#head.next.prev = node; this.#head.next = node;
  }

  get(key) {
    const node = this.#map.get(key);
    if (!node) return undefined;
    if (node.expiresAt && node.expiresAt < Date.now()) {   // lazy TTL
      this.#remove(node); this.#map.delete(key); return undefined;
    }
    this.#remove(node); this.#addFront(node);              // touch = move to front
    return node.value;
  }

  set(key, value, ttlMs) {
    const existing = this.#map.get(key);
    if (existing) { this.#remove(existing); this.#map.delete(key); }

    const node = { key, value, expiresAt: ttlMs ? Date.now() + ttlMs : null };
    this.#addFront(node);
    this.#map.set(key, node);

    if (this.#map.size > this.capacity) {
      const lru = this.#tail.prev;                          // O(1) eviction
      this.#remove(lru);
      this.#map.delete(lru.key);
      this.onEvict?.(lru.key, lru.value);
    }
  }
}
The extensions they ask for next
RequirementChange
Thread safetystriped locks — N shards each with its own map, list and lock, keyed by hash(key) % N. One global lock serialises every read and destroys the point.
Size-based evictiontrack bytes per entry and evict until under budget; a 10 MB entry and a 10 byte entry are not equivalent
TTLlazy expiry on read (above) plus a periodic sampling sweep, exactly as Redis does — a timer per key does not scale
LFU insteadfrequency buckets with a doubly linked list per count; move an item between buckets on access. Add ageing, or yesterday's popular item never leaves.
Scan resistancea large sequential scan evicts the whole working set. Use a segmented LRU (probation + protected) or admission control such as TinyLFU.

3 · Distributed ID generator

Requirements: unique across nodes, roughly time-sortable, no coordination on the hot path, compact. Snowflake — 64 bits ┌─┬────────────────────────────────────┬──────────┬────────────┐ │0│ timestamp ms (41 bits) │ node(10) │ seq (12) │ └─┴────────────────────────────────────┴──────────┴────────────┘ 1 41 bits ≈ 69 years from a custom epoch 10 bits = 1 024 nodes 12 bits = 4 096 ids per millisecond per node = 4.1 M/sec/node Sign bit is 0 so the value stays positive in languages with signed integers. Time-sortable: ids ordered by value are ordered by creation, which gives free chronological pagination and good B-tree insert locality.
javascript · with the clock-backwards case handled
class Snowflake {
  #lastMs = -1n; #seq = 0n;
  constructor(nodeId, epoch = 1735689600000n) {         // 2025-01-01
    if (nodeId < 0 || nodeId > 1023) throw new RangeError('nodeId 0..1023');
    this.nodeId = BigInt(nodeId);
    this.epoch = epoch;
  }

  next() {
    let now = BigInt(Date.now());

    // NTP can step the clock backwards. Reusing a millisecond we have already
    // emitted ids for would produce DUPLICATES — the one thing this must never do.
    if (now < this.#lastMs) {
      const drift = this.#lastMs - now;
      if (drift > 5n) throw new Error(`clock moved back ${drift}ms — refusing to generate`);
      while (BigInt(Date.now()) < this.#lastMs) { /* spin briefly */ }
      now = this.#lastMs;
    }

    if (now === this.#lastMs) {
      this.#seq = (this.#seq + 1n) & 0xfffn;
      if (this.#seq === 0n) {                            // 4096 exhausted this ms
        while (BigInt(Date.now()) <= this.#lastMs) { /* wait for the next ms */ }
        now = BigInt(Date.now());
      }
    } else {
      this.#seq = 0n;
    }

    this.#lastMs = now;
    return ((now - this.epoch) << 22n) | (this.nodeId << 12n) | this.#seq;
  }
}
// nodeId assignment is the operational detail people forget: from an ordinal
// (a StatefulSet index), a ZooKeeper/etcd lease, or a registry — never an
// environment variable someone can copy-paste into two pods.
SchemeSortableCoordinationSizeUse when
Snowflakeyesnode id only64 bityou control deployment and want compact, sortable ids
UUID v4nonone128 bitanywhere, but terrible index locality at high insert rates
UUID v7 / ULIDyesnone128 bitthe modern default — sortable, no node assignment, standard
Database sequenceyesevery id64 bitlow volume; a round trip per id
Ticket rangesyesper 10 k64 bithigh volume with a central authority; see the URL-shortener case study
Reach for UUID v7 unless you have a reason not to

It gives you Snowflake's time-ordering and index locality without node-id assignment, clock-backwards handling or an epoch to maintain — and it is a standard, so every language has one. Snowflake earns its place when 64 bits versus 128 genuinely matters at your row count, or when you want the node id embedded for debugging. Saying that trade-off out loud is a better answer than reciting the bit layout.

Interview follow-up

Each of these has one question that separates recall from understanding. Rate limiter: why is the fixed window wrong (the 2× boundary burst). Cache: why not sort by timestamp (O(n) eviction; the list makes recency positional). ID generator: what happens when the clock goes backwards (you must refuse rather than risk duplicates). If you volunteer those before being asked, you have demonstrated you have implemented them rather than read about them.

L04

LLD: parking lot, elevator & vending machine

Three classics. Treated as trivia they are worthless; treated as exercises in interface design, state modelling and concurrency they are exactly what the interview is testing.

1 · Parking lot

Clarify first — the answers change the design completely: one site or many? · vehicle sizes? · reservations? · payment on exit or on entry? · how many entrances? · electric charging bays? Entities Lot → Floor → Spot(type) · Vehicle(type) · Ticket · Payment Strategies allocation (nearest / by-type / by-level) · pricing (hourly / tiered)
javascript · interfaces first, then the concurrency
// The public surface. Note what a failure looks like, and that park() returns
// a ticket rather than a boolean — the caller needs the identifier.
//   park(vehicle) → Ticket | throws LotFull
//   unpark(ticketId, payment) → Receipt | throws Unpaid
//   availability(type) → count

// Allocation is a STRATEGY, because "nearest to the lift" and "cheapest level"
// are the same operation with different rules — and the next requirement will
// be a third rule. Open/closed in practice.
class NearestFirst {
  pick(freeSpots, vehicle) {
    return freeSpots
      .filter((s) => s.fits(vehicle))
      .sort((a, b) => a.distanceToEntrance - b.distanceToEntrance)[0] ?? null;
  }
}

class ParkingLot {
  constructor({ spots, allocator = new NearestFirst(), pricer }) {
    this.spots = spots; this.allocator = allocator; this.pricer = pricer;
    this.free = new Map();                    // type -> Set<spotId>, for O(1) counts
  }

  // THE concurrency question: two cars arrive at two entrances at once.
  // A naive "find a free spot, then mark it taken" assigns both to the same bay.
  park(vehicle) {
    for (let attempt = 0; attempt < 5; attempt++) {
      const candidate = this.allocator.pick(this.#freeSpotsFor(vehicle), vehicle);
      if (!candidate) throw new LotFull(vehicle.type);

      // Atomic claim: compare-and-set on the spot's own state. The FIRST
      // caller wins; the loser simply picks again. No global lock, so two
      // entrances never serialise against each other.
      if (candidate.tryOccupy(vehicle.id)) {
        this.free.get(candidate.type).delete(candidate.id);
        return this.#issueTicket(vehicle, candidate);
      }
      // lost the race — try the next candidate
    }
    throw new LotFull('too much contention');
  }
}

class Spot {
  #occupantId = null;
  tryOccupy(vehicleId) {
    if (this.#occupantId !== null) return false;   // in a real system: a CAS
    this.#occupantId = vehicleId;                  // or a conditional DB update
    return true;
  }
  release() { this.#occupantId = null; }
  fits(v) { return SIZE_ORDER[this.type] >= SIZE_ORDER[v.type]; }
}
The extension questions

Reservations (a spot has a third state, reserved, with an expiry — the same hold pattern as seat booking). Multiple entrances (already handled by per-spot CAS rather than a lot-wide lock). Electric bays (a spot capability, not a new class hierarchy). Pricing changes mid-stay (pin the rate to the ticket at entry). Each of these should slot in without editing existing classes — if it does not, the abstraction was wrong.

2 · Elevator

The genuinely interesting one, because there are TWO kinds of request: hall call pressed outside: floor + DIRECTION, and any car may serve it car call pressed inside: floor only, and only THAT car can serve it A design that models only "requested floors" cannot express direction, and will send a car going down to collect someone who wants to go up. Per-car state machine IDLE ──▶ MOVING_UP ──▶ DOORS_OPENING ──▶ DOORS_OPEN ──▶ DOORS_CLOSING ──▶ … └─▶ MOVING_DOWN ──┘ ▲ └─ obstruction: reopen Scheduling within one car FCFS fair, and terrible: floor 1 → 10 → 2 → 9 traverses the shaft 4× SCAN keep going in the current direction, serving calls along the way; reverse only when nothing remains ahead. This is the elevator algorithm, and it is what real lifts do. LOOK SCAN, but reverse at the last actual call rather than the end Dispatching across N cars — assign a hall call by cost: cost = distance + (wrong direction ? penalty : 0) + queueLength + capacity
javascript · SCAN, and the dispatcher
class Car {
  constructor(id, floors) {
    this.id = id; this.floor = 0; this.direction = 'IDLE';
    this.up = new Set();                       // stops wanted while going up
    this.down = new Set();                     // stops wanted while going down
  }

  // SCAN: serve everything ahead in the current direction before reversing.
  next() {
    if (this.direction === 'UP') {
      const ahead = [...this.up].filter((f) => f > this.floor).sort((a, b) => a - b);
      if (ahead.length) return { floor: ahead[0], direction: 'UP' };
      // nothing above: reverse to the HIGHEST down-request, then sweep down
      const below = [...this.down].sort((a, b) => b - a);
      if (below.length) { this.direction = 'DOWN'; return { floor: below[0], direction: 'DOWN' }; }
    }
    if (this.direction === 'DOWN') {
      const ahead = [...this.down].filter((f) => f < this.floor).sort((a, b) => b - a);
      if (ahead.length) return { floor: ahead[0], direction: 'DOWN' };
      const above = [...this.up].sort((a, b) => a - b);
      if (above.length) { this.direction = 'UP'; return { floor: above[0], direction: 'UP' }; }
    }
    this.direction = 'IDLE';
    return null;
  }

  cost(call) {
    if (this.direction === 'IDLE') return Math.abs(this.floor - call.floor);
    const sameWay = this.direction === call.direction;
    const ahead = this.direction === 'UP' ? call.floor >= this.floor : call.floor <= this.floor;
    // On the way and going the right way: nearly free. Otherwise it must
    // finish its sweep first, so penalise heavily.
    if (sameWay && ahead) return Math.abs(this.floor - call.floor);
    return Math.abs(this.floor - call.floor) + PENALTY + this.queueLength;
  }
}

class Dispatcher {
  assign(call) {                                // hall call: pick the best car
    const best = this.cars.reduce((a, b) => (a.cost(call) <= b.cost(call) ? a : b));
    best.accept(call);
    return best.id;
  }
}
The concurrency model matters here too

Each car runs its own control loop; the dispatcher is shared. Two hall calls arriving simultaneously must not both be assigned to the same car by two threads that each computed cost before either assignment landed. Either serialise the dispatcher (it is cheap — one decision per call), or make accept a compare-and-swap that the dispatcher retries. Saying this unprompted is what distinguishes the answer.

3 · Vending machine

The canonical State pattern example — and the one place it genuinely earns its keep, because behaviour differs per state rather than just data. Idle ──insertCoin──▶ HasMoney ──select──▶ Dispensing ──▶ Idle │ │ └─sold out──▶ refund ──▶ Idle │ └─refund──▶ Idle └─select──▶ error: insert money first Each state is a class implementing the same interface. select() in Idle rejects; in HasMoney it dispenses. No if (state === …) chains, and adding a state does not touch the others.
javascript · state pattern, and the change-making trap
class HasMoneyState {
  constructor(machine) { this.m = machine; }
  insertCoin(c) { this.m.credit += c; }
  refund() { const r = this.m.credit; this.m.credit = 0; this.m.setState('idle'); return r; }
  select(code) {
    const item = this.m.inventory.get(code);
    if (!item || item.count === 0) throw new SoldOut(code);
    if (this.m.credit < item.priceCents) throw new InsufficientFunds(item.priceCents - this.m.credit);

    const change = this.m.computeChange(this.m.credit - item.priceCents);
    if (change === null) throw new CannotMakeChange();   // refuse BEFORE dispensing

    this.m.setState('dispensing');
    return { item, change };
  }
}

// Greedy change-making is CORRECT for canonical coin systems (UK, EU, US)
// and WRONG in general. With coins [1, 3, 4] and a target of 6, greedy gives
// 4+1+1 = three coins; the optimum is 3+3 = two. If the coin set is
// configurable, you need dynamic programming, not greed.
computeChange(amount) {
  const coins = [...this.coinInventory.keys()].sort((a, b) => b - a);
  const used = new Map();
  for (const c of coins) {
    const take = Math.min(Math.floor(amount / c), this.coinInventory.get(c));
    if (take > 0) { used.set(c, take); amount -= c * take; }
  }
  return amount === 0 ? used : null;      // null = cannot make change; refuse the sale
}
Check change before dispensing, not after

Dispensing the item and then discovering you cannot make change leaves the machine holding money it owes and the customer with no recourse. The order of operations is the design: verify stock, verify funds, verify change is available, then commit to the transaction. This is the same shape as reserving inventory before capturing a payment — check every precondition before any irreversible step.

Interview · what these three are really probing

Parking lot: whether you notice two entrances are concurrent, and whether allocation is pluggable. Elevator: whether you distinguish hall calls from car calls, and whether you know SCAN rather than reinventing FCFS. Vending machine: whether behaviour lives in states rather than in conditionals, and whether you order the preconditions before the irreversible action. In all three the class diagram is the easy part — the marks are in the state model and the concurrency.

L05

LLD: expense splitting & the booking core

Two problems with real algorithmic content underneath the object modelling — one about representing debt, one about representing time.

1 · Expense splitting

The modelling decision that everything hinges on: how do you store who owes whom? PAIRWISE DEBT MATRIX debt[A][B] = 30, debt[B][C] = 20, … n² entries, and every expense updates several of them. Worse: it encodes redundant information. A owes B £30 and B owes A £10 are two facts describing one £20 debt, and they will drift apart. NET BALANCE PER PERSON balance[A] = −50, balance[B] = +30, balance[C] = +20 n entries. Sum is always zero — an invariant you can assert. Every expense is: payer += total, each participant −= their share. Who-owes-whom is DERIVED at settlement time, not stored. The second is right, and the derivation is the interesting algorithm.
javascript · split strategies, with the rounding problem solved
// Strategies, because "equally / exact / percentage / shares" is exactly the
// shape the Strategy pattern exists for — and there will be a fifth one.
const splits = {
  equal: (totalCents, participants) => {
    const base = Math.floor(totalCents / participants.length);
    const remainder = totalCents - base * participants.length;   // 0..n-1 pennies
    // SOMEONE must get the extra penny. Choose deterministically — rotating by
    // expense id spreads it fairly over time, and is reproducible in a test.
    return participants.map((p, i) => ({
      userId: p, cents: base + (i < remainder ? 1 : 0),
    }));
  },

  exact: (totalCents, entries) => {
    const sum = entries.reduce((a, e) => a + e.cents, 0);
    if (sum !== totalCents) throw new ValidationError(`splits sum to ${sum}, not ${totalCents}`);
    return entries;
  },

  percentage: (totalCents, entries) => {
    const pct = entries.reduce((a, e) => a + e.percent, 0);
    if (Math.abs(pct - 100) > 1e-9) throw new ValidationError('percentages must total 100');
    const raw = entries.map((e) => ({ userId: e.userId, exact: totalCents * e.percent / 100 }));
    return largestRemainder(raw, totalCents);        // never lose a penny to rounding
  },
};

// Applying an expense: one pass, and the invariant holds by construction.
function applyExpense({ paidBy, totalCents, shares }, balances) {
  balances.set(paidBy, (balances.get(paidBy) ?? 0) + totalCents);
  for (const { userId, cents } of shares) {
    balances.set(userId, (balances.get(userId) ?? 0) - cents);
  }
  // assert: [...balances.values()].reduce((a,b) => a + b, 0) === 0
}
Integer minor units, and a deterministic remainder

Splitting £10 three ways is 333, 333, 334 — never 3.333333. Store integers, decide explicitly who absorbs the remainder, and make that decision reproducible so the same expense always splits identically. Floating point here produces balances that fail to sum to zero, and a group ledger that never quite settles is the bug users actually report.

javascript · debt simplification — the algorithm they are looking for
// From net balances, produce the MINIMUM number of transfers that settles up.
// Greedy: repeatedly match the largest creditor with the largest debtor.
function settle(balances) {
  const creditors = [...balances].filter(([, v]) => v > 0).map(([u, v]) => ({ u, v }));
  const debtors   = [...balances].filter(([, v]) => v < 0).map(([u, v]) => ({ u, v: -v }));

  // Max-heaps would be better at scale; a group is small, so sorting is fine.
  creditors.sort((a, b) => b.v - a.v);
  debtors.sort((a, b) => b.v - a.v);

  const transfers = [];
  let i = 0, j = 0;
  while (i < creditors.length && j < debtors.length) {
    const amount = Math.min(creditors[i].v, debtors[j].v);
    transfers.push({ from: debtors[j].u, to: creditors[i].u, cents: amount });
    creditors[i].v -= amount;
    debtors[j].v -= amount;
    // At least one side is now zero, so each iteration removes a person.
    if (creditors[i].v === 0) i++;
    if (debtors[j].v === 0) j++;
  }
  return transfers;                                    // at most n-1 transfers
}

// HONEST CAVEAT: greedy gives at most n-1 transfers, which is a good bound,
// but it is NOT always the true minimum. Finding the exact minimum is
// NP-hard (it is a subset-sum problem: if a subgroup's balances cancel
// exactly, they can settle among themselves). Say this — the interviewer is
// usually testing whether you claim optimality you cannot deliver.

2 · The booking core, generalised

Seats, hotel nights, meeting rooms, equipment hire, appointment slots — all the same shape: a resource, a time interval, and exclusivity. Two intervals [s1,e1) and [s2,e2) overlap if: s1 < e2 AND s2 < e1 Half-open intervals matter: a checkout at 11:00 and a check-in at 11:00 do NOT overlap, and getting this wrong loses a night of inventory per booking. Three implementations, three failure modes: 1 discrete slot documents one field per unit of time claim = conditional update asserting all fields absent ✔ atomic, simple ✘ only works when time is naturally discrete (nights, seats, 30-min slots) 2 exclusion constraint the database enforces non-overlap directly Postgres: EXCLUDE USING gist (resource WITH =, period WITH &&) ✔ the strongest guarantee available; the DB refuses to store an overlap ✘ not available in most document stores 3 distributed lock per resource ✘ serialises the resource, cannot span a payment, breaks on a GC pause Use only when neither of the above is possible, and make the work idempotent.
javascript · hold → confirm → expire, with every guard in place
// Discrete slots. { _id: resourceId, slots: { '2026-06-12': holdOrBookingId, ... } }
class BookingCore {
  async hold({ resourceId, from, to, userId, idempotencyKey, ttlMs = 8 * 60_000 }) {
    const existing = await this.holds.findOne({ idempotencyKey });
    if (existing) return existing;                       // retry-safe

    const units = enumerate(from, to);                   // half-open
    const holdId = newId();
    const now = new Date();
    const expiresAt = new Date(Date.now() + ttlMs);

    const filter = { _id: resourceId };
    const set = {};
    for (const u of units) {
      // Free, OR held by someone whose hold has lapsed. Treating an expired
      // hold as free is what makes the sweeper an optimisation rather than a
      // correctness dependency.
      filter.$and ??= [];
      filter.$and.push({ $or: [
        { [`slots.${u}`]: { $exists: false } },
        { [`slots.${u}.expiresAt`]: { $lt: now } },
      ] });
      set[`slots.${u}`] = { holdId, userId, expiresAt, state: 'held' };
    }

    const res = await this.resources.updateOne(filter, { $set: set, $inc: { version: 1 } });
    if (res.modifiedCount === 0) {
      throw new Conflict('unavailable', { units: await this.#taken(resourceId, units, now) });
    }
    await this.holds.insertOne({ _id: holdId, resourceId, units, userId, expiresAt,
                                 idempotencyKey, state: 'active' });
    return { holdId, expiresAt };
  }

  async confirm(holdId, paymentRef) {
    const h = await this.holds.findOne({ _id: holdId, state: 'active' });
    if (!h) throw new NotFound('hold', holdId);
    if (h.expiresAt < new Date()) throw new Conflict('hold expired');

    const filter = { _id: h.resourceId };
    const set = {};
    for (const u of h.units) {
      filter[`slots.${u}.holdId`] = holdId;              // STILL ours — not swept
      set[`slots.${u}`] = { state: 'booked', bookingId: holdId, paymentRef };
    }
    const res = await this.resources.updateOne(filter, { $set: set, $inc: { version: 1 } });
    if (res.modifiedCount === 0) throw new Conflict('hold was released');

    await this.holds.updateOne({ _id: holdId }, { $set: { state: 'confirmed' } });
    return holdId;
  }

  // Sweeper: guarded by holdId, so it can never clear a slot that has since
  // been confirmed. Safe to run late, safe to run twice, safe to not run.
  async sweep(limit = 500) {
    for (const h of await this.holds.find({ state: 'active', expiresAt: { $lt: new Date() } })
                                    .limit(limit).toArray()) {
      const filter = { _id: h.resourceId };
      const unset = {};
      for (const u of h.units) { filter[`slots.${u}.holdId`] = h._id; unset[`slots.${u}`] = ''; }
      await this.resources.updateOne(filter, { $unset: unset, $inc: { version: 1 } });
      await this.holds.updateOne({ _id: h._id }, { $set: { state: 'expired' } });
    }
  }
}
The three guards that make this correct

The hold filter asserts every unit is free or lapsed, so a partial booking is impossible and no transaction is needed. The confirm filter asserts the slots are still held by this hold, so a confirm racing a sweep loses cleanly rather than resurrecting a released booking. The sweep filter asserts the same thing in reverse, so it can never clear a slot that was just confirmed. Remove any one and you have a race that occurs rarely enough to reach production.

Interview follow-up

For Splitwise, the question is “how do you store balances” and the answer is net-per-person rather than pairwise, with the settlement derived greedily and an honest note that greedy is not provably minimal. For the booking core, the question is always “what if two people book at the same time”, and the answer must be a single conditional write whose filter is the availability check — not a read followed by a write, and not a lock. If you also volunteer that half-open intervals prevent losing a night per booking, you have shown you have shipped one of these.

L06

From design to production

A design is a hypothesis. This is the checklist that turns it into something that survives contact with real traffic, real failures and real people on call.

Production readiness

Correctness

Every unsafe operation is idempotent. Invariants are enforced by database constraints, not by application code. State machines reject illegal transitions rather than silently ignoring them. Every multi-step flow has a compensating path.

Reliability

Timeouts on every outbound call, decreasing inwards. Retries only where idempotent, with jitter and a budget. Each dependency classified critical or optional, with a tested fallback for the optional ones. Graceful shutdown drains before exiting.

Scalability

The first bottleneck is named, with the measurement that identifies it and the plan for it. The partition key still works at ten times the load. Nothing grows without bound — no unbounded arrays, queues, caches or retention.

Operability

RED metrics per endpoint. Correlation ids through every hop including async ones. An SLO with a burn-rate alert. A runbook per alert naming the first three commands. You can answer “is it slow for everyone or one tenant” from a dashboard.

Security & privacy

Authorisation checked against the object, in the data layer. Short-lived credentials. No PII in logs. Retention and deletion defined per data class. The irreversible privacy decisions — residency, tenancy — made deliberately.

Delivery

Rollback in under a minute, rehearsed. Expand/contract migrations so every deploy is reversible. Feature flags for anything risky. The artefact is built once and promoted, never rebuilt per environment.

Running a design review

  1. Requirements and numbers on the board first. A design cannot be reviewed without the constraints it was drawn for. If the author cannot state peak QPS, read:write ratio and the consistency requirement per operation, stop there.
  2. Ask what is irreversible. Shard key, public API contract, tenancy model, event schema, id format. Spend the review's time here; the rest can be changed on a Tuesday.
  3. Ask what breaks first. Not "will it scale" — which component saturates first, at what number, and how will you know.
  4. Ask about the failure you have not discussed. The dependency is slow rather than down. Two users do this simultaneously. The queue backs up for an hour. A region goes dark mid-transaction.
  5. Ask what was deliberately left out, and what signal would bring it back. A design with no explicit non-goals is a design that has not been scoped.
  6. Ask who carries the pager, and what it costs per month. Complexity has an ongoing price paid by people.
A good review produces decisions, not comments

The output should be a short list: what changed, what was explicitly accepted as a risk with an owner, and what will be revisited at a named trigger. A review that generates forty comments and no decisions has cost everyone an hour and changed nothing.

Answering “how would you scale this to 10×”

WEAK "add more servers, add caching, shard the database" — generic, unfalsifiable, and true of every system ever built STRONG a specific sequence, with the signal for each step: "At 10× the write rate the single primary saturates first — I'd see it as rising replication lag and write latency before throughput drops. The fix is sharding on tenantId, which works because no query spans tenants. Next is the fan-out worker: at 10× it cannot keep up during peak, visible as oldest-job age climbing. I'd raise concurrency to the partition count and lower the celebrity threshold so fewer accounts are fanned out. Third is cache memory — the working set grows linearly and the hit rate degrades. I'd measure the hit rate against size before adding capacity, because a lower hit rate on a bigger cache means the access pattern changed rather than the cache being too small. I would NOT shard the cache first, because it is not the bottleneck and it would add a failure mode for no benefit."

What is actually being scored

DimensionWeakStrong
Requirementsstarts drawing immediatelyasks about scale, consistency and scope before designing
Structurejumps between layersfollows a visible order and signposts it
Depthuniform shallowness across every boxgoes deep on the one or two genuinely hard components
Trade-offslists optionschooses, justifies, and names what was given up
Failurediscusses only the happy pathvolunteers failure modes and degradation before being asked
Realismassumes unbounded resourcesquotes numbers, admits limits, and defers complexity deliberately
Communicationmonologuechecks in, adapts to signals, thinks out loud
The most common failure is over-engineering

Proposing Kafka, a service mesh, multi-region active-active and CQRS for a system with a thousand users a day signals that you pattern-match rather than reason. The strongest answers frequently start with a boring design and then say precisely what would make them change it. Complexity you can justify is engineering; complexity you cannot is a liability someone else will operate.

The closing thought

Every system in Part II is a set of decisions made under stated constraints. Change the constraints and the right answer changes: the feed design flips at a different follower distribution, the booking design changes if the resource is continuous rather than discrete, the crawler changes if politeness is not a legal obligation. There is no catalogue of correct architectures to memorise.

What transfers is the method: establish the numbers, model the data around the access patterns, find the one or two places where correctness is genuinely at risk and make the database enforce it, then decide what happens when each dependency is slow rather than absent. Do that, and you can design a system you have never seen before — which is the actual job, in an interview and afterwards.

One sentence to take away

Design for the failure and the numbers, enforce correctness with a constraint rather than with discipline, and be able to say what you deliberately did not build. Everything else in this book is an elaboration of those three.

Ten Systems Handbook — the method, the primitives, fifteen designs and the low-level detail underneath them.

Every case study states its assumptions before its architecture, because a design is only correct relative to the numbers it was drawn for.

If you read only three chapters: requirements and estimation, consistency and CAP, and the seat-booking case study — which is where correctness under contention is at its most unforgiving.
